Quantum Authenticated Key Expansion with Key Recycling
Wen Yu Kon, Jefferson Chu, Kevin Han Yong Loh, Obada Alia, Omar Amer, Marco Pistoia, Kaushik Chakraborty, Charles Lim

Abstract
Data privacy and authentication are two main security requirements for remote access and cloud services. While QKD has been explored to address data privacy concerns, oftentimes its use is separate from the client authentication protocol despite implicitly providing authentication. Here, we present a quantum authentication key expansion (QAKE) protocol that (1) integrates both authentication and key expansion within a single protocol, and (2) provides key recycling property – allowing all authentication keys to be reused. We analyse the security of the protocol in a QAKE framework adapted from a classical authentication key exchange (AKE) framework, providing separate security conditions for authentication and data privacy. We experimentally implemented the protocol with appropriate post-selection. Additional results on the security of pseudorandom basis generation in QAKE and decoy state BB84 are provided.



1
Introduction

In this distributed digital era, remote access and cloud services are becoming increasingly popular, where a client device can access another device or a server, at any time, and from anywhere.
Such services typically has two main security requirements: authentication and data privacy.
To restrict access to these devices/servers for security or compliance reasons, such as in banking, healthcare, or government, it is necessary for the identity of the client to be authenticated.
Data privacy, on the other hand, ensures that the data transferred between the client and server, which may contain highly sensitive business or personal information (e.g. while looking at a remote desktop display of client details), is private from any eavesdroppers.
Fig.
1
presents a scenario where such a central server can identify its end-users via authentication and aid in the key generation between distant end-users.

Figure 1:
Schematic for end user key generation via a central server in a star network topology. (a) End users share a direct physical connection to the central server, and can establish keys with it via the authenticated key exchange protocol. (b) By performing operations on the keys, the central server is able aid the end users establish shared keys between them without the end users sharing a direct link.

Many current remote access and cloud services establishes a
virtual private networks
(VPNs) by means of internet protocols like IPsec.
Such protocols rely on either
public key infrastructure
(PKI) or symmetric key cryptography to provide the necessary security requirements.
PKI offers convenient key exchange without pre-shared secrets, but older schemes such as RSA
[
33
]
are vulnerable to quantum attacks
[
35
]
.
While post-quantum PKI schemes such as lattice-based cryptography that have been standardized by NIST
[
27
]
are believed to be quantum-safe, PKI remains susceptible to
harvest-now-decrypt-later
attacks.
On the other hand, symmetric key cryptography offers stronger long-term data privacy, but requires pre-shared secrets keys that have to be regularly refreshed after a fixed number of uses or duration
[
3
]
, posing a practical challenge.

To address the need for key refresh in symmetric key cryptography, internet service providers explored the use of
quantum key distribution
(QKD) for key expansion.
In this process, a separate authentication protocol is utilised to authenticate the client of the remote access service before utilising QKD to expand secret keys shared between the client and server.
Therefore, we propose a
quantum authenticated key expansion
(QAKE) protocol that improves efficiency by combining all entity and message authentication steps into two authentication steps: one from Alice to Bob and one from Bob to Alice.
This integrated approach provides both client-server authentication and key expansion within a single protocol session.

Another useful property for authentication to have in practice is key recycling
[
17
]
, where all secret keys (e.g. authentication keys) remain secure if the protocol succeeds.
In the context of cloud services, it eliminates the need for cloud service providers to update the clients’ authentication key across all its cloud servers after every session, saving significant communication resources.
Authentication schemes with key recycling have been proposed, but they either do not recycle the full authentication key (require recycling of an OTP key, which we call authentication masking key)
[
9
,
29
]
, or do not account for experimental imperfections such as loss and multi-photon events
[
30
,
17
,
23
]
.
The QAKE proposed here is designed with key recycling in mind, using the corrected bitstring, which is expected to have high min-entropy when the protocol passes, to mask the authentication key and authentication masking keys and allow them to be reused.

To analyse both the authentication and key exchange tasks in QAKE, we adapt the classical authenticated key exchange (AKE) framework
[
13
]
, where separate security conditions for authentication and key exchange are presented.
This highlights the role of various protocol components in providing data privacy and authentication, and allows for comparison with the case of two separate protocols – one for key exchange and one for authentication.

We demonstrated the protocol using commercial QKD devices.
The raw data collected from the QKD devices are post-processed offline through a software stack.
This includes error correction by adapting the LDPC IP cores from Xilinx
[
1
]
, and privacy amplification using Toeplitz hashing.
With a detection rate of about
1\text{\times}{10}^{-4}\text{\,}
and a quantum bit error rate (QBER) of about
2\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
, the QAKE protocol is able to provide a key rate of about
1\text{\times}{10}^{-5}\text{\,}
.

2
Quantum Authenticated Key Exchange

2.1
Message Authentication in QKD

In QKD, message authentication, which can double as entity authentication, typically relies on information-theoretic message authentication protocols (such as Wegman-Carter
[
9
]
) to authenticate every message sent between the parties.
These message authentication protocols have composable security and can thus be used in QKD securely.
Oftentimes, the QKD keys generated are then utilized for the next session’s authentication.

However, a direct utilization of the typical protocol design above may not be desirable in the practical setting.
While authenticating every message may simplify the security analysis, it might lead to significant authentication costs due to the shared randomness required, thereby reducing key rates.
This issue may be exacerbated by for instance the use of Cascade
[
5
]
as a means for error correction since it requires multiple rounds of communication, each of which may need to be authenticated if we naively apply message authentication to every communication steps.
This is addressed in Ref.
[
21
]
, where authentication is performed only in the last two communication steps, thereby reducing the authentication costs.

Using QKD keys for future authentication sessions requires updating the authentication keys after each use.
If the authentication keys can remain static, this would enable a wider range of applications.
For instance, in applications where a clients access services at multiple locations (e.g. ATM), authentication keys may be stored in multiple regional databases.
Updating the authentication keys in all databases after every authentication session would be operationally inefficient.

2.2
QAKE Protocol

Figure 2:
Summary of the QAKE protocol proposed. The protocol begins with both parties establishing the label to use, followed by Alice sending quantum states to Bob. Bob announces his basis choice and detected rounds and Alice responds with the sifted rounds. Bob announces the test round results, for which Alice can use for parameters estimation. If this passes, error correction and validation of Alice’s identity would occur followed by validation of Bob’s identity. Finally, the two parties independently decide whether to generate secret keys or update their label.

As such, the QAKE protocol focuses on incorporating the entity authentication check into the QKD process in an efficient manner, namely: (1) reducing authentication costs, (2) removing the need for key updating, (3) combining error verification with authentication, (4) secure reusing of privacy amplification seed.

To reduce communication costs, the QAKE protocol follows the proposal in Ref.
[
21
]
and performs authentication only twice, once to authenticate Alice (and communication transcript) and once to authenticate Bob.
Since the order of certain steps in QKD is crucial for security, most notably that Bob’s measurement should occur before the basis is announced, careful design is necessary.
For instance, we require that Bob announces his basis choice before Alice announces hers for sifting.
This prevents Eve from storing the quantum states and measuring them when the basis is announced since that requires Eve to commit to a response of Bob’s measurement basis choice to Alice before Bob has actually selected the basis.

To remove the need for key updating, the idea of key recycling or reusing of authentication keys has been proposed.
Early proposals of key recycling
[
9
,
29
]
requires pre-sharing of authentication masking keys (one for each authentication) to act as a one-time pad for the authentication tag, which may be impractical in practice for lightweight devices (e.g. ATM cards).
Later proposals
[
30
,
17
,
23
]
that only require updating of authentication masking keys when authentication fails require additional quantum communication separate from the QKD process for message authentication, but the protocols proposed do not fully account for experimental imperfections (loss, errors, multi-photon events etc).
Here, we propose the utilization of the corrected bitstring in QKD, where min-entropy can be guaranteed by the parameter estimation in QKD, as a means to protect
the authentication key and authentication masking key when the protocol succeeds.
Besides straightforwardly accounting for experimental imperfections via the QKD protocol, combining the quantum process for both protocols can reduce finite-size effects, since there need not be separate parameter estimation for both the process in QKD and authentication of Ref.
[
17
,
23
]
.

We also provide further improvements by having the message authentication scheme double as error verification, since the corrected bitstring is hashed there.
This reduces the number of shared randomness between Alice and Bob that is utilized in the post-processing of the QKD protocol.

The final modification made is to allow the privacy amplification seed
R
to be pre-shared between Alice and Bob and reused for future privacy amplification.
Unlike typical QKD protocols where Alice (or Bob) selects a random seed and publicly announces it, in this case, Alice and Bob do not announce
R
.
As such, reusing
R
does not impact security since for quantum-proof strong extractors, the keys generated are close to independent on
R
.
This use of a pre-shared random
R
could simplify practical implementation since the
field programmable gate array
(FPGA) used for privacy amplification can be pre-programmed with the seed and also reduce communication and authentication costs since
R
does not need to sent from Alice to Bob.

To illustrate the effects of key recycling and reusing of privacy amplification seed
R
, we need to consider that the protocol can be repeated multiple times.
We call each complete run of the protocol as a session, and assume that we can have up to
n_{sess}
sessions of the protocol.
In each session, Alice and Bob exchange
n_{rnd}
quantum states and we call each quantum state exchange as a single round (like QKD).

\do@columngrid

oneΔ

Protocol

1
Quantum Authenticated Key Exchange

Goal.
Alice and Bob authenticates one another, and performs key exchange.

1.

Label Agreement
: Alice and Bob exchange
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
. Alice (resp. Bob) sends
\alpha
(resp.
\alpha^{\prime}
) and receives
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}
(resp.
\alpha_{\textup{r}}
), which results in a label choice
\beta=\max\{\alpha,\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}\}
(resp.
\beta^{\prime}=\max\{\alpha_{\textup{r}},\alpha^{\prime}\}
).

2.

Alice State Preparation
: Alice randomly chooses a
n_{rnd}
-bit basis string
\theta
, a
n_{rnd}
-bit string
X
and a
n_{rnd}
-trit string
v\in\{0,1,2\}^{n_{rnd}}
according to probability distribution
p_{v}
. She then prepares
n_{rnd}
quantum systems
Q_{i}
in phase-randomised coherent BB84 states
\left\{\rho_{Q_{i}}^{\theta_{i},X_{i},\mu_{v_{i}}}\right\}_{i\in[1,n_{rnd}]}
, with basis
\theta_{i}
, bit value
X_{i}
, and intensity
\mu_{V_{i}}
.

3.

Bob Measurement
: Alice sends
Q^{n_{rnd}}
to Bob, who measures subsystems
Q_{i}
using a randomly chosen basis
\theta^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n_{rnd}}
, and records outcome
X_{i}^{\prime}
. If Bob detects no clicks, he declares
X_{i}^{\prime}=\perp
. If Bob detects multiple clicks, he randomly selects
x_{i}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.

4.

Sifting
: Bob records the detection rounds,
P=\{i:X_{i}^{\prime}\neq\perp\}
, and announces
P
and
\theta_{P}^{\prime}=\{\theta_{i}^{\prime}:i\in P\}
to Alice. Alice computes the sifted rounds
P^{\textup{sift}}=\{i:i\in P_{\textup{r}},\theta_{i,\textup{r}}^{\prime}=\theta_{i}\}
, and forwards it to Bob.

5.

Test Round Announcement
: Bob randomly splits the sifted rounds into
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}
and
P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}
, with
\absolutevalue{\Psift_1}=\lceil f_{P_{1}}\absolutevalue{\Psift}\rceil
, where
f_{P_{1}}
is some pre-determined fraction of rounds. Bob announces
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}
,
P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}
and
X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}}^{\prime}
.

6.

Parameter Estimation
: Alice checks if: (1) the number of test rounds matches the expected number
\absolutevalue{\Psift_{1,\rr}}=\lceil f_{P_{1}}\absolutevalue{\Psift}\rceil
, (2) the number of sifted rounds exceeds a threshold,
\absolutevalue{\Psift_{\rr}}\geq P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}
, (3) the lower bound on single-photon events in sets
P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}
and
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}}
(estimated via decoy state analysis) exceeds a threshold,
\hat{N}^{\textup{LB}}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}},1}\geq N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}},1}^{\textup{tol}}
,
\hat{N}^{\textup{LB}}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}},1}\geq N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}},1}^{\textup{tol}}
, (4) the upper bound on single-photon bit-error rate in set
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}}
(estimated via decoy-state analysis) falls below a threshold,
\hat{e}_{\textup{bit},P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}},1}^{\textup{UB}}\leq e_{\textup{bit},1,\textup{tol}}
, and (5) the upper bound on bit error rate in set
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}}
(estimated via Serfling bound) falls below a threshold,
\hat{e}_{\textup{bit},P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}^{\textup{UB}}\leq e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}
. If all checks pass, Alice sets
D_{\textup{PE}}=1
, otherwise she sets
D_{\textup{PE}}=0
.

7.

Error Correction
: If
D_{\textup{PE}}=1
, Alice generates a syndrome
syn=f_{\textup{syn}}(X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}})
of length
l_{syn}
and forwards it to Bob, otherwise, Alice sends a random string of length
l_{syn}
to Bob. Bob receives the syndrome
syn_{\textup{r}}
and computes the corrected bit string
\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},\textup{r}}=f_{\textup{ec}}(X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}^{\prime},syn_{\textup{r}})
.

8.

Alice Validation
: If
D_{\textup{PE}}=1
, Alice generates a tag
T_{\textup{AV}}=h_{1}(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}||P_{\textup{r}}||\theta_{P,\textup{r}}^{\prime}||P^{\textup{sift}}||X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},\textup{r}}^{\prime}||P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}}||P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}||syn)\oplus K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}
and forwards it to Bob, otherwise, Alice sends a random string of the same length as the tag. Bob receives tag
T_{\textup{AV},\textup{r}}
and generates verification tag
\tilde{T}_{\textup{AV}}=h_{1}(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}||P||\theta_{P}^{\prime}||P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{r}}||X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}}^{\prime}||P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}||P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}||syn_{\textup{r}})\oplus K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta^{\prime}}
and checks if
T_{\textup{AV},\textup{r}}=\tilde{T}_{\textup{AV}}
. If the tags matches, Bob validates Alice and output
F_{\textup{B}}=1
, otherwise, he sets
F_{\textup{B}}=0
.

9.

Bob Validation
:
If
F_{\textup{B}}=1
, Bob generates a tag,
T_{\textup{BV}}=h_{2}(K_{2},\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}})
, and sends
T_{\textup{BV}}
to Alice. If
F_{\textup{B}}=0
, Bob sends a random string of the same length instead. Alice receives the tag
T_{\textup{BV},\textup{r}}
, and computes the verification tag,
\tilde{T}_{\textup{BV}}=h_{2}(K_{2},X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}})
. If
T_{\textup{BV},\textup{r}}=\tilde{T}_{\textup{BV}}
, Alice validates Bob,
D_{\textup{BV}}=1
, otherwise, she sets
D_{\textup{BV}}=0
.

10.

Secret Key Generation and Label Update
: Alice decides whether to accept the session based on her parameter estimation and validation of Bob, i.e.
F_{\textup{A}}=D_{\textup{PE}}\land D_{\textup{BV}}
. If Alice (resp. Bob) accepts the session and decides to perform key generation,
F_{\textup{A}}=1
(resp.
F_{\textup{B}}=1
), she (resp. he) performs privacy amplification
K_{\textup{A}}=h_{\textup{PA}}(R,X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}})
(resp.
K_{\textup{B}}=h_{\textup{PA}}(R,\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}})
) and sets
\alpha=\beta
(resp.
\alpha^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}
), where
K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}
are the cryptographically secure keys that can be used for other purposes. If key generation is not performed, the labels are updated, i.e. if
F_{\textup{A}}=0
, Alice updates her label
\alpha=\beta+1
and if
F_{\textup{B}}=0
, Bob updates his label
\alpha^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}+1
.

The full
n_{sess}
-session protocol begins with two parties, Alice and Bob, sharing secrets
S
containing: (1) authentication keys
K^{\textup{h}}_{1}
and
K_{2}
, (2) privacy amplification seed
R
, and (3) authentication masking key with
m
separate keys
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1}=\{K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j}\}_{j=1,\cdots,m}
.
Alice and Bob each has a label,
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
respectively, that notes the secrets to utilise for the round, thereby allowing secrets to be replaced (by never using them again) when authentication fails.
They also publicly share a
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}
-almost XOR 2-universal hash function
h_{1}
(uses authentication key
K_{1}^{h})
, a
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}
-almost strongly 2-universal hash function
h_{2}
(uses authentication key
K_{2}
) to generate the authentication tags
T_{AV}
and
T_{BV}
respectively
[
34
,
29
,
36
]
, and a 2-universal hash function
h_{\textup{PA}}
(uses privacy amplification seed
R
) for privacy amplification to generate secret keys
[
29
,
36
]
.
Alice and Bob also agrees on a fraction of rounds
f_{P_{1}}
to use as test rounds for parameter estimation and an error correction protocol which includes syndrome generation function
f_{\textup{syn}}
(to generate a syndrome of length
l_{syn}
) and error correction function
f_{\textup{ec}}
(to correct the message using the syndrome).
The protocol steps are summarised in Fig.
2
, with additional details provided in protocol 1 below.
We note here that the subscript
r
on any random variables represent the received variable, e.g.
\alpha_{\textup{r}}
is received by Bob when
\alpha
is sent by Alice, and
\land
refers to the AND gate.
Each protocol session is able to be triggered by either Alice and Bob at any time (e.g. when an ATM card is plugged into an ATM) by for instance using a handshake protocol without authentication.
During the session, Alice and Bob update the parameters
F_{\textup{A}}
and
F_{\textup{B}}
respectively to bit 0 or 1, which indicates whether they wish to accept the session and generate a key.
The values of
F_{\textup{A}}
and
F_{\textup{B}}
are initialised as
\phi
and is always reset independently by Alice and Bob to
F_{\textup{A}}=F_{\textup{B}}=\phi
at the end of each session if they participated in the session, but we analyse the security of the protocol at the point before this reset occurs.
Note that if Alice and Bob did not participate in a session, their value of
F_{\textup{A}}
and
F_{\textup{B}}
respectively would remain as
\phi
.

3
Protocol Security

QKD, while achieving the task of authenticated key exchange (AKE)
[
26
]
, separates the message authentication and key exchange tasks completely, and combines their security using composability arguments.
This makes it difficult to analyse the QAKE protocol directly in the QKD security framework since (1) message authentication is only used in the final two communication steps, which may allow the adversary to alter the ordering of prior steps, and (2) the message authentication steps are also used to check for matching bit strings between Alice and Bob (correctness condition in QKD).
Therefore, we introduce a QAKE security definition which includes both authentication and key exchange, similar to those discussed in Ref.
[
28
]
.

3.1
Multi-Session Security and Reduction to Single-session Security

Let
\rho_{0}
be the initial quantum state, where the shared secrets
S
are generated, and
\mathcal{P}_{i}
be the
i
-th session of the protocol.
For simplicity, we assume that the sessions are sequential, i.e. one session must end before a new session can begin, and we can express
i
sessions of the protocol as
\mathcal{P}^{i}=\mathcal{P}_{i}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{P}_{1}
.
For each session, we can consider an overall input state
\rho^{\textup{in}}
shared across all parties that acts as an input to the channel
\mathcal{P}_{i}
and an output state
\rho^{\textup{out}}=\mathcal{P}_{i}(\rho^{\textup{in}})
shared by all parties at the end of each session.
Note that the output state also acts as the input state to the next session.
We define the security of the multi-session protocol by requiring the output state to be close to an ideal output state for every session, i.e. trace distance between output state and ideal output state is
\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{i}(\rho_{0}),\rho^{\textup{out}})\leq\varepsilon
for all
i=1,\cdots,n_{sess}
, where
\rho^{\textup{out}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
is an ideal output state satisfying QAKE security conditions (to be defined later).
Since each session accepts the secrets (such as authentication key and privacy amplification seeds) as inputs, there could be input states where the secrets are correlated to the adversary.
Therefore, the protocol cannot be composable in the usual sense – that
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is
\varepsilon
-close to an ideal functionality.
However, assuming that these secrets are not additionally leaked between sessions via side-channels, we can restrict the input states to an ideal set of input states
\mathcal{S}_{in}
(to be defined later) that results in the output state being
\varepsilon
-close to an ideal output state
1
1

1

Note that QKD implicitly requires such an assumption when authentication keys are carried between sessions, since any leakage of authentication keys outside of the QKD protocol can compromise QKD security. Additionally, if one can guarantee the input states are ideal (like QKD), it may be possible to define an equivalent protocol
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}
that is composable, where the secrets are generated within each protocol session.
.

We can reduce the multi-session analysis to a single-session security with appropriate definitions of
\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
contains an additional condition that the privacy for the secrets that are utilised in the next session be maintained.
If the protocol maps
\mathcal{S}_{in}
to
\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
, an inductive argument can be made that the protocol is secure – similar to composability arguments, except with restricted input states.
This is presented formally as

Theorem 1
.

Suppose we have the conditions:

1.

The initial state is an ideal input state
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
.

2.

There exists an ideal output state which preserves any necessary secrets that is
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}
-close to the output state of a single session
\mathcal{P}_{i}
, i.e. for any
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
, there exists a state
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
such that

\Delta(\mathcal{P}_{i}(\sigma),\rho)\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}.

3.

The set of ideal output states is a subset of the set of ideal input states
\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
.

Then, for all
i\in[1,n_{sess}]
, there exists
\rho_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
such that

\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{i}(\rho_{0}),\rho_{i})\leq i\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}.

Proof.

See Appendix
C.1
.
∎

The theorem reduces the security analysis to the analysis of single-session protocol.

3.2
Security Conditions

To analyse the single-session security, we define the set of ideal input states
\mathcal{S}_{in}
and output states
\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}
.
We make two assumptions here, that the generated secret keys can be leaked to the adversary between sessions (e.g. if the keys are used for encryption), and that the bitstring
X
is no longer accessible to any party at the end of a session except what is announced or leaked during the session.
We note that the latter assumption is also implicit in device-dependent QKD, where the bit values are freshly sampled in each session
2
2

2

For any correlations between bit values of different sessions, i.e. source correlation, it can be accounted in the same way as QKD, such as introducing a leakage channel.
.

For the ideal input state, we observe that the privacy amplification seed
R
and authentication key
K_{2}
should always be secret between sessions, while authentication masking keys
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i}
that will no longer be utilised (i.e. for indices
i<\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
) can be traced out.
However, the secrets
(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i})
cannot in general be guaranteed to remain private since Alice may reveal tag
T_{\textup{AV}}
to the adversary without an authentication check occurring prior to Alice’s validation step.
This would leak some information on
(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i})
, which we quantify by allowing the adversary in later sessions to generate
T_{\textup{AV}}
for any chosen message
M
prior to that session.
As such, the ideal input state can be defined by
\rho^{in}=\sum_{jj^{\prime}}p_{jj^{\prime}}\rho^{\textup{in},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE}
,

\rho^{\textup{in},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE}=\outerproduct{jj'}{jj'}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes\tau_{K_{2}RK^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j}\cdots K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,m}}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{K_{1}^{h}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j-1}E_{j-1}\rightarrow E}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{E}_{K_{1}^{h}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}E_{j^{\prime}}\rightarrow E_{j^{\prime}+1}}(\tau_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}\cdots K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j-1}}\otimes\rho_{E_{j^{\prime}}}),

(1)

where
\tau_{A}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{A}}}\outerproduct{a}{a}
is a uniformly distributed state of variable
A
,
\mathcal{E}_{K_{1}^{h}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i}E_{i}\rightarrow E_{i+1}}
are quantum channels that allow the adversary to generate
T_{\textup{AV}}
for any chosen message,
L=\alpha\alpha^{\prime}
are the labels and
S=K_{1}^{h}K_{2}RK^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j}\cdots K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,m}
refer to the secrets.

The set of ideal output states is defined based on the ideal functionality given the ideal input states, which are required to satisfy a mix of QKD and authentication conditions as well as the requirement for
\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{in}
.
As such, we adapt a recently proposed classical authenticated key exchange (AKE) framework
[
13
]
, where the tasks of authentication and key secrecy are defined and their relation made explicit.
Let
\rho_{B}^{a}
be the conditional state of system
B
conditioned on event
A=a
, and
\rho_{B\land A=a}=p_{a}\rho_{B}^{a}
be the sub-normalised conditional state.
This gives us a set of five security conditions (including one from
\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{in}
), given by

1.

Robustness
: In the absence of any adversary, the authentication should pass with high probability, i.e.
\Pr[F_{\textup{A}}=F_{\textup{B}}=1]\geq 1-\varepsilon_{\textup{rob}}
.

2.

Explicit Entity Authentication
: When Alice accepts the QAKE session,
F_{\textup{A}}=1
, Bob must have accepted as well,
F_{\textup{B}}=1
. This condition on Alice, termed full (f) explicit entity authentication, requires that
\Pr[F_{\textup{A}}=1,F_{\textup{B}}\neq 1]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,f}}
. A similar condition, termed almost-full (af) explicit entity authentication, requires that when Bob accepts the session,
F_{\textup{B}}=1
, Alice must be involved in the session, i.e.
F_{\textup{A}}\neq\phi
. As such, the condition requires that
\Pr[F_{\textup{A}}=\phi,F_{\textup{B}}=1]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,af}}
, with
\varepsilon_{\textup{EA}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,af}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,f}}
(Recall
F_{\textup{A}}=\phi
if Alice did not participate in the session).

3.

Match Security
: When Alice and Bob accepts the authentication, they will generate the same key, i.e.
\Pr[K_{\textup{A}}\neq K_{\textup{B}},F_{\textup{A}}=1,F_{\textup{B}}=1]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}
.

4.

Key Secrecy
: When keys are generated by Bob, they should remain uniform and secret from the adversary, i.e.
\rho_{K_{B}LSE\land(F_{A},F_{B})\in\{01,11\}}
is
\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}
-close in trace distance to
\tau_{K_{B}}\otimes\rho_{LSE\land(F_{A},F_{B})\in\{01,11\}}
.

5.

Shared Secrets Privacy
: The privacy of the shared secrets
S
should be maintained as necessary, which is achieved by the output state being
\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}}
-close to an ideal input state in
\mathcal{S}_{in}
. More formally,
\sum_{(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}})\not\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{SP}}}p_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\rho^{f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}}}_{LSE}
is
\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}}
-close in trace distance to
\sum_{(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}})\not\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{SP}}}p_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\sigma^{f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}}}_{LSE}
, where
\sigma^{f_{A},f_{B}}_{LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
,
p_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}
is the probability of
(F_{A},F_{B})=(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}})
and
\mathcal{S}_{SP}=\{0\phi,\phi 0,10\}
.

The property of explicit entity authentication highlights an observation, also discussed in the context of QKD
[
29
,
21
,
28
]
, where the abort decision may not be shared by both Alice and Bob, and one party could choose to generate keys while the other does not.
This condition is imposed on Alice and Bob differently.
Since Alice is the receiver of the final authentication tag, when Bob accepts and sends this final tag, he is unable to guarantee that Alice will accept since this final tag can be modified by the adversary, as noted also in Ref.
[
21
]
.
We note that the match security condition matches the correctness condition in QKD and the key secrecy matches the secrecy condition in QKD.
For shared secrets privacy, the events
(F_{A},F_{B})=(0\phi),(\phi 0),(10)
are excluded since these events are accounted for in explicit entity authentication.

The steps to generalize the AKE framework to the quantum regime could be of independent interest, since similar the classical AKE framework could be generalized to other quantum variants.
Examples include quantum anonymous key exchange where authentication is made one-way, or QAKE with computationally secure message authentication.
The generalization of the AKE framework to QAKE can be found in Appendix
B
.

The set of ideal output states would thus be those satisfying conditions 2 to 5 perfectly.
The explicit entity authentication condition will define the combinations of
F_{A}F_{B}
present in the ideal output state, with the match security requiring
K_{A}=K_{B}
and key secrecy enforcing independence of the keys from
E
.
The shared secrets privacy requirement enforces that the output state forms a mixture of
\sigma^{f_{A}f_{B}}_{LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
.
Therefore, the set of ideal output state
\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}=\{\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}\}
contains states of the form

\begin{split}&\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}=\outerproduct{\perp\perp}{\perp\perp}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\left[p_{0\phi}\outerproduct{0\phi}{0\phi}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sigma^{0\phi}_{LSE}+p_{\phi 0}\outerproduct{\phi 0}{\phi 0}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sigma^{\phi 0}_{LSE}+p_{00}\outerproduct{00}{00}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sigma^{00}_{LSE}\right]\\
&+p_{01}\outerproduct{01,\perp}{01,\perp}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}K_{\textup{A}}}\otimes\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sigma^{01}_{LSE}+p_{11}\outerproduct{11}{11}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\tilde{\tau}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sigma^{11}_{LSE},\end{split}

(2)

where
\tilde{\tau}_{K_{A}K_{B}}=\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{K}}}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\outerproduct{kk}{kk}_{K_{A}K_{B}}
is a uniformly distributed state of variable
K_{A}
and
K_{B}
, with
K_{A}=K_{B}
.
The single-session security is thus given by the statement: for any
\sigma_{LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
, there exists
\rho_{F_{A}F_{B}K_{A}K_{B}LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}
such that
\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\sigma_{LSE}),\rho_{F_{A}F_{B}K_{A}K_{B}LSE})\leq\varepsilon_{sec,int}
.

To simplify the security analysis, we can reduce the trace distance to the security conditions, similarly to how QKD security can be reduced to correctness and secrecy,

Theorem 2
.

Consider a protocol
\mathcal{P}
with an ideal input state
\sigma_{LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
. Then, overall protocol security is satisfied when the security parameter is

\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{EA}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}}.

Proof.

See Appendix
C.2
.
∎

3.3
Single-session Security

The defined set of input and output states satisfy conditions 1 and 3 in Thm.
1
trivially, while the remaining condition is demonstrated in the theorem below, where we assume an ideal state preparation and fair sampling (transmission
\eta
is basis-independent).
A sketch of the security proof is provided below, with the full security proof presented in Appendix
C.3
.

Theorem 3
.

The QAKE protocol
\mathcal{P}
is secure with security parameters

\begin{gathered}\varepsilon_{\textup{EA}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+2^{-P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}[1+h_{\textup{bin}}(f_{P_{1}})]+\log_{2}(P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}+1)}+\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}}\\
\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}\\
\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}=2\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1}}+\frac{1}{2}\times 2^{-\frac{1}{2}[H^{\prime}-l_{K_{\textup{B}}}]}+\varepsilon_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\\
\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{SP},01,11}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},\end{gathered}

where
l_{K_{B}}
is the length of
K_{B}
,
\varepsilon_{serf,1}
is a penalty term associated with the use of modified Serfling bound
[
39
,
12
]
to estimate the single-photon bit error rate in
P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}
from that of
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}
. The penalty associated with shifting to an idealised protocol
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}=2\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{SO}}\right)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\textup{SO}}=2^{-P^{\textup{sift}}_{tol}}+2^{-N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,1}}^{\textup{tol}}}+2^{-P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}h_{\textup{bin}}(f_{P_{1}})+\log_{2}(P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}+1)},

the shared secrets privacy parameter for the idealised protocol when
F_{\textup{B}}=1
is

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\textup{SP},01,11}^{\prime}=4\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1}}+2(\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{3})

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{\textup{SP},\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{SP},\textup{MAC},2}

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\textup{SP},\textup{MAC},1}=\sqrt{(\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}-1)+2^{\log_{2}\left(\frac{2+\varepsilon_{3}}{\varepsilon_{3}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}}\right)-H^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\textup{SP},\textup{MAC},2}=\sqrt{(\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}-1)+2^{\log_{2}\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{2}}+1\right)-H^{\prime}}},

the smooth min-entropy term is

H^{\prime}=N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})]-2-\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}-\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}},

\varepsilon_{2}
and
\varepsilon_{3}
are parameters to optimise over,
\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{AV}}
and
\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{BV}}
are the size (dimension) of the respective tag,
P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}
is the minimum tolerated number of sifted rounds,
\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}
accounts for errors associated with decoy state analysis,
N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,1}}^{\textup{tol}}
is the minimum tolerated number of single-photon rounds in set
P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}
, and
e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime}
is the maximum tolerated single-photon phase error rate in set
P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}
, and
\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}
is the information leakage due to error correction.

Proof Sketch.

The explicit entity authentication security condition relies on the fact that when
F_{\textup{A}}=\phi
(resp.
F_{\textup{B}}\neq 1
), the tag
T_{\textup{AV}}
(resp.
T_{\textup{BV}}
) is not generated.
As such, the adversary has to guess a correct message-tag pair to trigger
F_{B}=1
(resp.
F_{A}=1
).
By the nature of the strong 2-universal hash function, the probability guessing a correct message-tag pair is small.

For match security, like the correctness condition of QKD, the security is based on error verification. Bob’s tag
T_{\textup{BV}}
involves the corrected bitstring
\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}
, which has to be checked against tag
\tilde{T}_{\textup{BV}}
involving
X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}
.
If the bitstrings do not match (and thus the keys are mismatched as well), the probability of matching tags are small.

For key secrecy, message authentication provided by the authentication steps allows the security condition to be reduced to that of QKD with “idealised” message authentication and where protocol step ordering is obeyed.
The proof
then follows from
that of QKD, where we seek to lower bound the smooth min-entropy
H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}}(\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}|\beta K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K_{2}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}E)
.

Shared secrets privacy requires that necessary secrets remain private.
The main protection on
K_{2}
and
(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta})
when the protocol succeeds and they are reused is based on the value of
\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}
included as part of the message while generating tags
T_{\textup{BV}}
and
T_{\textup{AV}}
.
Based on the ideal key privacy property
[
17
]
along with quantum leftover hash lemma
[
41
]
, the overall hash function
h_{1}
with mask and
h_{2}
are
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}
and
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}
strong extractors respectively, thereby preserving the secrecy of the authentication and mask keys
K_{2}
and
(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta})
.
∎

The final condition to consider is the robustness of the protocol.
The main sources of non-acceptance of a session would be either failure of the parameter estimation checks or error correction failure.
We can use concentration bounds
[
48
]
to compute the probability of failure of the parameter estimation checks, which we label as
\varepsilon_{\textup{ds},\textup{rob}}
for terms associated with the decoy state checks,
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{E},P_{1},\textup{rob}}
for the bit error check in set
P_{1}
, and
\varepsilon_{\textup{rob},P}
for terms associated with bounding
\absolutevalue{P}
(bounds
P^{\textup{tol}}\leq\absolutevalue{P}\leq P^{\textup{UB}}
).
The error correction failures can either be due to the bit error rate in set
P_{2}
exceeding some tolerance bound, or failure to correct for errors even within the tolerance bound.
In the former case, knowing the bit error rate in set
P_{1}
falls below
e_{\textup{bit},1,\textup{tol}}
, the probability that the error rate in set
P_{2}
exceeding some tolerance value
e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}^{\prime}
can be computed based on the modified Serfling bound
[
39
,
12
]
, with failure probability
\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},2}
.
The failure probability of the latter case depends on the error correction protocol utilised, and we define the error probability as
\varepsilon_{\textup{EC}}
for total communication
l_{syn}
-bits during the error correction phase.
As such, the overall robustness can be quantified by
\varepsilon_{\textup{rob}}
,

\varepsilon_{\textup{rob}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{rob},P}+\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},2}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ds},\textup{rob}}+\varepsilon_{\mathcal{E},P_{1},\textup{rob}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{EC}}.

(3)

4
Numerical Analysis

We analyse the performance of the QAKE protocol by simulating the length of keys generated based on Thm.
3
.
We assume a simple experimental model with Alice preparing decoy BB84 states, sending it through a channel with loss
\eta
, and Bob performing measurement in a random basis, i.e. with detection probability
p_{\textup{det},\mu_{j}}=p_{\mu_{j}}(1-e^{-\eta\mu_{j}})
.
Additionally, Bob’s detector is assumed to have zero dark counts.
We fix
\mu_{0}=0.45
,
\mu_{1}=0.225
,
\mu_{2}=0
as the decoy intensities,
p_{\mu_{0}}=0.2
,
p_{\mu_{1}}=0.6
and
p_{\mu_{2}}=0.2
as their respective probabilities.
We also fix the length of the authentication tags to be 80 bits each, with
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}=\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}=2^{-80}
, i.e. the authentication is 2-universal, allowing us to use the tighter bound for
\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}}
shown in Thm.
19
.
The error correction is assumed to utilise
30\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
of the sifted bits.

The simulation is then performed by rearranging the result in Thm.
3
, and optimising
l_{K_{\textup{B}}}
over the splitting ratio (also size of test set)
f_{P_{1}}
, robustness and secrecy parameters (components of
\varepsilon_{rob}
and
\varepsilon_{sec,int}
) in Matlab, with other parameters fixed as described in the results.
We choose the security parameter of each session of the protocol as
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}=

1\text{\times}{10}^{-15}\text{\,}
, which yields an overall protocol security of
1\text{\times}{10}^{-6}\text{\,}
when we allow it to run up to
1\text{\times}{10}^{9}\text{\,}
sessions.
The robustness parameter is chosen at
\varepsilon_{\textup{rob}}=

1\text{\times}{10}^{-10}\text{\,}
, and each session involves
n_{rnd}=

1\text{\times}{10}^{10}\text{\,}
signals being sent by Alice to Bob.
The concentration bound used for robustness parameters is the tight bound on binomial distribution
[
48
]
due to i.i.d. state preparation noting independence of the detection probability on basis selection by fair sampling assumption.
The concentration bound used for estimation of the expectation values,
\mathbb{E}[N_{\mu_{v},P_{i}}]
, from the observed values,
N_{\mu_{v},P_{i}}
, associated with decoy state estimation (errors form part of decoy state related parameters
\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}
) is Kato’s bound
[
19
,
11
,
46
]
.
The simulation results are shown in Fig.
3
for various QBER values
Q
.

In terms of key generation rate, QAKE has a slightly higher key rate than standard QKD, though the extent of improvement depends on the length of the authentication tags used and the number of communication rounds.
If we use the QAKE protocol and revert it to a standard QKD protocol, i.e. using authenticated communication channel and having error verification step, one would need to generate seven authentication tags (2 for label agreement, 2 for sifting, 1 for test round announcement, 1 for error correction, 1 for error verification) instead of two, leading to an additional 400 bits of key utilized.
Therefore, QAKE may provide a significant advantage when key generation rates are low, such as when block sizes are small (e.g. satellite QKD) or loss is high, or when there is a large number of communication rounds (e.g. for Cascade) – since more bits have to be sacrificed for message authentication.

Figure 3:
Simulated key length for QAKE protocol at various signal loss values (including detector loss), various QBER values
Q
, with
n_{rnd}=

2\text{\times}{10}^{10}\text{\,}
signals sent,
\varepsilon_{\textup{rob}}=

1\text{\times}{10}^{-10}\text{\,}
, and
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}=

1\text{\times}{10}^{-15}\text{\,}
. The authentication tag used is 80-bits, i.e.
\absolutevalue{T_{\AV}}=\absolutevalue{T_{\BV}}=80
, and we assume the error correction to utilise
30\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
of the sifted bits. The system parameters are chosen to be
\mu_{0}=0.45
,
\mu_{1}=0.225
,
\mu_{2}=0
as the decoy intensities,
p_{\mu_{0}}=0.2
,
p_{\mu_{1}}=0.6
and
p_{\mu_{2}}=0.2
as their respective probabilities, and we assume the system to have zero dark counts.

5
Experimental Validation

5.1
Experimental Setup

We validated our results by implementing the QAKE protocol on the ID Quantique Clavis XGR QKD systems, with the setup shown in Fig.
4
.
We connected Alice and Bob in a back-to-back configuration with an optical attenuator of 10 dB emulating a fiber distance of
45\text{\,}\mathrm{km}
(
0.22\text{\,}\mathrm{dB}\text{\,}{\mathrm{km}}^{-1}
).
We also configured the intensity
\mu_{0}=0.45
and the probability of Alice selecting each basis to about
50\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
.
The QKD systems provide QKD raw data, which includes the encoded qubit values from the transmitter (Alice) and the measured qubit values from the receiver (Bob) during the key exchange process.
We collected the raw data using an Application Programming Interface (API) command in a text format, which was utilised to estimate the QBER and detection rate.

Figure 4:
Experimental setup implementing the protocol, with
10\text{\,}\mathrm{dB}
of channel loss from a
45\text{\,}\mathrm{km}
fiber (emulated with an optical attenuator). The quantum state preparation and measurement are performed by the ID Quantique Clavis XGR QKD systems, which outputs the relevant raw data (including state prepared and measurement outcomes). Post-selection is performed on the raw data to match the protocol requirements of having balanced (
50\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
) detector basis choice. The remaining steps of the protocols are then simulated (including error correction, authentication tag generation and privacy amplification) using the post-selected data.

Due to the lack of a direct interface between the QKD system’s raw data output and our FPGA, the post-processing of the raw data was performed offline (not in real-time).
Firstly, to balance the basis detection rates, we performed post-selection of the data such that the detection probability in each basis is about
50\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
.
Secondly, sifting is performed to ensure that Alice and Bob has matching basis.
The data is then utilised to run the protocol in a software stack.
This includes the use of Toeplitz hashing as the XOR two-universal hash function and as the the two-universal hash function respectively for the authentication step and privacy amplification steps, and the use of a two-way
low-density parity-check
(LDPC) code for error correction.
Details of the LDPC and Toeplitz hashing implementation are presented in the subsequent subsections.

We utilise similar parameters for authentication tag size as the numerical simulation, while the number of bits communicated for error correction is set to a maximum of
30\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
of the size of set
P_{2}
(containing bits used for key generation) based on LDPC performance.

5.2
Toeplitz Hashing

Toeplitz hashing is implemented on a Virtex Ultrascale+ XCVU9p FPGA, running at a clock frequency of 332 MHz.
Due to the limitation of resources in FPGA, we decomposed the large Toeplitz matrix evenly into small submatrix multiplication steps
[
47
]
.
We designed pipelined modules for Toeplitz submatrix construction, matrix multiplication and intermediate accumulation for processing 32 input bits from the FPGA interface on every clock cycle.
The matrix construction module consists of a 32-bit wide FIFO that stores the large Toeplitz matrix, and a shift register that stores the submatrix.
On every clock cycle, the submatrix is constructed by reading 32-bits from the FIFO and using it to shifting the register.
Matrix multiplication is realized by bitwise-AND and XOR-reduction.
An accumulation register performs bitwise-XOR for matrix multiplication outputs on each clock cycle, to generate the final output bits.

5.3
Error Correction

For error correction, we implemented a two-way
low-density parity-check
(LDPC) code on the same FPGA by adapting LDPC IP cores from Xilinx
[
1
]
.
We used the following settings on the LDPC IP: base matrix choice
\text{bg}=0
, syndrome length control
\text{mb}=5
, codeword length controls
z_{\text{set}}=5
and
z_{j}=5
, normalisation factor
\text{sc}_{\text{idx}}=14
, and scaling
\text{llr}_{\text{scale}}=14
.
This results in a codeword size of 1188 bytes and syndrome size of 220 bytes.
The LDPC IP core is designed for standard error correction usage (e.g. in 5G), where the syndrome is also sent through the channel and experiences the same error rate as the data, unlike in QAKE where the syndrome is assumed to be error-free.
Let
X
be a 1188-byte data string (
X^{\prime}
being Bob’s data string),
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
be the first 968 bytes and last 220 bytes of data, and
syn
be the syndrome. The adapted protocol we use is as follows:

1.

For every 1188 bytes of data, Alice generates a 220-byte syndrome from the first 968 bytes of data,
X_{1}
, and XOR the syndrome with
X_{2}
, i.e.
syn^{\prime}=syn\oplus X_{1}
.

2.

Alice computes a 16-byte (128-bit) hash of the data string using SHA-256 (second half of the hash is discarded),
T=\text{SHA256}(X)
.

3.

Alice sends
syn^{\prime}
and
T
to Bob.

4.

Bob performs XOR of his last 220 bytes of data with the syndrome, i.e.
syn^{\prime\prime}=syn^{\prime}\oplus X_{2}^{\prime}
. Note this is equivalent to introducing the error bits
X_{2}^{\prime}\oplus X_{2}
onto the syndrome,
syn^{\prime\prime}
.

5.

Bob performs error correction with
X_{1}^{\prime}
and
syn^{\prime\prime}
using the LDPC IP core, and obtains a corrected data string
\hat{X}_{1}
and corrected syndrome
\hat{syn}
.

6.

Bob computes the correction on the second part of his data string, with
\hat{X}_{2}=X_{2}^{\prime}\oplus\hat{syn}\oplus syn^{\prime}
.

7.

Bob computes the hash of the corrected data string using SHA-256,
T^{\prime}=\text{SHA256}(\hat{X})
, and compares it with the hash sent by Alice.

8.

If the hash does not match, Bob will inform Alice to increase the syndrome length by 44 bytes, and send that additional 44 bytes to Bob.

9.

Alice and Bob repeat steps 4 to 8 until the hash matches. If the syndrome length longer than the maximum transmission unit (MTU) size the FPGA can support (mb = 10, syndrome = 440 bytes, maximum of 6 rounds), Alice will simply reveal all 1188 bytes of data to Bob instead.

Based on our tests for a bit error rate of
2\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
, the LDPC code can almost always succeed with a sacrifice of at most
30\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
of the sifted bitstring.

Table 1:

n_{rnd}
number of signals sent (after post-selection) for each run. The detection probability per signal,
p_{\textup{det}}
, and average bit error rate is computed from the measurement results. The key length is optimised over the various epsilon terms, including errors associated with decoy state method,
\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}
, and the modified Serfling bound error for single-photon bit error rate
\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1}
, with the optimal values presented here, for secrecy parameter
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}=

1\text{\times}{10}^{-15}\text{\,}
.

5.4
Experimental Results

We run the protocol a total of three separate times, and fix a testing probability of
25\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
, which is close to the optimal testing probability at the same transmission range in the numerical simulation.
Table
1
show the results obtained for the various runs.
All three runs recorded around
2\text{\times}{10}^{10}\text{\,}
signals sent after post-selection, with an average bit error rate of
2\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
and key length of
2\text{\times}{10}^{5}\text{\,}
.
This is roughly in line with the simulated results for an ideal setting presented in Fig.
3
.

6
Discussion

We presented a QAKE protocol that incorporates entity authentication with key expansion.
It compresses the authentication steps to the last two communication steps to reduce authentication cost.
The protocol is also designed to allow for key recycling, where the authentication keys and masking keys need not be refreshed after each protocol session.
Key recycling can be useful for applications since communication overhead from the sharing of the refreshed secrets by the server (e.g. between all remote access servers) can be removed when the protocol passes.

Open Problems:
There are some limitations to the presented protocol and security analysis, which can open up areas that can be explored in the future.

An interesting area to explore is the expansion of QAKE or QKD to achieve other tasks within the classical AKE framework.
For instance, classical AKE can allow for anonymous key exchange by not requiring mutual authentication for all parties (perhaps necessary only to authenticate the client but not for the client to authenticate the server), or allow for client-server authentication (without key generation).
Such expansion of tasks may require protocol changes that can be informed by the modifications within the classical AKE framework, which is well-studied by the classical cryptography community.

It is also worthwhile to have a more comprehensive AKE framework that incorporates both classical and quantum AKE.
This brings the analysis closer to a practical setting, where both classical authentication and key exchange methods may live with QAKE and other quantum protocols.
Analysing the interplay between these protocols and their respective guarantees can provide insight into how these diverse systems can be incorporated in practice, and allow us to understand the types of attacks and risks we should consider in QKD or QAKE design.
Moreover, such an endeavour can build a common language with classical cryptography that would make QKD or similar quantum cryptographic protocol security more accessible.

Additional Results:
Pre-sharing of basis information between Alice and Bob can avoid penalties to key rate from sifting, but renders the protocol inefficient due to the large size of
\theta
required.
The most practical solution to this problem is to use a shorter pre-shared basis seed and generate a longer string as the shared basis from a quantum-safe
pseudorandom number generator
(PRNG) like AES
[
16
]
.
In Appendix
D
, we present a QAKE protocol that is adapted from the authentication protocol by Fehr et. al.
[
17
]
, which utilises pre-shared pseudorandom basis, and we present a formal analysis of the use of pseudorandom basis in decoy state BB84 in Appendix
E
.
We note that the secret keys generated remains information-theoretic secure, provided that the adversary is computationally bounded during the protocol itself.
Inspired by the authentication achieved by Fehr et. al.
[
17
]
with a single communication step, we also developed a two-step (one challenge and one response) client authentication protocol that is secure in the practical setting (with channel loss, noise and multi-photon events).
This result is presented in Appendix
F
.

Appendix A
Preliminaries

A.1
Quantum Systems

The state of a generic quantum system
A
can be represented by a density matrix
\rho_{A}
.
For a classical random variable
Y
that takes on values
y\in\mathcal{Y}
according to probability distribution
p_{y}
, it can be expressed as a quantum state
\rho_{Y}=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}p_{y}\tilde{\Pi}_{Y}^{y}
,
where
\tilde{\Pi}_{Y}^{y}=\outerproduct{y}{y}_{Y}
for simplicity.
Two special classical states are the uniformly distributed system, with
p_{y}=\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{Y}}}
represented by
\tau_{Y}
, and the uniformly distributed systems with matching values,
\tilde{\tau}_{YY^{\prime}}
, where
p_{y}=\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{Y}}}
and
y=y^{\prime}
for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}
.
A classical-quantum state with classical random variable
A
and quantum subsystem
B
can be expressed as

\rho_{AB}=\sum_{a}p_{a}\tilde{\Pi}_{A}^{a}\otimes\rho_{B}^{a},

where
\rho_{B}^{a}
(or sometimes
\rho_{B|A=a}
) is the quantum state of subsystem
B
conditioned on event
A=a
.
We further define the sub-normalized conditional state
\rho_{B\land A=a}
as
p_{a}\rho_{B}^{a}
.
We label a quantum channel that maps inputs
I
to output
O
, with fixed inputs
a
by
\mathcal{E}_{I\rightarrow O}^{a}
.
The symbol
\mathcal{E}
is used to label quantum channels controlled by an adversary while letters
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
are used to label quantum channels corresponding to actions by honest or hypothetical parties.
We label a quantum measurement on system
A
with outcome
X=x
by the general measurement operator
F_{A}^{x}
, with corresponding positive operator-value measure (POVM)
\Pi_{A}^{x}=(O_{A}^{x})^{\dagger}O_{A}^{x}
.
For projections onto an orthonormal basis, the POVMs (also general measurement operators) are expressed as projectors,
\{\tilde{\Pi}_{Y}^{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}
.

A.2
Quantum Information Theory

We use the generalised trace distance measure to measure the distinguishability of two (possibly sub-normalized) quantum states
\rho
and
\sigma

[
42
]
,

\Delta(\rho,\sigma):=\frac{1}{2}\norm{\rho-\sigma}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{Tr(\rho-\sigma)},

(4)

where
\norm{\cdot}_{1}
is the trace norm.
Another useful distance measure is the purified distance,

P(\rho,\sigma)=\sqrt{1-\left[\Tr\absolutevalue{\sqrt{\rho}\sqrt{\sigma}}+\sqrt{(1-\Tr\rho)(1-\Tr\sigma)}\right]^{2}},

(5)

which can be bounded by the trace distance,
P(\rho,\sigma)\leq\sqrt{2\Delta(\rho,\sigma)}
.
The min-entropy is defined as
[
42
]

H_{\textup{min}}(A|E)_{\rho_{AE}}:=\sup\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\sigma_{B}:\rho_{AE}\leq 2^{-\lambda}\mathbb{I}_{A}\otimes\sigma_{B}\},

(6)

with max-entropy defined as a min-entropy through the duality relation,
H_{\textup{max}}(A|B)_{\rho}:=-H_{\textup{min}}(A|E)_{\rho}
for pure
\rho_{ABE}
.
The smooth min- and max-entropy are defined as

\begin{gathered}H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(A|E)_{\rho_{AE}}:=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho^{\prime}_{AE}:\Tr[\rho_{AE}^{\prime}]\leq 1,\\
P(\rho_{AE},\rho_{AE}^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon\end{subarray}}H_{\textup{min}}(A|E)_{\rho_{AE}^{\prime}}\\
H_{\textup{max}}^{\varepsilon}(A|E)_{\rho_{AE}}:=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho^{\prime}_{AE}:\Tr[\rho_{AE}^{\prime}]\leq 1,\\
P(\rho_{AE},\rho_{AE}^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon\end{subarray}}H_{\textup{max}}(A|E)_{\rho_{AE}^{\prime}}\end{gathered}

(7)

A.3
Two-Universal Hash Functions

For information-theoretic authentication schemes constructed from universal hash functions, a client generates a valid tag from the hash function with an authentication key and the message, which is then verified by a server with the same hash function and authentication key.
We note that there are alternative frameworks of authentication with quantum access to the hash function based on unforgeability
[
15
]
, but we assume here that only classical access to the hash function is allowed, given that the adversary in our proposed protocols has no direct access to the hash function.
Utilising a
\varepsilon
-almost strong 2-universal hash function prevents message tampering,

Definition 1

(
\varepsilon
-almost strong 2-universal hash function
[
36
,
29
]
)
.

A family of hash functions
\{h(k,.):\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}\}_{k\in\mathcal{K}}
is
\varepsilon
-almost strongly 2-universal if for all messages
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathcal{M}
and
m_{1}\neq m_{2}
, and all
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
,

\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{K}}}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\Pr[h(k,m_{1})=t_{1}\land h(k,m_{2})=t_{2}]\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}}},

and

\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{K}}}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\Pr[h(k,m)=t]=\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}}}.

An
\varepsilon
-almost strong 2-universal hash function
h
can be formed from an
\varepsilon
-almost XOR 2-universal hash function
h^{\prime}
where the average probability of
h^{\prime}(k,m_{1})\oplus h^{\prime}(k,m_{2})=t
for any
t
is bounded by
\varepsilon

[
34
]
, and a authentication masking key (used as a one-time pad),
h(k,m)=h^{\prime}(k^{\textup{h}},m)\oplus k^{\textup{mask}}

[
9
,
29
]
.
A weaker notion of universality can be defined, where an
\varepsilon
-almost 2-universal hash function refers to one where the average probability of
h(m_{1})=h(m_{2})
is bounded by
\varepsilon

[
41
]
.

It is clear that if the authentication masking key is never used again after the hash is performed,
K^{\textup{h}}
remains secure and tag
T
appears as a random string to the adversary,

Theorem 4
.

Let
h^{\prime}
be a
\varepsilon
-almost XOR 2-universal hash function with authentication key
K^{h}
, and
h(k,m)=h^{\prime}(k^{\textup{h}},m)\oplus k^{\textup{mask}}
, with a fully random masking string
K^{\textup{mask}}
. Consider an adversary which was provided with a valid message-tag pair
(m,t)
, and the honest party which discards (trace away)
K^{\textup{mask}}
(i.e.
K^{\textup{mask}}
acts as a one-time pad). The seed
K^{\textup{h}}
remains private, in the sense that

\rho_{K^{\textup{h}}MTE}=\tau_{K^{\textup{h}}}\otimes\tau_{T}\otimes\rho_{ME}.

A property of
\varepsilon
-almost 2-universal hash functions that is useful is that they are strong extractors.
This is demonstrated from the quantum leftover hash lemma (QLHL)
[
41
,
38
]
,

Theorem 5

(Quantum leftover hash lemma)
.

Let
h
be a
\varepsilon
-almost 2-universal hash function with key
K
. Consider a protocol which begins with state
\tau_{K}\otimes\rho_{ME}
, and the hash is computed as
Y=h(K,M)
.
Then, for any
\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}\geq 0
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
,

\Delta(\rho_{YKE},\tau_{Y}\otimes\tau_{K}\otimes\rho_{E})\leq 2\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}+2\varepsilon^{\prime}+\sqrt{2^{l}\varepsilon-1+2^{l-H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(M|E)+\log_{2}\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{{}^{\prime}2}}+1\right)}},

where
l=\absolutevalue{Y}
is the length of tag
Y
.
We call the hash function a
\delta
-strong extractor, where
\delta
matches the RHS of the inequality.
If
h
is instead 2-universal, then

\Delta(\rho_{YKE},\tau_{Y}\otimes\tau_{K}\otimes\rho_{E})\leq 2\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}+\frac{1}{2}\times 2^{-\frac{1}{2}[H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(M|E)-l]}.

A.4
Pseudorandom Number Generation

The security of cryptographically-secure PRNGs are defined by the probability that any distinguishing protocol with limited resources can distinguish between the PRNG output and an ideal random number generator (IRNG) output
[
20
]
.
We note that this security is in general composable when considering computational security.
[
7
,
8
]
.
We can consider quantum adversaries in general since the QAKE protocol involves quantum steps.
Since we are interested in computing key generation rates, we adopt a more concrete security definition with resources
t

[
4
,
14
]
(e.g. time) instead of presenting security against quantum polynomial-time (QPT) adversaries
3
3

3

QPT adversaries are adversaries with polynomial access to both classical and quantum resources (e.g. polynomial storage and computational power)
.

Definition 2

(Quantum-secure PRNG)
.

A pseudorandom number generator (PRNG) is a function
G^{\textup{PRNG}}:\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
that takes as input a seed
k\in\mathcal{K}
with length
l
and outputs a bit string
y\in\mathcal{Y}
with length
n
, where
l<n
.
A
(l,n)
-standard PRNG is
(t,\varepsilon)
quantum-secure if for any quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{D}^{t}
constrained by some resources
t
with output
D
, the PRNG equipped with a random seed
G^{\textup{PRNG}}_{\mathcal{K}}
is indistinguishable from an ideal random number generator
G^{\textup{IRNG}}
that samples random numbers
r
uniformly at random from
\mathcal{Y}
,

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\vars{A}^{G^{\PRNG}_{\vars{K}},t}_{D}=1]-\Pr[\vars{A}^{G^{\TRNG},t}_{D}=1]}\leq\varepsilon.

One important property of the PRNG is the difficulty in guessing the seed of the PRNG when given partial information of the output, which can only succeed with a small probability
\varepsilon_{\textup{guess}}
, as summarised below.

Theorem 6
.

Consider a scenario where a random number
y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
is prepared from a
(t^{\prime},\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}})
-quantum-secure PRNG with a random seed
k
of length
l
with security parameter
\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}
, i.e.
y=G^{\textup{PRNG}}(k)
, and a
n
-bit bit string
z
, with
\Pr[z_{i}=1]=p_{1}
, for
i=1,\cdots,n
, with
n>2l
. Suppose an adversary is provided with
y_{z=1}:=\{y_{i}:z_{i}=1\}
,
G^{\textup{PRNG}}
and
z
, and is tasked to guess the seed
k
. When
t^{\prime}\geq t+t_{SG,PR}
, the probability that any quantum adversary with resource upper bounded by
t
can correctly guess
k
is bounded by

\Pr[\hat{k}=k]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{guess}}

where
\varepsilon_{\textup{guess}}\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}+2^{-n+l}
, and
t_{SG,PR}
is the total resources required for generating string
z
and generating
y
from a master key using the PRNG.

Proof.

We start with noting that correctly guessing the seed allows one to guess all the outcomes
y
from the PRNG. If there is an algorithm which can guess the seed from the set
y_{z=1}
, it can be used to distinguish whether a string
y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
is being generated from a PRNG or from an IRNG. Therefore, it will violate the security of the
(t^{\prime},\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}})
-PRNG.

Suppose for contradiction that an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
with resources
t
is able to guess the seed
k
correctly with probability at least
\varepsilon_{\textup{guess}}>2^{-l}
(note
\varepsilon_{\textup{guess}}\leq 2^{-l}
is trivially achievable with random guess), i.e.,

\Pr[\mathcal{A}(y_{z=1})=k]>\varepsilon_{\textup{guess}}.

(8)

We construct an algorithm
\mathcal{\hat{A}}
that distinguishes
G^{\textup{PRNG}}(k)
from
G^{\textup{IRNG}}
as follows:

1.

Upon receiving a string
y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, it generates a string
z=z_{1}\ldots z_{n}
, where
z_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, and
\Pr[z_{i}=1]=p_{1}
, for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

2.

From
z
, and
y
, it constructs the set
y_{z=1}
, and feed it to the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
to guess the seed. Suppose
\mathcal{A}(y_{z=1})=\hat{k}
.

3.

If
G^{\textup{PRNG}}(\hat{k})=y
, the algorithm returns
1
, indicating
y
is being generated via a PRNG.

4.

Otherwise, the algorithm returns
0
, indicating
y
is sampled via an IRNG.

The additional resources required for the generation of string
z
and generation of
y
from the master key guess
\hat{k}
is defined as
t_{SG,PR}
.
As such,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
requires
t+t_{SG,PR}
resources.

When the protocol is implemented on the PRNG, it is clear that if the guess of
\hat{k}
is correct, i.e.
\hat{k}=k
, the algorithm returns 1.
Therefore,

\Pr[\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{G^{\textup{PRNG}},t+t_{SG,PR}}=1]>\varepsilon_{\textup{guess}}.

When the protocol is implemented on an IRNG, the range of
G^{PRNG}
is limited by the size of the seed,
2^{l}
.
For any
y
outside the range of
G^{PRNG}
, it is clear that
G^{PRNG}(k)\neq y
for any
k
, and
\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{G^{\textup{IRNG}},t+t_{SG,PR}}
returns 0.
Therefore, for uniformly generated
y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\Pr[\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{G^{\textup{IRNG}},t+t_{SG,PR}}=1]\leq 2^{-n+l}.

Given the condition that
n>2l
and
\varepsilon_{\textup{guess}}>2^{-l}
, the IRNG term is smaller and the ability of
\mathcal{\hat{A}}
to distinguish between the PRNG and IRNG is then

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\vars{\hat A}^{G^{\PRNG},t+t_{SG,PR}} = 1] - \Pr[\vars{\hat A}^{G^{\TRNG},t+t_{SG,PR}} = 1]}>\varepsilon_{\textup{guess}}-2^{-n+l}.

According to the definition of the PRNG,
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\vars{\hat A}^{G^{\PRNG},t'}_D = 1] - \Pr[\vars{\hat A}^{G^{\TRNG},t'}_D = 1]}\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}
.
Since
t^{\prime}\geq t+t_{SG,PR}
, the bound holds in particular for algorithm
\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{G^{\textup{RNG}},t+t_{SG,PR}}
.
Therefore, when
\varepsilon_{\textup{guess}}-2^{-n+l}=\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}
, we encounter a contradiction and
\Pr[\hat{k}=k]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{guess}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}+2^{-n+l}
.
∎

Appendix B
Adapting AKE Framework to QAKE Security

We seek to incorporate standard QKD with authentication security in a QAKE security framework, which utilises the same terminology and tools as a classical AKE framework.
Here, we present only the main points of the classical AKE framework relevant in the context of QKD, and we refer the reader to Ref.
[
13
]
for more details on the classical AKE framework.

B.1
Security Model

We begin by first examining the security model of QAKE, based off the QKD security model and authentication requirements before linking the model to the classical AKE framework.
QAKE adopts a security model where the target key generation partner is between honest Alice and Bob, with an adversary (or eavesdropper) present in their communication channel that could seek to impersonate either party or steal the shared keys between Alice and Bob.
The protocol can be generalised to multiple parties, with any pre-shared secrets used for authentication being shared pairwise.
In general, the protocol involve input registers
S
representing the pre-shared secrets (e.g. authentication keys),
L
for any labels (e.g. to indicate the secret to use), with
E
labelling any side-information of the adversary.
After the protocol, the output would include
F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}
, which represent the respective parties’ choice: (1)
F=\phi
representing that it is not involved in the session, (2)
F=0
representing that it chose not to authenticate the other party, and (3)
F=1
representing that it chose to authenticate the other party.
It would also output keys
K_{\textup{A}}
and
K_{\textup{B}}
respectively if
F_{\textup{A}}=1
and
F_{\textup{B}}=1
(Note that this can in principle be decoupled – i.e. parties can choose to authenticate, but not generate keys, but we couple them for simplicity).
Otherwise, it would output
\perp
signalling that no secure keys are generated.

The classical AKE framework
[
13
]
is broader in its model, examining multiple parties, each with its own unique identity.
While classical AKE do not require all parties to provide information for other parties to authenticate its identity (to provide for instances where anonymous key generation is acceptable), we require mutual authentication for the QAKE protocol, i.e. setting the set of parties that need to provide authentication information to be the set of all parties.
During each session of the protocol, each party can set up local sessions (an instance that is present locally which runs the protocol steps and stores the outputs, e.g. in QKD, this could be Alice’s QKD box running Alice’s steps in a single protocol session and storing the outputs), labelled by
l=(i,j,k)
, with its identity
i
(e.g. Alice), intended peer
j
(e.g. Bob), and session number
k
.
The parties are assumed to hold public-private key pairs
(\text{pk}_{i},\text{sk}_{i})
for authentication purposes.
For QAKE, we generalize to allow for the use of symmetric keys by having parties hold “matching keys”
\text{mk}_{i}
instead, which can either contain
\text{sk}_{i}
for symmetric keys or
\text{pk}_{i}
for public keys
4
4

4

We note that this generalization has an impact on certain attacks. For instance, the classical AKE model captures the key-compromise impersonation attack, where an adversary that knows the secret key of Alice attempts to impersonate Bob and get Alice to accept the authentication. The leakage of
\text{sk}_{A}
alone would not cause this compromise, but with the use of symmetric keys, the protocol would no longer provide resistance to key-compromise impersonation. However, since this attack is not relevant to the QAKE of interest, where both Alice and Bob are assumed to be honest, we leave the account of such changes to future work.
.
We note also that the corresponding peers should hold the corresponding matching key
\text{mk}_{i}
that allows them to authenticate party
i
.

During the protocol, the adversary is present in the network between all parties, and is able to interfere with the communication, e.g. delay, redirect or alter messages.
In addition, the adversary is in general allowed to call on the session to reveal the session key and corrupt any party, which forces the reveal of the party’s secret keys
\text{sk}_{i}
.
We note that the security conditions (e.g. key secrecy) will impose conditions (e.g. party not corrupted) on the use of such attacks.
For QAKE, since Alice and Bob are assumed to be honest, the reveal of the session key, along with corrupting of Alice and Bob is disallowed.

During the protocol or at the end of the protocol, various outputs or information would be present with each party’s local sessions.
These are:

1.

Session acceptance: Indicates if a party has accepted or rejected the session. If a party is not involved, the session acceptance is maintained as
\phi
as initialized. This matches exactly the choice
F_{A}F_{B}
of the QAKE protocol.

2.

Keys: The session outputs keys, which is set to
\perp
unless the session is accepted – matching
K_{A}K_{B}
in QAKE.

3.

Session identifier: Initialized as
\perp
, and changed to other values when the session is accepted. This value is meant to identify local sessions that are partners – local sessions with same session identifier are partners (note that they have to both accept the session). This value is implicitly part of QAKE protocols, where Alice’s and Bob’s generated local sessions are partners. To explicitly include the identifier, one can simply modify the local session label
L
to an identifier, e.g. setting identifier as
(i,j,k)
if
i<j
and
(j,i,k)
otherwise, or include a random string that is sent and authenticated during the protocol. We take the first case for simplicity, and also note that for the QAKE protocol, these partnering local sessions are always between two different parties (Alice and Bob).

4.

Entity confirmation identifier: Indicates the local sessions that eventually would partner, i.e. similar to session identifier, but can be set earlier than protocol acceptance stage. For the QAKE protocol, we can set these values as
(i,j,k)
(or
(j,i,k)
) after the first message is sent or arrives at the party.
5
5

5

The choice of the confirmation identifier should coincide with when the identity of the peer (e.g. Bob) is made known to the the party (e.g. Alice). In classical AKE and QAKE, a pre-specified peer model is assumed, where each local session knows its intended partner’s identity. As such, these information should be exchange at the start of the protocol in practice.

5.

Key confirmation identifier: Indicates the local sessions that eventually would generate the same key, and this can be chosen similarly to the entity confirmation identifier for QAKE.

B.2
Security Conditions

With the input and output registers defined, let us formally introduce the security definitions.
We note that the classical AKE framework has multiple security conditions that are related and corresponds to desirable properties.
However, we choose only the most suitable, with many of the other properties being derivable from these conditions.

The first condition, robustness, is not part of the classical AKE framework, but is important nonetheless.
It necessitates that the protocol can succeed when the adversary is absent, and the protocol is not trivially rejecting all authentication to always guarantee security.

Definition 3

(
\varepsilon_{\textup{rob}}
-robustness)
.

A QAKE protocol is
\varepsilon_{\textup{rob}}
-robust if it passes with high probability in the absence of any adversary, i.e.

\Pr[F_{\textup{A}}=F_{\textup{B}}=1]\geq 1-\varepsilon_{\textup{rob}}.

The second condition, explicit entity authentication, addresses the security associated with mutual authentication between Alice and Bob.
It comes in two variants: full explicit entity authentication and almost-full explicit entity authentication.

We impose full explicit entity authentication on Alice, requiring that Bob will generate a partnering local session when Alice accepts.
More formally, the definition in Ref.
[
13
]
presents a predicate that states that for all local sessions
l
, when the local session accepts, there exists a partner local session
l^{\prime}
, and this local session belongs to the intended peer of the local session
6
6

6

We note there is a separate subtle implication that any partner to
l
must belong to the intended peer defined in explicit entity authentication. This means that the predicate does not preclude the possibility that two local sessions can be established on Bob that partners the same Alice. Since Alice and Bob are considered honest in QAKE, this can be simply prevented by having them to individually check that they do not have local sessions with the same session identifiers.
.
This includes a simplification noting that for QAKE, mutual authentication is expected, and both Alice and Bob are assumed to be honest.
As such, we have the implication that
F_{A}=1\implies F_{B}=1
, which the security condition requires to be true with high probability.

The condition on almost-full explicit entity authentication is imposed on Bob, who sends the final authentication message to Alice.
As such, he is unable to guarantee that at the end of the protocol, Alice would generate an accepting local session (necessary for session identifier generation and partnering) since the adversary can interfere with the final authentication message transmission.
More formally, the definition in Ref.
[
13
]
presents a predicate that states that for all local sessions
l
, when the local session accepts, there exists a local session
l^{\prime}
with the same entity confirmation identifier, which is a partner to
l
if
l^{\prime}
generates a session identifier, and this partner must be the peer of
l
.
This includes the same simplification that mutual authentication is expected, and both Alice and Bob are assumed to be honest.
As such, we have the implication that
F_{B}=1\implies F_{A}=0,1
, where Alice’s local session either rejects the session (same entity confirmation identifier, but not partners) or accepts the session (partners)
7
7

7

There is similar subtle implication as per full explicit entity authentication where the definition does not prevent multiple accepting local sessions to be established on Alice, though that can be addressed similarly since Alice is honest.
.
