The security of both conditions requires that the probability of the predicates being false is small, i.e. the probability of “bad events” is small.
We note that the security is defined relative to a computationally bounded adversary, with the small probability given as negligible relative to a security parameter.
We can generalise here (and in the following security definitions) to a general unbounded adversary, and define some small parameter
\varepsilon
to quantify the “negligible” probability.
As such, we define the explicit entity authentication as

Definition 4

((
\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,f}}
,
\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,af}}
)- explicit entity authentication)
.

A QAKE protocol has
\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,f}}
-full explicit entity authentication for Alice if the authentication fails with high probability when Bob is not accepting, i.e.

\Pr[F_{\textup{A}}=1,F_{\textup{B}}=0]+\Pr[F_{\textup{A}}=1,F_{\textup{B}}=\phi]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,f}},

and it has
\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,af}}
-almost full explicit entity authentication for Bob if the authentication fails with high probability when Alice is not involved in the session, i.e.

\Pr[F_{\textup{A}}=\phi,F_{\textup{B}}=1]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,af}}.

Note that we label
\varepsilon_{\textup{EA}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,f}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,af}}
for brevity.

The third security condition is termed Match-security, though in the context of the QAKE protocol, it reduces to the correctness condition.
This security condition mainly elevates the entity authentication security condition to provide key authentication as well -- where Alice and Bob can guarantee that the other party will generate the same key confirmation identifier and keys if both local sessions are accepting
8
8

8

There is a separate condition termed key-match soundness that is required to elevate explicit entity authentication to explicit key authentication. This condition is proven in classical AKE framework based on match secrecy and key secrecy conditions.
.
Formally, Ref.
[
13
]
defines the Match-predicate as four conditions: (1) partner local sessions generate the same key, (2) partner local sessions generate same key confirmation identifier, (3) at most two local sessions can generate the same session identifier, and (4) local sessions with the same key confirmation identifier would generate the same key if both local sessions are accepting.
The four conditions, in the context of QAKE, can be simplified:

1.

This condition can be summarised as when partnering local sessions are accepting (in QAKE this means that they are key generating as well), they will generate the same keys, i.e.
F_{A}=F_{B}=1\implies K_{A}=K_{B}
.

2.

Since we set key confirmation identifier and session identifier in the same way for QAKE, this condition is always true.

3.

This condition is always guaranteed in QAKE since the choice of session identifier as
(i,j,k)
(or
(j,i,k)
), along with honest Alice and Bob, and the fact that only one pair of local sessions have same set of parties
(i,j)
and same index
k
, means that only two local sessions can generate the same session identifier.

4.

Equivalent to condition 1 since key confirmation identifier and session identifier are set in the same way.

Condition 1 in the context of QAKE therefore matches the correctness condition of QKD, while the remaining are either always true or reduce to condition 1.
Therefore, we can define the Match-security as

Definition 5

(
\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}
-Match security)
.

A QAKE protocol has
\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}
-Match security if

\Pr[K_{\textup{A}}\neq K_{\textup{B}},F_{\textup{A}}=1,F_{\textup{B}}=1]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}.

The final condition is key secrecy (also termed BR-secrecy), which requires that any keys generated be secret from the adversary.
Ref.
[
13
]
defines the secrecy as a secrecy game, being the ability for an adversary to distinguish between an ideal key sampled from the key distribution and the actual key.
Certain secrecy freshness conditions are necessary for the game, such as the owner and peer being honest, the session key not directly revealed to the adversary (e.g. announced), and that there is expected mutual authentication, which are all part of the assumptions of QAKE, i.e. the freshness condition is satisfied.
More formally, the secrecy condition is defined by
[
13
]

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\vars{A}_D^{\reall}=1]-\Pr[\vars{A}_D^{\ideal}=1]}=\text{negl},

(9)

where a distinguisher
\mathcal{A}
makes the guess
D
on whether the keys are the actual key (
D=1
) or an ideal key
(D=0)
, guess
D
is set as
\perp
if the local session is not accepting
[
6
]
, and negl representing neglible in some security parameter.
Noting that when
D=\perp
, the ideal and real cases are identical, and with the upgrade from a computationally bounded adversary to an unbounded adversary with quantum capabilities, the secrecy condition can be redefined in terms of trace distance, representing the ability to distinguish between the ideal and real keys.
Note that we explicitly take only the cases of
(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})
being
(0,1)
and
(1,1)
, since the probability of
F_{\textup{B}}=1
when
F_{\textup{A}}=\phi
is small, and accounted for in entity authentication.
As such, we define key secrecy as

Definition 6

(
\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}
-key secrecy)
.

A QAKE protocol has
\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}
-key secrecy if

\Delta(\rho_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}K_{\textup{B}}LSE\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}},\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}LSE\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}.

Appendix C
Security Analysis of QAKE Protocol

C.1
Multi-Session to Single-Session Security Reduction

Here, we prove an tighter version of Thm.
1
using an inductive proof, noting that the final session need not keep the secrets private, where we define the set of ideal states for this final session as
\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}
.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose we have the conditions:

1.

The initial state is an ideal input state
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
.

2.

There exists an ideal output state which preserves any necessary secrets that is
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}
-close to the output state of a single session
\mathcal{P}_{i}
, i.e. for any
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
, there exists a state
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
such that

\Delta(\mathcal{P}_{i}(\sigma),\rho)\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}.

3.

The set of ideal output states is a subset of the set of ideal input states
\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
.

4.

There exists an ideal output state that is
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}
-close to the output state of a single session
\mathcal{P}_{i}
, i.e. for any
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
, there exists a state
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}
such that

\Delta(\mathcal{P}_{i}(\sigma),\rho)\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}.

Then, for all
i\in[1,n_{sess}]
, there exists
\rho_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}
such that

\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{i}(\rho_{0}),\rho_{i})\leq(i-1)\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}.

Proof.

The proof is by induction.
Let us consider a statement that for any
i\in[1,n_{sess}-1]
, there exists
\rho_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
such that

\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{i}(\rho_{0}),\rho_{i})\leq i\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}.

(10)

For
i=1
, the statement is trivially true from conditions 1 and 2.
Suppose the statement is true for index
j
, i.e. there exists
\rho_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
such that
\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{j}(\rho_{0}),\rho_{j})\leq j\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}
.
From condition 3, we know that
\rho_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
is a valid input state as well.
As such, by condition 2, there exists a state
\rho_{j+1}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
such that

\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho_{j}),\rho_{j+1})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}.

(11)

We can expand the LHS term for the
(j+1)
-th index, where there exists
\rho_{j+1}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
such that

\begin{split}&\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{j+1}(\rho_{0}),\rho_{j+1})\\
\leq&\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{j+1}(\rho_{0}),\mathcal{P}(\rho_{j}))+\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho_{j}),\rho_{j+1})\\
\leq&\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{j}(\rho_{0}),\rho_{j})+\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}\\
\leq&(j+1)\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}},\end{split}

(12)

where the first line applies the triangle inequality, and the second line notes that the CPTP map
\mathcal{P}
cannot increase trace distance.
Since the statement is true for
i=1
, and the statement being true for
j
implies that it is true for
j+1
, by mathematical induction, the statement is true for any
i\in[1,n_{sess}-1]
.

To prove the statement of Thm.
1
, we first note that since
\rho_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
, condition 4 states that there exists a state
\rho_{i+1}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}
such that

\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho_{i}),\rho_{i+1})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}.

(13)

As such, we can use the triangle inequality to arrive at

\begin{split}\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{i+1}(\rho_{0}),\rho_{i+1})\leq&\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{i+1}(\rho_{0}),\mathcal{P}(\rho_{m}))+\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho_{m}),\rho_{i+1})\\
\leq&\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{i}(\rho_{0}),\rho_{i})+\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}\\
\leq&i\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}\end{split}

(14)

for any
i\in[1,n_{sess}]
.
The same statement is true for
i=0
by condition 4, and thus we can arrive at the theorem.
∎

Condition 2 requires an additional condition for the set of ideal states, which we express as
\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
, to match
\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
, where the necessary secrets
S
are kept private.
Crucially, the main difference between
\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
and
\rho^{\textup{ideal}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}
is the form of the
LSE
subsystem.
In general,
\rho^{\textup{ideal}}
has
LSE
subsystems that can take any form, including one matching the actual output state while the form of
\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}
has
LSE
subsystems that are of a specific form (depending on the protocol requirement).
This forms the rationale behind the definition of the shared secrets privacy condition
9
9

9

In classical AKE
[
13
]
, this requirement of maintaining secret values from one session to the next is part of the parameter crypt.
Here, we make the requirement explicit with
\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}}
distance from full secrecy.
:

Definition 7

(
\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}}
-Shared Secrets Privacy)
.

A QAKE protocol has
\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}}
-shared secrets privacy if

\Delta\left(\sum_{(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}})\not\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{SP}}}p_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\rho^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}_{LSE},\sum_{(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}})\not\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{SP}}}p_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\sigma_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\right)\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}},

where
p_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}
is the probability of the output state recording
(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})=(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}})
,
\mathcal{S}_{\textup{SP}}=\{\phi 1,1\phi,10\}
and
\sigma_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
.

C.2
Single-Session Security Conditions

Recall that the set of ideal input state is defined by
\mathcal{S}_{in}=\{\rho^{\textup{in}}=\sum_{jj^{\prime}}p_{jj^{\prime}}\rho^{\textup{in},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE}\}
, where

\begin{split}\rho^{\textup{in},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE}=&\outerproduct{jj'}{jj'}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes\tau_{K_{2}RK^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j}\cdots K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,m}}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{K_{1}^{h}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j-1}E_{j-1}\rightarrow E}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{E}_{K_{1}^{h}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}E_{j^{\prime}}\rightarrow E_{j^{\prime}+1}}(\tau_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}\cdots K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j-1}}\otimes\rho_{E_{j^{\prime}}}),\end{split}

where
L=\alpha\alpha^{\prime}
are the labels and
S
refer to the secrets.
We note here an observation that when a partial trace of
K^{\textup{mask}}_{K_{1,j^{\prime}}}
is applied to the state, it effectively removes the corresponding channel
\mathcal{E}_{K_{1}^{h}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}E_{j^{\prime}}\rightarrow E_{j^{\prime}+1}}
, with appropriate relabelling of
E_{j^{\prime}}
as
E_{j^{\prime}+1}
.
10
10

10

More formally, the partial trace can be shifted to before the corresponding channel
\mathcal{E}_{K_{1}^{h}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}E_{j^{\prime}}\rightarrow E_{j^{\prime}+1}}
, which describes the adversary querying the oracle, followed by
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}
being discarded. Since
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}
acts as an one-time pad on the tag
T_{\textup{AV}}
, Thm.
4
implies that
T_{\textup{AV}}
would appear random to the adversary. As such, this is equivalent to the adversary randomly sampling
T_{\textup{AV}}
and using that value instead. As such, the process can be described by simply
\mathcal{E}_{E_{j^{\prime}}\rightarrow E_{j^{\prime}+1}}
, an internal update of the adversary’s internal state.

Similarly, recall that the set of ideal output state is given by
\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}=\{\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}\}
, where

\begin{split}&\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}=\outerproduct{\perp\perp}{\perp\perp}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\left[p_{0\phi}\outerproduct{0\phi}{0\phi}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sigma^{0\phi}_{LSE}+p_{\phi 0}\outerproduct{\phi 0}{\phi 0}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sigma^{\phi 0}_{LSE}+p_{00}\outerproduct{00}{00}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sigma^{00}_{LSE}\right]\\
&+p_{01}\outerproduct{01,\perp}{01,\perp}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}K_{\textup{A}}}\otimes\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sigma^{01}_{LSE}+p_{11}\outerproduct{11}{11}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\tilde{\tau}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sigma^{11}_{LSE},\end{split}

for
\sigma_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
.
We note that for
\mathcal{S}_{out}
, the ideal output state would be of a similar form,

\begin{split}\rho^{\textup{ideal}}=&\outerproduct{\perp\perp}{\perp\perp}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\left[p_{0\phi}\outerproduct{0\phi}{0\phi}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{0\phi}_{LSE}+p_{\phi 0}\outerproduct{\phi 0}{\phi 0}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{\phi 0}_{LSE}+p_{00}\outerproduct{00}{00}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{00}_{LSE}\right]\\
&+p_{01}\outerproduct{01,\perp}{01,\perp}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}K_{\textup{A}}}\otimes\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{01}_{LSE}+p_{11}\outerproduct{11}{11}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\tilde{\tau}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{11}_{LSE}.\end{split}

(15)

With the output state descriptions, and noting that
K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}
can be incorporated into
E
for the next session, we can observe that the output state in
\mathcal{S}_{\textup{out}}^{\prime}
is a linear combination of
\sigma_{LSE}^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}
, matching the general input state and thus satisfying condition 3 in Thm.
1
.
Condition 1 in the same theorem is trivially satisfied since all secrets begin as private with index
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}=1
.
Therefore, we can focus on analysing security conditions 2 and 4.
The security definition is given by the statement: for any
\sigma_{LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
, there exists
\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}\in\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}
and
\rho^{\textup{ideal}}\in\mathcal{S}_{out}
such that

\begin{gathered}\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\sigma_{LSE}),\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}_{F_{A}F_{B}K_{A}K_{B}LSE})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}\\
\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\sigma_{LSE}),\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{F_{A}F_{B}K_{A}K_{B}LSE})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}\end{gathered}

(16)

Here, we prove the generalized version of Thm.
2
, which allows the security analysis to be reduced to various security conditions.

Theorem 8
.

Consider a protocol
\mathcal{P}
with an ideal input state
\sigma_{LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{\textup{in}}
. Then, overall protocol security is satisfied when the intermediate session security parameter is

\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{EA}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}},

and the final session security parameter is

\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{EA}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}.

Proof.

For any input state
\sigma_{LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
, let
\rho_{F_{A}F_{B}K_{A}K_{B}LSE}=\mathcal{P}(\sigma_{LSE})
be the output state.
We seek find an ideal output state
\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{F_{A}F_{B}K_{A}K_{B}LSE}
that is close to the actual output state with probabilities
p_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}^{\textup{ideal}}
, where we note the additional superscript
ideal
to distinguish it from the actual output distribution
p_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}^{\textup{real}}
of
\rho_{F_{A}F_{B}K_{A}K_{B}LSE}
.
We define

p_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}^{\textup{ideal}}=\begin{cases}p_{\phi 0}^{\textup{real}}+p_{\phi 1}^{\textup{real}}&F_{\textup{A}}=\phi,F_{\textup{B}}=0\\
p_{0\phi}^{\textup{real}}+p_{1\phi}^{\textup{real}}&F_{\textup{A}}=0,F_{\textup{B}}=\phi\\
p_{00}^{\textup{real}}+p_{10}^{\textup{real}}&F_{\textup{A}}=0,F_{\textup{B}}=0\\
p_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}^{\textup{real}}&Otherwise\end{cases},

(17)

while the
\rho^{\textup{ideal},f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}_{LSE}=\rho_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}
of
\rho_{F_{A}F_{B}K_{A}K_{B}LSE}
for
(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\not\in\{\phi 1,1\phi,10\}
.
With this definition, we can split the trace distance for
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}
-security into components, first extracting the components where
F_{\textup{A}}
or
F_{\textup{B}}
is
\phi
,

\begin{split}\Delta(\rho,\rho^{\textup{ideal}})\leq&\Delta(\rho_{\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}},\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}})+\Delta(\rho_{\land F_{\textup{A}}=\phi},\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{\land F_{\textup{A}}=\phi})+\Delta(\rho_{\land F_{\textup{B}}=\phi},\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{\land F_{\textup{B}}=\phi})\\
&+\Delta(\rho_{\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{00,10\}},\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{00,10\}}),\end{split}

(18)

where the subsystem labels
K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}LSE
are dropped for brevity.

We now show that the latter three trace distances are bounded by the explicit entity authentication security condition.
Expanding the first term explicitly, we have

\begin{split}&\Delta(\rho_{\land F_{\textup{A}}=\phi},\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{\land F_{\textup{A}}=\phi})\\
\leq&p_{\phi 0}^{\textup{real}}\Delta(\rho_{|F_{\textup{A}}=\phi,F_{\textup{B}}=0},\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{|F_{\textup{A}}=\phi,F_{\textup{B}}=0})+p_{\phi 1}^{\textup{real}}\Delta(\rho_{|F_{\textup{A}}=\phi,F_{\textup{B}}=1},\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{|F_{\textup{A}}=\phi,F_{\textup{B}}=0})\\
\leq&\Delta(\outerproduct{\perp\perp\phi 0}{\perp\perp\phi 0}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho_{LSE}^{\phi 0},\outerproduct{\perp\perp\phi 0}{\perp\perp\phi 0}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho_{LSE}^{\phi 0})+p_{\phi 1}^{\textup{real}}\\
=&p_{\phi 1}^{\textup{real}},\end{split}

(19)

where we note that the trace distance in the third line is 0.
A similar argument can be made for the second term for
F_{\textup{B}}=\phi
, resulting in an upper bound of
p_{1\phi}^{\textup{real}}
.
The final term gives

\begin{split}&\Delta(\rho_{\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{00,10\}},\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{00,10\}})\\
\leq&\Delta(\outerproduct{\perp\perp 00}{\perp\perp 00}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho_{LSE}^{00},\outerproduct{\perp\perp 00}{\perp\perp 00}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho_{LSE}^{00})+p_{10}^{\textup{real}}\\
=&p_{10}^{\textup{real}}.\end{split}

(20)

As such, the three trace distances are upper bounded by

\Pr[F_{A}=1,F_{B}=0]+\Pr[F_{A}=1,F_{B}=\phi]+\Pr[F_{A}=\phi,F_{B}=1]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{EA}}.

(21)

This accounts for the explicit entity authentication security condition contribution to the security.

The second security condition to extract is match security.
To isolate its contribution, we need to first introduce an intermediate state
\tilde{\rho}
, for which the value of
K_{\textup{A}}
when
F_{\textup{A}}=1,F_{\textup{B}}=1
is replaced by
K_{\textup{B}}
, i.e.
\tilde{\rho}=\mathcal{M}_{K_{\textup{B}}\rightarrow K_{\textup{A}}}(\rho)
, where
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\textup{B}}\rightarrow K_{\textup{A}}}=\outerproduct{11}{11}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\left(\sum_{k}\outerproduct{kk}{kk}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}\circ\Tr_{K_{\textup{A}}}\right)
.
As such, we can apply the triangle inequality to expand

\begin{split}&\Delta(\rho_{\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}},\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}})\\
\leq&\Delta\left(\tilde{\rho}_{\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}},\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}}\right)+\Delta\left(\rho_{\land F_{\textup{A}}=F_{\textup{B}}=1},\tilde{\rho}_{\land F_{\textup{A}}=F_{\textup{B}}=1}\right),\end{split}

(22)

noting that
\rho_{\land F_{\textup{A}}=0,F_{\textup{B}}=1}
is identical to
\tilde{\rho}_{\land F_{\textup{A}}=0,F_{\textup{B}}=1}
since
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\textup{B}}\rightarrow K_{\textup{A}}}
does not alter the input state.
We now focus on the latter term, where we can expand the trace distance

\begin{split}&\Delta\left(\rho_{\land F_{\textup{A}}=F_{\textup{B}}=1},\tilde{\rho}_{\land F_{\textup{A}}=F_{\textup{B}}=1}\right)\\
=&p_{11}^{\textup{real}}\Delta\left(\sum_{k_{\textup{A}}k_{\textup{B}}}p_{k_{\textup{A}}k_{\textup{B}}|11}\outerproduct{\kA\kB}{\kA\kB}_{K_{A}K_{B}}\otimes\rho^{11,k_{\textup{A}}k_{\textup{B}}}_{LSE},\sum_{k_{\textup{A}}k_{\textup{B}}}p_{k_{\textup{A}}k_{\textup{B}}|11}\outerproduct{\kA\kA}{\kA\kA}_{K_{A}K_{B}}\otimes\rho^{11,k_{\textup{A}}k_{\textup{B}}}_{LSE}\right)\\
=&\frac{p_{11}^{real}}{2}\norm{\sum_{\kA\neq k_B}p_{\kA\kB|11}(\dyad{\kA\kB}_{K_AK_B}-\dyad{k_Bk_B}_{K_AK_B})\otimes\rho^{11,\kA\kB}_{LSE}}_{1}\\
\leq&\frac{p_{11}^{\textup{real}}}{2}\sum_{k_{\textup{A}}\neq k_{\textup{B}}}p_{k_{\textup{A}}k_{\textup{B}}|11}\norm{\dyad{\kA\kB}_{K_AK_B}-\dyad{\kB\kB}_{K_AK_B}}_{1}\norm{\rho^{11,\kA\kB}_{LSE}}_{1}\\
=&\Pr[K_{\textup{A}}\neq K_{\textup{B}},F_{\textup{A}}=1,F_{\textup{B}}=1]\\
\leq&\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}.\end{split}

(23)

This accounts for the contribution of match security.

The final term can be reduced to the key secrecy condition.
We first note that when
F_{\textup{A}}=F_{\textup{B}}=1
, whenever the key
K_{\textup{A}}
is generated, it will be identical to
K_{\textup{B}}
in both
\tilde{\rho}
and
\rho^{\textup{ideal}}
.
When
F_{\textup{A}}=0,F_{\textup{B}}=1
instead,
K_{\textup{A}}
is simply
\perp
.
As such, we can define a CPTP map to get a value of
K_{\textup{A}}
based on
F_{\textup{A}}
and
K_{\textup{B}}
.
Since CPTP maps cannot increase trace distance, we can remove the
K_{\textup{A}}
subsystem from the trace distance.
Expanding the final term, we get

\begin{split}&\Delta\left(\tilde{\rho}_{\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}},\rho^{\textup{ideal}}_{\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}}\right)\\
\leq&\Delta\left(\sum_{(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}})\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}p_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}^{\textup{real}}\outerproduct{\fA\fB}{\fA\fB}\otimes\rho_{K_{\textup{B}}LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}},\sum_{(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}})\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}p_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}^{\textup{real}}\outerproduct{\fA\fB}{\fA\fB}\otimes\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\right)\\
=&\Delta\left(\rho_{K_{\textup{B}}F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}LSE\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}},\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho_{K_{\textup{B}}F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}LSE\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}}\right)\\
\leq&\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}\end{split}

(24)

where
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
refers to the set
\{01,11\}
.
Combining the results, we have that

\Delta(\rho,\rho^{\textup{ideal}})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{EA}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}},

(25)

which we can use to define
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}
.

In the case of the intermediate sessions,
we can find an
\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}
with the same probability distribution
p_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}^{\textup{ideal}}
, but with
\sigma_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
.
We first note that if we choose an ideal state
\rho^{\textup{ideal},1}
that is the same as
\rho^{\textup{ideal}}
except that
\rho^{\textup{ideal},f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}}}_{LSE}=\frac{p^{\textup{real}}_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}}{p^{\textup{real}}_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}+p^{\textup{real}}_{f_{\textup{A}}^{\prime}f_{\textup{B}}^{\prime}}}\rho_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}+\frac{p^{\textup{real}}_{f_{\textup{A}}^{\prime}f_{\textup{B}}^{\prime}}}{p^{\textup{real}}_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}+p^{\textup{real}}_{f_{\textup{A}}^{\prime}f_{\textup{B}}^{\prime}}}\sigma_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}^{\prime}f_{\textup{B}}^{\prime}}
for
(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}},f_{\textup{A}}^{\prime},f_{\textup{B}}^{\prime})\in\{(\phi,0,\phi,1),(0,\phi,1,\phi),(0,0,1,0)\}
and any
\sigma_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}^{\prime}f_{\textup{B}}^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
, the analysis above remains unchanged and by the triangle inequality,

\Delta(\rho,\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{EA}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}+\Delta(\rho^{\textup{ideal},1},\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}).

(26)

The trace distance can be expanded as

\begin{split}&\Delta(\rho^{\textup{ideal},1},\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}})\\
\leq&\Delta\left(\sum_{(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}})\notin\mathcal{S}_{\textup{SP}}}p_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}^{\textup{real}}\sigma_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}},\sum_{(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}})\notin\mathcal{S}_{\textup{SP}}}p_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}^{\textup{real}}\sigma_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sigma_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\right)\\
\leq&\Delta\left(\sum_{(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}})\notin\mathcal{S}_{\textup{SP}}}p_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}^{\textup{real}}\rho_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}},\sum_{(f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}})\notin\mathcal{S}_{\textup{SP}}}p_{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}^{\textup{real}}\sigma_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}\right)\\
\leq&\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}},\end{split}

(27)

where
\sigma_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}
is the ideal form of the keys (either
\perp
or uniform) depending on
f_{\textup{A}},f_{\textup{B}}
, and
\sigma_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}}}
is identical in both
\rho^{\textup{ideal}}
and
\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}
.
The second inequality stems from reversing the CPTP map of selecting
K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}
from
F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}
, which is implicitly part of
E
since these values can be public in general.
Combining the results, we get that

\Delta(\rho,\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{EA}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}},

(28)

which we can use to define
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}
.
∎

C.3
Single-Session Security Analysis

Since the protocol
\mathcal{P}
is a linear map, we can break the output state into components based on the input state, i.e.
\rho^{\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}=\mathcal{P}(\rho^{\textup{in},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE})
, and the examine their trace distances separately,

\Delta(\rho^{\textup{out}},\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}})\leq\sum_{jj^{\prime}}p_{jj^{\prime}}\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho^{\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}),\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int},jj^{\prime}}).

(29)

As such, it is sufficient to analyse the single-session security for a single component,

\begin{gathered}\Delta(\rho^{\textup{out},jj^{\prime}},\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int},jj^{\prime}})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int},jj^{\prime}}\\
\Delta(\rho^{\textup{out},jj^{\prime}},\rho^{\textup{ideal},jj^{\prime}})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},jj^{\prime}},\end{gathered}

(30)

with overall security
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}=\max_{jj^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},jj^{\prime}}
and
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}=\max_{jj^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int},jj^{\prime}}
.
Furthermore, noting that there are restrictions of labels
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
when the ideal input state has label
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
, the ideal state for a single component can be further restricted to states of the form

\begin{split}\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int},jj^{\prime}}=&\outerproduct{\perp\perp}{\perp\perp}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\left[\outerproduct{0\phi}{0\phi}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sum_{\tilde{j}\geq j}p_{0\phi,\tilde{j}}\sigma^{0\phi,\tilde{j}+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}\right.+\outerproduct{\phi 0}{\phi 0}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sum_{\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}}p_{\phi 0,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\sigma^{\phi 0,j,\tilde{j}^{\prime}+1}_{LSE}\\
&\left.+\outerproduct{00}{00}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sum_{\tilde{j}\geq j,\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}}p_{00,\tilde{j}\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\sigma^{00,\tilde{j}+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime}+1}_{LSE}\right]+\outerproduct{01,\perp}{01,\perp}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}K_{\textup{A}}}\otimes\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sum_{\tilde{j}\geq\max\{j,j^{\prime}\}}p_{01,\tilde{j}}\sigma^{01,\tilde{j}+1,\tilde{j}}_{LSE}\\
&+\outerproduct{11}{11}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\tilde{\tau}_{K_{\textup{A}}K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\sum_{\tilde{j}\geq\max\{j,j^{\prime}\}}p_{11,\tilde{j}}\sigma^{11,\tilde{j}\tilde{j}}_{LSE},\end{split}

(31)

where
\sigma^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
,
\tilde{j}
and
\tilde{j}^{\prime}
indicates
\beta=\tilde{j}
and
\beta^{\prime}=\tilde{j}^{\prime}
, with the summation of
\tilde{j}
or
\tilde{j}^{\prime}
stemming from the ability for an adversary to force Alice and Bob to adopt a larger index
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
during the session by attacking the label agreement step.
As such, when we perform the security analysis, we focus on a single component ideal input state and ideal output states with the form above.

The single-session security can be summarised as Thm.
3
, which is restated below.
See
3

Figure 5:
Summary of Protocol 1.
M_{\textup{AV}}
labels the messages to be authenticated for which Alice hashes and
M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}
labels the corresponding messages hashed by Bob to authenticated these messages.

Sketch Proof.

To aid in the proof, we present a figure summarising the protocol in Fig.
5
.
Thm.
9

presents the proof of explicit entity authentication.
When Alice is not involved,
F_{\textup{A}}=\phi
, even with access to an oracle giving one message-tag pair for
T_{AV}
, the
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}
-almost 2-universal hash function ensures that
F_{\textup{B}}=0
since it is difficult for the adversary to find a second message-tag pair.
On the other hand, when
F_{\textup{B}}=\phi
or
F_{\textup{B}}=0
, no
T_{BV}
is generated by Bob and thus by
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}
-almost 2-universal property of
h_{2}
, Alice would not authenticate Bob,
F_{\textup{A}}=0
.

Thm.
10

bounds the match security parameter, which is identical to the correctness property of QKD.
This is guaranteed by the error verification using hash function
h_{2}
.

The authentication steps guarantee not only entity authentication, but also message authentication.
This ensures ideal features, such as: (1) labels match,
\beta=\beta^{\prime}
, (2) step ordering is obeyed, (3) messages match, (4) tags unaltered.
In Thm.
12
, we show that we can convert the authentication checks into ideal checks for these features without much penalty.
Furthermore, in Thm.
11
, we also reduce the parameter estimation to an ideal parameter check.
These modification reduces the analysis for key secrecy to a QKD analysis – where these ideal features occur, and we prove key secrecy in Thm.
13
.

Finally, shared secrets privacy accounts for the fact that in the worst case when keys are not generated
F_{\textup{B}}\neq 1
, either no secrets is leaked when authentication fails or at most
T_{AV}
is announced.
Thm.
17
and Thm.
18
shows that this leads to an ideal output state in
\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}
.
When keys are generated, i.e. parameter estimation passes, Thm.
19
shows that the min-entropy of
X_{P_{2}^{sift}}
ensures that authentication tags
T_{AV}
and
T_{BV}
leak minimal information on the authentication and masking key, while the strong extractor property for key generation ensures the privacy amplification key
R
is kept secret.
∎

We note that while the overall security parameters
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}
and
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}
can be determined by a sum of the respective security parameters, the bound may be tightened due to overlapping considerations for different security conditions.

C.3.1
Explicit Entity Authentication

Here, we consider the first security condition of explicit entity authentication, which consists of Alice’s full explicit entity authentication and Bob’s almost-full explicit entity authentication.
We note that the events of
F_{\textup{A}}=\phi
,
F_{\textup{B}}=\phi
and
F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}}\neq\phi
are mutually exclusive and the adversary is assumed to select one
attack at the start of the protocol.

Theorem 9
.

Consider the QAKE protocol
\mathcal{P}
, the full explicit entity authentication security parameter is

\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,f}}=\frac{\Pr[F_{\textup{B}}\neq 1]}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}}.

and the almost-full explicit entity authentication security parameter is

\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,af}}=\Pr[\FA=\phi](\epsMACa+2^{-\Psift_{\tol}[1+\hbin(f_{P_1})]+\log_2(\Psift_{\tol}+1)}).

Proof.

We begin with the case of full explicit entity authentication, which we recall bounds the probability of events
(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{10,1\phi\}
.
To obtain
F_{\textup{A}}=1
, Alice needs to at least successfully validate Bob by checking if
\tilde{T}_{\textup{BV}}=T_{\textup{BV},\textup{r}}
, i.e.
\Pr[F_{\textup{A}}=1|F_{\textup{B}}=\phi]\leq\Pr[\tilde{T}_{\textup{BV}}=T_{\textup{BV},\textup{r}}|F_{\textup{B}}=\phi]
.
Since
K_{2}
remains private from the adversary without Bob sending
T_{\textup{BV}}
, we can use the uniformity of the strong 2-universal hash function to have

\Pr[F_{\textup{A}}=1|F_{\textup{B}}=\phi]\leq\Pr[h_{2}(K_{2},M)=T_{\textup{BV},\textup{r}}]=\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}}

(32)

for any
T_{\textup{BV},\textup{r}}
.
A similar argument holds when conditioned on

F_{\textup{B}}=0
since a random string is sent in place of a valid tag.
As such, full explicit entity authentication security parameter is
\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,f}}=\frac{\Pr[F_{\textup{B}}\neq 1]}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}}
.

For the case of almost-full explicit entity authentication, Alice does not participate in the protocol,
F_{\textup{A}}=\phi
.
When
\alpha>\beta^{\prime}
, the adversary may have partial information on
(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta^{\prime}})
from a previous session via a tag generated with a chosen message input
M
.
As such, the two events that can lead to
F_{\textup{B}}=1
, or successful validation
\tilde{T}_{\textup{AV}}=T_{\textup{AV},\textup{r}}
, are either: (1) message
M
generating the tag matches the message
M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}
in the current session, or (2) guessing the correct
T_{\textup{AV},\textup{r}}
to send to Bob for a mismatch message,
M\neq M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}
.
As such, we can bound

\begin{split}\Pr[F_{\textup{B}}=1|F_{\textup{A}}=\phi]\leq&\Pr[h_{1}(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime})\oplus K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta^{\prime}}=T_{\textup{AV},\textup{r}}|h_{1}(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},M)\oplus K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta^{\prime}}=T_{\textup{AV}},M\neq M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}]+p_{\textup{guess}}(M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime})\\
\leq&\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+2^{-P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}[1+h_{\textup{bin}}(f_{P_{1}})]+\log_{2}(P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}+1)},\end{split}

(33)

where the
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}
-almost strong 2-universal hash function guarantees that the adversary cannot successfully guess a second message-tag pair with probability higher than
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}
and the guessing of
M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}
for
F_{\textup{A}}=\phi
reduces to the ability to guess
\theta_{P}^{\prime}
and
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}
.
As such, the almost-full explicit entity authentication security parameter is
\varepsilon_{\textup{EA,af}}=\Pr[\FA=\phi](\epsMACa+2^{-\Psift_{\tol}[1+\hbin(f_{P_1})]+\log_2(\Psift_{\tol}+1)})
.
∎

C.3.2
Match Security

The match security condition is similar to the correctness condition of QKD, and the security guarantee can be provided during Bob validation, where the string
X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},\textup{r}}
and
\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},\textup{r}}
are matched via a hash.

Theorem 10
.

Consider the QAKE protocol
\mathcal{P}
, the match security parameter is

\varepsilon_{\textup{MS}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}.

Proof.

Since the keys generated
K_{\textup{A}}=h_{\textup{PA}}(R,X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}})
and
K_{\textup{B}}=h_{\textup{PA}}(R,\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}})
with the same privacy amplification seed
R
would match when
X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}=\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}
, i.e.
\{X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}=\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}\}\implies\{K_{\textup{A}}=K_{\textup{B}}\}
, the converse gives

\Pr[K_{\textup{A}}\neq K_{\textup{B}},F_{\textup{A}}=F_{\textup{B}}=1]\leq\Pr[X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}\neq\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}},F_{\textup{A}}=F_{\textup{B}}=1].

(34)

The event
F_{\textup{A}}=1
contains the event

\tilde{T}_{\textup{BV}}=T_{\textup{BV},\textup{r}}
(part of
D_{\textup{BV}}=1
), allowing us to upper bound by

\Pr[h_{2}(K_{2},X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}})=T_{\textup{BV},\textup{r}}|X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}\neq\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}},h_{2}(K_{2},\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}})=T_{\textup{BV}}],

(35)

which describes the probability that an adversary can provide a valid tag to a message when given the tag to a different message.
Since
K_{2}
is private from the adversary, by the
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}
-almost strong 2-universal property of the hash,

\Pr[K_{\textup{A}}\neq K_{\textup{B}},F_{\textup{A}}=F_{\textup{B}}=1]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}
.
∎

C.3.3
Idealised Parameter Estimation

To simplify analysis, we introduced an “idealised” version of parameter estimation related to the decoy state analysis.
We define an idealised version of the the check
D_{\textup{PE}}
as

\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}=\begin{cases}1&\begin{subarray}{c}N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}},1}\geq N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}},\,N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}},1}\geq N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},1}^{\textup{tol}},\,D_{\textup{PE}}=1\\
\frac{wt(X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}},1}\oplus X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},\textup{r}}^{\prime}[\{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}},1\}])}{N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}},1}}\leq e_{\textup{bit},1,\textup{tol}}\end{subarray}\\
0&otherwise\end{cases},

(36)

where
X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},\textup{r}}^{\prime}[\{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}},1\}]
refers to the bit values of the bitstring
X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},\textup{r}}^{\prime}
at indices where
i\in P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}},
and a single photon is sent, and
wt(S)
gives the Hamming weight of a string
S
, i.e. the final condition means the actual single photon events in
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}}
gives a bit error value upper bounded by
e_{\textup{bit},1,\textup{tol}}
.
Here, we added the three desired conditions (actual single-photon detection/error fall within the bounds).

Theorem 11
.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
be the protocol when
D_{PE}
is replaced with
\tilde{D}_{PE}
. Then, for any
\sigma_{LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
,

\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\sigma_{LSE}),\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{LSE}))\leq 2\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}},

where
\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}
accounts for errors associated with decoy state analysis.

Proof.

The replacement of
D_{\textup{PE}}
with
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}
would result in a difference only when
D_{\textup{PE}}=1
but
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}=0
.
The probability of this event is tied to decoy state analysis.
From decoy state analysis
[
24
]
, the single-photon quantities of interest can always be bounded by

\displaystyle N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},1}\geq

\displaystyle\frac{p_{1}\mu_{0}}{(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})(\mu_{0}-\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})}\times\left[\frac{e^{\mu_{1}}}{p_{\mu_{1}}}\left(1-\frac{\mu_{2}(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})}{\mu_{0}^{2}}\right)\mathbb{E}[N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},\mu_{1}}]-\frac{(\mu_{1}^{2}-\mu_{2}^{2})e^{\mu_{0}}}{\mu_{0}^{2}p_{\mu_{0}}}\mathbb{E}[N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},\mu_{0}}]\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{e^{\mu_{2}}}{p_{\mu_{2}}}\left(1-\frac{\mu_{1}(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})}{\mu_{0}^{2}}\right)\mathbb{E}[N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},\mu_{2}}]\right]

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},1}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{p_{1}}{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}\left(\frac{e^{\mu_{1}}}{p_{\mu_{1}}}\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathcal{E},P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},\mu_{1}}]-\frac{e^{\mu_{2}}}{p_{\mu_{2}}}\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathcal{E},P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},\mu_{2}}]\right),

where
\mathcal{E}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},1}
is the number of error bits in the single photon events in set
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}
, and
p_{1}
is the probability of single-photon signals sent by the source.
The estimated quantities
\hat{N}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},1}^{\textup{LB}}
and
\hat{e}_{\textup{bit},P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},1}^{\textup{UB}}
can be computed similarly from the above bounds, but utilises appropriate bounds on the expectation values
\mathbb{E}[N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},\mu_{j}}]
and
\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathcal{E},P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},\mu_{j}}]
derived from the values observed during the protocol.
For instance,
\hat{N}_{P_{i},1}^{\textup{LB}}
utilises the estimated lower bound
\mathbb{E}[N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},\mu_{1}}^{\textup{LB}}]
instead of
\mathbb{E}[N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},\mu_{1}}]
(which it does not have access to), estimated using concentration bounds (we use Kato’s inequality
[
19
,
11
]
as the concentration bound) from the observed
N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},\mu_{1}}
value.
The probability of the estimated lower bound
\mathbb{E}[N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},\mu_{1}}^{\textup{LB}}]
exceeding the true expectation value
\mathbb{E}[N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},\mu_{1}}]
is quantified by
\varepsilon_{N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},\mu_{1}}}
.
As such, when these concentration bounds are respected, we have that
N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},1}\geq\hat{N}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},1}^{\textup{LB}}
, which implies that the probability that
N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},1}<N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},1}^{\textup{tol}}
while
\hat{N}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},1}^{\textup{LB}}\geq N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{i},1}^{\textup{tol}}
is bounded by the events where the concentration bounds are violated.
As such, the probability of
D_{\textup{PE}}=1
but
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}=0
is the sum of all such concentration bound violation events and is labelled
\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}
.
Therefore, the trace distance on the output states gains a
2\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}
penalty when we replace the decision
D_{\textup{PE}}
with its idealised version
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}
.

∎

Applying Thm.
11
replaces the parameter check with an ideal check of the single qubit parameters.
As such, the later analysis also applies to a QAKE protocol where single-qubit BB84 states are prepared and single qubit bit error and number of rounds can be observed directly.

C.3.4
Idealised Authentication

The validation steps in the protocol also serve as message authentication steps.
To further simplify the analysis, we introduce an “idealised” version of message authentication checks, replacing the authentication decisions
F_{\textup{B}}
and
D_{\textup{BV}}
with idealised versions.
The purposes of authentication checks are twofold; They serve to ensure that the message transmitted between Alice and Bob match, and that the order of the communication steps are obeyed.
As such, when we define the idealised authentication checks, these are the conditions that we impose.
Crucially, there are several events that we desire in an ideal check which are provided with high probability:

1.

Matching label agreement,
\beta=\beta^{\prime}
, is desirable to ensure the label agreement step is obeyed. Its security is provided by the fact that the masking key
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}
choice is label-dependent, and mismatching masking key would lead to failed authentication.

2.

Parameter estimation passing,
D_{\textup{PE}}=1
, is meant to indicate to Bob that parameter estimation has passed. Its security is guaranteed by Alice’s decision to not send a valid
T_{\textup{AV}}
when
D_{\textup{PE}}=0
.

3.

Alice’s tag generation occurs before Alice’s tag is validated, which indicates the ordering of Alice’s Validation step is obeyed, i.e. the event
\Omega_{5\rightarrow 6}
, where
\Omega_{i\rightarrow j}
labels that the
i
-th step occur before the
j
-th step. Note here that the steps refers a set of action by either Alice or Bob, receiving an input and providing and output from the communication channel. For QAKE, there are a total of seven steps.

4.

Matching message,
M_{\textup{AV}}=M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}
, where
M_{\textup{AV}}=(X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}},P_{\textup{r}},\theta_{P,\textup{r}}^{\prime},P^{\textup{sift}},X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},\textup{r}}^{\prime},P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}},P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}},syn)
and
M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}=(\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}},P,\theta_{P}^{\prime},P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{r}},X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}}^{\prime},P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},syn_{\textup{r}})
, which also contains the error verification check (
X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}=\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}})
.

5.

Tags unaltered,
T_{\textup{AV}}=T_{\textup{AV},\textup{r}}
.

6.

Protocol steps before Alice’s validation are obeyed, namely the event
\{\Omega_{i\rightarrow i+1}\}_{i\in[2,4]}
. We note that the first step, Alice’s state preparation, would WLOG occur before step 2 (Bob responding with
P
and basis
\theta_{P}
)
11
11

11

If the state preparation occurs after step 2, the state is always equivalent to one where the state preparation occurs before step 2 but with the adversary not interacting with the quantum state sent by Alice prior to step 2. This is because the state preparation does not require any input from the channel.
.

As such, we label a replacement decision
\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}
, which is made at the same time as
F_{\textup{B}}
, but with separate checks
12
12

12

The checks can be considered to be performed by a hypothetical third-party, though such a party need not be physically present in the protocol.
.
For validation of Alice, this replacement made is

\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=\begin{cases}1&\begin{subarray}{c}\beta=\beta^{\prime},\,D_{\textup{PE}}=1,\,T_{\textup{AV}}=T_{\textup{AV},\textup{r}},\\
M_{\textup{AV}}=M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime},\Omega_{2\rightarrow 3\rightarrow 4\rightarrow 5\rightarrow 6}\end{subarray}\\
0&otherwise\end{cases},

(37)

where
\Omega_{2\rightarrow 3\rightarrow 4\rightarrow 5\rightarrow 6}
is the event where the step ordering is
2\rightarrow 3\rightarrow 4\rightarrow 5\rightarrow 6
.
The list of steps are:

1.

Alice’s state preparation and sending of quantum state.

2.

Bob’s measurement and reply of
P
and
\theta_{P}^{\prime}
.

3.

Alice’s sifting and response
P^{\textup{sift}}
.

4.

Bob’s choice of test round and announcement of
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}
,
P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}
and
X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}}^{\prime}
.

5.

Alice’s parameter estimation and validation tag generation, sending syndrome
syn
and tag
T_{\textup{AV}}
to Bob.

6.

Bob performs Alice’s validation, and responds with his own validation tag
T_{\textup{BV}}
.

7.

Alice validates Bob’s tag.

The replacement of
D_{\textup{BV}}
is simpler, with

\tilde{D}_{\textup{BV}}=\begin{cases}1&\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1,\,T_{\textup{BV}}=T_{\textup{BV},\textup{r}},\,\Omega_{6\rightarrow 7}\\
0&otherwise\end{cases},

(38)

where
\Omega_{6\rightarrow 7}
indicates that Bob sends
T_{\textup{BV}}
before Alice is requested to verify, and
\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}:=\tilde{D}_{\textup{BV}}\land D_{\textup{PE}}
.

Theorem 12
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the protocol and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be the idealised protocol (with
D_{\textup{PE}}
,
F_{\textup{B}}
,
D_{\textup{BV}}
replaced with
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}
,
\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}
and
\tilde{D}_{\textup{BV}}
). Then, for any
\sigma_{LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
,

\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\sigma_{LSE}),\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\sigma_{LSE}))\leq 2(\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{SO}})+2\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}+2\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}},

where
\varepsilon_{\textup{SO}}=2^{-P^{\textup{sift}}_{tol}}+2^{-N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,1}}^{\textup{tol}}}+2^{-P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}h_{\textup{bin}}(f_{P_{1}})+\log_{2}(P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}+1)}
.

Proof.

From Thm.
11
, the switch to
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}
introduces a
2\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}
penalty.
As such, we can consider only the changes in
F_{\textup{B}}
and
D_{\textup{BV}}
, and combine the results using triangle inequality at the end.

For the switch
from
F_{\textup{B}}
to
\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}
for scenarios where
(K_{1}^{h},K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta^{\prime}})
is private (where
\beta^{\prime}\geq\alpha
),
the replacement results in a penalty of
2\Pr[F_{\textup{B}}=1,\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=0]
.
This probability can be computed,

\begin{split}&\Pr[F_{\textup{B}}=1,\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=0]\\
\leq&\Pr[F_{\textup{B}}=1,(\Omega^{c}_{5\rightarrow 6}\lor D_{\textup{PE}}=0)]+\Pr[F_{\textup{B}},D_{\textup{PE}}=1,\Omega_{5\rightarrow 6},\beta\neq\beta^{\prime}]\\
&+\Pr[F_{\textup{B}},D_{\textup{PE}}=1,\Omega_{5\rightarrow 6},\beta=\beta^{\prime},M_{\textup{AV}}\neq M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}]\\
&+\Pr[F_{\textup{B}},D_{\textup{PE}}=1,\Omega_{5\rightarrow 6},\beta=\beta^{\prime},M_{\textup{AV}}=M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime},T_{\textup{AV}}\neq T_{\textup{AV},r}]\\
&+\Pr[F_{\textup{B}},D_{\textup{PE}}=1,\Omega_{5\rightarrow 6},\beta=\beta^{\prime},M_{\textup{AV}}=M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime},T_{\textup{AV}}=T_{\textup{AV},r},\Omega^{c}_{1\rightarrow 2\rightarrow 3\rightarrow 4}]\\
\leq&\frac{\Pr[\Omega^{c}_{5\rightarrow 6}\lor D_{\textup{PE}}=0]}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}}+\frac{\Pr[D_{\textup{PE}}=1,\Omega_{5\rightarrow 6},\beta\neq\beta^{\prime}]}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}}+\Pr[D_{\textup{PE}}=1,\Omega_{5\rightarrow 6},\beta=\beta^{\prime},M_{\textup{AV}}\neq M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}]\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}\\
&+\Pr[M_{\textup{AV}}=M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime},\Omega^{c}_{2\rightarrow 3}]+\Pr[M_{\textup{AV}}=M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime},\Omega_{2\rightarrow 3},\Omega^{c}_{3\rightarrow 4}]+\Pr[M_{\textup{AV}}=M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime},\Omega_{2\rightarrow 3\rightarrow 4},\Omega^{c}_{4\rightarrow 5}]\\
\leq&\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+p_{\textup{guess}}(\theta_{P}^{\prime})+p_{\textup{guess}}(\theta_{P})+p_{\textup{guess}}(P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}),\end{split}

(39)

where
p_{\textup{guess}}(Z)
is the probability of the adversary guessing the parameter
Z
successfully before
Z
is announced.
The first inequality splits the “bad events” into different cases.
The second inequality uses the fact that when
\Omega_{5\rightarrow 6}^{c}
or
D_{\textup{PE}}=0
, no valid tag is generated before Bob checks Alice’s authentication tag.
Therefore, by the property of the strong 2-universal hash function, the probability of
F_{\textup{B}}=1
is no better than a random guess of the tag.
When
\beta\neq\beta^{\prime}
, since authentication key
K_{1,\beta}
is random and uncorrelated to
K_{1,\beta^{\prime}}
, the adversary can do no better than make a random guess of the tag, which results in a
\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}}
penalty.
As for message mismatch, the property of strong 2-universal hash function ensures that the probability of generating a correct tag for
M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}
is bounded by
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}
.
The final inequality combines the first three terms to upper bound it by
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}
, while events
\Omega_{i\rightarrow j}^{c}
with matching messages indicate that the adversary made a correct guess of the message sent in step
i
, which we label
p_{\textup{guess}}(Z)
for message
Z
.
For step 2 (Bob response after quantum state measurement), the adversary has to guess
\theta_{P}^{\prime}
, while for step 3, this quantity is
\theta_{P}
(equivalent to guessing
P^{\textup{sift}}
with knowledge of
\theta_{P}^{\prime}
from step 2), and in step 4, the quantity is the choice of test round
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}
from
P^{\textup{sift}}
.

We can bound the guessing probability explicitly.
The guessing probability of
\theta_{P}^{\prime}
is
2^{-\absolutevalue{P}}\leq 2^{-P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}}
since the basis are randomly chosen, and
\absolutevalue{P}\leq P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}
.
The probability of guessing
\theta_{P}
can be bounded by the probability of guessing Alice’s basis choice for the
N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,1}}^{\textup{tol}}
single-photon rounds, where the quantum state cannot provide any information on the basis.
Finally, the probability of guessing the test round choice is
[
10
]

\begin{pmatrix}\absolutevalue{\Psift}\\
f_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}}\absolutevalue{\Psift}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\leq 2^{-\absolutevalue{\Psift}h_{\textup{bin}}(f_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}})+\log_{2}(\absolutevalue{\Psift}+1)}\leq 2^{-P^{\textup{sift}}_{tol}h_{\textup{bin}}(f_{P_{1}})+\log_{2}(P^{\textup{sift}}_{tol}+1)},

(40)

where we drop the ceiling function (
\lceil\rceil
) for simplicity and note that the second term is decreasing in
\absolutevalue{P}
for any chosen number of test rounds (at least 1 test and key round).
The overall penalty to satisfy step order can be summarised as
\varepsilon_{\textup{SO}}=2^{-P^{\textup{sift}}_{tol}}+2^{-N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,1}}^{\textup{tol}}}+2^{-P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}h_{\textup{bin}}(f_{P_{1}})+\log_{2}(P^{\textup{sift}}_{\textup{tol}}+1)}
.

To make the same switch for cases when
(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta^{\prime}})
is accessible via a single oracle access (
\mathcal{E}_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1},K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta^{\prime}}E_{i}\rightarrow E_{i+1}}
), we first note that such cases require
\beta^{\prime}<\alpha
and by extension
\beta^{\prime}<\beta
, i.e.
\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}
should always return 0 since
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
are mismatched.
We first note that
T_{\textup{AV}}
generated with private
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}
provides no advantage to the adversary in passing
F_{\textup{B}}
.
As such, we can follow the argument in Thm.
9
to show that the probability of
F_{\textup{B}}=1
is upper bounded by
the probability of
F_{\textup{B}}=1
when
F_{\textup{A}}=\phi
.
Combining both results, we can thus replace
F_{\textup{B}}
by
\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}
in general in this protocol with a penalty of
2(\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{SO}})
.

By similar arguments, the replacement of
D_{\textup{BV}}
to
\tilde{D}_{\textup{BV}}
, which involves mainly Bob’s validation step, yields an error of up to
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}
.
As such, these changes result in a
penalty to the trace distance of
2\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}
.
∎

C.3.5
Key Secrecy

Key secrecy analyses the two specific cases where Bob generates a key,
(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}
.
Intuitively, since the protocol is similar to decoy-state BB84 with modifications only in its authentication steps, the key secrecy security condition relies heavily on the secrecy condition of BB84.
We can summarise the result as a theorem,

Theorem 13
.

Consider the QAKE protocol
\mathcal{P}
. Then, the key secrecy security parameter is

\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}^{\prime}+2\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{SO}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}\right)

where

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\textup{KS}}^{\prime}=2\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1}}+\frac{1}{2}\times 2^{-\frac{1}{2}[N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}-N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})-2-\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}-\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}-l_{K_{\textup{B}}}]}

is the key secrecy of the idealised protocol
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We begin by performing a swap to an “idealised” version of the protocol
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
by replacing
F_{\textup{B}}
,
D_{\textup{BV}}
and
D_{\textup{PE}}
.
By Thm.
12
, this gives

\Delta(\rho^{\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}K_{\textup{B}}LSE\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}},\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}LSE\land(F_{\textup{A}},F_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}})\\
\leq\Delta(\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}K_{\textup{B}}LSE\land(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}},\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}LSE\land(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}})\\
+2\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{SO}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}\right),

(41)

where
\rho^{\prime}
label the output state when an idealised version of the protocol is utilised.
We first combine 01 and 11 cases into a single one by arguing that the trace distance is independent of the final communication step using Thm.
14
.
This is followed by analysing the trace distance shown in Thm.
15
.
Combining the results, we obtain the security parameter stated in the theorem.
∎

To simplify the analysis, we combine the cases of
(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})
being 01 and 11 into a single condition on
\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1
since only the key
K_{\textup{B}}
is of interest.

Theorem 14
.

Consider the QAKE protocol
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. Then, we have

\Delta(\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}K_{\textup{B}}LSE\land(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}},\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}LSE\land(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}})\leq\Delta(\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{K_{\textup{B}}L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1},\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1}),

where the states on RHS has subsystems
L^{\prime}S^{\prime}
, indicating the labels and secrets correspond to that when the final step of Alice has been removed.

Proof.

We first note that since
\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1
, we have
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}=1
and the decision of
\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}
depends solely on
\tilde{D}_{\textup{BV}}
generated in the final step.
Moreover, since
T_{\textup{BV}}\neq\perp
, the
\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}
condition depends solely on
T_{\textup{BV}}=T_{\textup{BV},\textup{r}}
, i.e. whether the adversary altered
T_{\textup{BV}}
transmission from Bob to Alice.
As such, the final step can be re-expressed as a
CPTP map that maps
T_{\textup{BV}}
and
T_{\textup{BV},\textup{r}}
(part of
E
) to
\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}
followed by generating secret
S
from
S^{\prime}
.
Since CPTP maps cannot increase trace distance, we can reverse the map (i.e. reversing Alice’s decision step and subsequent index update, which WLOG is the final step of the protocol), yielding

\Delta(\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{F_{\textup{A}}F_{\textup{B}}K_{\textup{B}}LSE\land(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}},\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}LSE\land(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}})\leq\Delta(\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{K_{\textup{B}}L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1},\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1}),

(42)

where the
L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E
indicates that the state of the subsystem before Alice’s final protocol step.
This aligns with our understanding that
\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}
outcome has no impact on the privacy of
K_{\textup{B}}
since it does not reveal additional information.
∎

With the simplification, we proceed to prove the key secrecy security parameter for the QAKE protocol with idealised parameter estimation and authentication checks,

Theorem 15
.

Consider the QAKE protocol
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. Then, we have that

\Delta(\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{K_{\textup{B}}L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1},\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1})\leq 2\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1}}+\frac{1}{2}\times 2^{-\frac{1}{2}[N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}-N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})-2-\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}-\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}-l_{K_{\textup{B}}}]},

where
e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime}
is the error tolerance inclusive of the correction due to Serfling bound.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
be the event where parameter estimation checks
N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},1}\geq N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},1}^{\textup{tol}}
,
N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},1}\geq N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}
,
\frac{wt(X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},1}\oplus X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,1}}^{\prime})}{N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},1}}\leq e_{\textup{bit},1,\textup{tol}}
are successful, for which the actual
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}
and
P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}
sets are utilised instead of the received
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1,\textup{r}}
and
P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}
in the
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}=1
condition.
Due to the matching message condition in idealised authentication check, when
\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1
,
\rho_{\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1\land\Omega_{\textup{PE}}}=\rho_{\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}
.
Noting that
h_{\textup{PA}}
is 2-universal, we bound the trace distance using the quantum leftover hash lemma
[
38
,
41
]
,

\begin{split}\Delta(\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{K_{\textup{B}}L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E^{\prime\prime}\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1},\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1})\leq&\Delta(\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{K_{\textup{B}}L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}},\tau_{K_{\textup{B}}}\otimes\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}})\\
\leq&\frac{1}{2}\left\{4\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}+2^{-\frac{1}{2}\left[H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}}(\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}|L^{\prime}S^{\prime\prime}E)_{\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{\hat{X}_{P_{2,\textup{r}}}L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}-l_{K_{\textup{B}}}\right]}\right\},\end{split}

(43)

where
S^{\prime\prime}=S^{\prime}\setminus R
.
Focusing only on the terms relevant to the current session, we can express the smooth min-entropy as

H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}}(\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}|L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E)_{\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}L^{\prime}S^{\prime}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}=H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}}(\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K_{2}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}E)_{\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K_{2}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}},

(44)

where we note the labels are implicitly contained in
E
.
Before simplifying the min-entropy, we first list the important conditions required for
\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1
(inclusive of
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}=1
).
These are

1.

\beta=\beta^{\prime}
: The index chosen for the protocol session matches.

2.

M_{\textup{AV}}=M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}
: Classical messages exchanged between Alice and Bob before Alice Validation step matches.

3.

\Omega_{2\rightarrow 3\rightarrow 4\rightarrow 5\rightarrow 6}
: Protocol step order is respected from steps 2 to 6.

4.

\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
: Standard parameter estimation checks.

With the conditions, we can simplify the smooth min-entropy term similarly to QKD, using Thm. 10 of Ref.
[
38
]
to remove
\land\Omega
conditions when necessary,

\begin{split}&H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}}(\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K_{2}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}E)_{\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}}K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K_{2}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}E\land\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=1\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}\\
=&H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}}(X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K_{2}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}T_{\textup{BV}}{\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}}E_{6})_{\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K_{2}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}T_{\textup{BV}}{\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}}E_{6}\land\beta=\beta^{\prime}\land M_{\textup{AV}}=M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}\land\Omega_{2\rightarrow 3\rightarrow 4\rightarrow 5\rightarrow 6}\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}\\
\geq&H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}}(X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}T_{\textup{AV}}\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}synE_{5})_{\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}T_{\textup{AV}}\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}synE_{5}\land\beta=\beta^{\prime}\land M_{\textup{AV}}=M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}\land\Omega_{2\rightarrow 3\rightarrow 4\rightarrow 5}\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}-1-\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}\\
\geq&H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}}(X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}|P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}}^{\prime}E_{4})_{\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}}^{\prime}E_{4}\land M_{\textup{AV}}=M_{\textup{AV}}^{\prime}\land\Omega_{2\rightarrow 3\rightarrow 4}\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}-2-\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}-\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}\\
\geq&H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}}(X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}|P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}}^{\prime}\theta_{P}\theta_{P}^{\prime}PE_{1})_{\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}}^{\prime}\theta_{P}\theta_{P}^{\prime}PE_{1}\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}-2-\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}-\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}\\
\end{split}

(45)

The first equality uses the fact that Bob’s corrected bit string matches Alice’s and that
P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}=P^{\textup{sift}}_{2,\textup{r}}
, and expands
E=E_{5}T_{\textup{BV}}\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}
, corresponding to output of the sixth step.
The second inequality notes the removal of the classical
T_{\textup{BV}}
and
\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}
using the chain rule of smooth min-entropy for classical systems
[
42
]
, followed by removing
K_{2}
, noting that it is independent of the rest of the state without
T_{\textup{BV}}
.
Furthermore, the CPTP map describing Eve taking the outputs of step 5 and preparing inputs of step 6 is reversed.
The third inequality removes the classical
T_{\textup{AV}}
,
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}
and
syn
using the chain rule of smooth min-entropy, followed by removal of
K_{1}^{h}
and
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}
since they are independent of the state after removal of
T_{\textup{AV}}
.
Furthermore, the CPTP map describing Eve taking the outputs of step 4 and preparing the inputs of step 5 is reversed.
The final inequality reverses the remaining CPTP map to arrive at the state after step 1.

The quantum state
\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}}^{\prime}\theta_{P}\theta_{P}^{\prime}PE_{1}\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}
can be viewed as being generated from a standard decoy-state BB84.
As such, assuming ideal state preparation and fair sampling, we can bound the min-entropy by
[
24
,
38
]
,

H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}}(X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}|P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}}^{\prime}\theta_{P}\theta_{P}^{\prime}PE_{1})_{\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}}P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}X_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}}^{\prime}\theta_{P}\theta_{P}^{\prime}PE_{1}\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}\geq N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})],

(46)

where
\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}\geq\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1}}
,
N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}
is a lower bound on the number of single-photon events in set
P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}
, with phase error tolerance

e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime}=e_{\textup{bit},1,\textup{tol}}+g(N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{1},1}^{\textup{tol}},N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}},\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1}),

(47)

and
g(k,n,\varepsilon)=\sqrt{\frac{(n+k)(k+1)}{2nk^{2}}\ln\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}
stemming from the modified Serfling bound estimating the single-photon bit error rate in the key generation rounds of
P^{\textup{sift}}_{2}
from that of
P^{\textup{sift}}_{1}

[
39
,
12
]
.
∎

C.3.6
Shared Secrets Privacy

The final security parameter to examine is the shared secrets privacy condition, which can be summarised as

Theorem 16
.

Consider the QAKE protocol
\mathcal{P}
. Then, the shared secrets privacy security condition satisfies

\varepsilon_{\textup{SP}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{SP},01,11}^{\prime}+2\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{SO}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}\right)

where

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\textup{SP},01,11}^{\prime}=4\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1}}+2(\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{3})+\varepsilon_{\textup{SP},\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{SP},\textup{MAC},2},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\textup{SP},\textup{MAC},1}=\sqrt{(\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}-1)+2^{\log_{2}\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{3}}+1\right)+\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}+2+\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}-N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})]}}

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\textup{SP},\textup{MAC},2}=\sqrt{(\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}-1)+2^{\log_{2}\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{2}}+1\right)+\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}+2+\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}-N_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})]}}

is the shared secrets privacy parameter for idealised protocol
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
for cases where
F_{\textup{B}}=1
.

Proof.

We begin by performing the swap to an “idealised” version of the protocol
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
following Thm.
12
,
with replacement of
F_{\textup{B}}
,
D_{\textup{BV}}
and
D_{\textup{PE}}
, which incurs a penalty of
2(\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},2}+\varepsilon_{\textup{SO}})
.
We then analyse the components of the shared secrets privacy trace distance separately, based on the
(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})
values.
For the
(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})=(\phi,0)
case, the switch to “idealised” checks means none of the secrets are utilised and thus are not leaked, as presented in Thm.
17
.
For the case of
(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})\in\{0\phi,00\}
, which is analysed together in Thm.
18
, the main leakage concern is that from sending
T_{\textup{AV}}
.
There, we provide a clear reason for the necessity of the channels
\mathcal{E}_{K_{1}^{h}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i}E_{i}\rightarrow E_{i+1}}
– to quantify the leakage of information when
T_{\textup{AV}}
is sent, but where
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i}
is still required for future sessions.
Finally, the case of
(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})\in\{01,11\}
can be analysed in a similar way to the key secrecy condition.
The privacy of secrets
K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i}K_{2}
is maintained via the strong extractor property and QLHL
[
17
,
41
]
, where the adversary’s uncertainty of
\hat{X}_{P^{\textup{sift}}_{2},\textup{r}}
provides masking for the keys of the 2-universal hash function, as shown in Thm.
19
.
Combining all the results, we get the statement presented in the theorem.
∎

We analyse the condition with the ideal output state from Eqn.
31
, and we recall that an ideal state in
\mathcal{S}_{in}
has the form

\begin{split}\rho^{\textup{in},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE}=\outerproduct{jj'}{jj'}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes\tau_{K_{2}RK^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j}\cdots K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,m}}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{K_{1}^{h}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j-1}E_{j-1}\rightarrow E}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{E}_{K_{1}^{h}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}E_{j^{\prime}}\rightarrow E_{j^{\prime}+1}}(\tau_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}\cdots K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j-1}}\otimes\rho_{E_{j^{\prime}}}).\end{split}

(48)

We begin with the case of
(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})=(\phi,0)
,

Theorem 17
.

Consider the QAKE protocol
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. Then, there exists
\sigma_{LSE}^{\phi 0,jj^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
such that

\Delta\left(p_{\phi 0}^{\textup{real}}\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE|(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})=\phi 0},\sum_{\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}}p_{\phi 0,\tilde{j}}\sigma^{\phi 0,j,\tilde{j}^{\prime}+1}_{LSE}\right)=0.

Proof.

The goal here and in subsequent theorems is to prove that secrets that should not be leaked remains private.
The trace distance can be expressed as

\Delta\left(p_{\phi 0}^{\textup{real}}\rho^{{}^{\prime}\text{out},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE|(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})=\phi 0},\sum_{\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}}p_{\phi 0,\tilde{j}}\sigma^{\phi 0,j,\tilde{j}^{\prime}+1}_{LSE}\right)\leq\sum_{\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}}p_{\phi 0,\tilde{j}}\Delta\left(\rho^{{}^{\prime}\text{out},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE|(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})=\phi 0,\beta^{\prime}=\tilde{j}^{\prime}},\sigma^{\phi 0,j,\tilde{j}^{\prime}+1}_{LSE}\right),

(49)

for
\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}
, noting that the choice of
\beta^{\prime}<j^{\prime}
is not possible (by definition of
\beta^{\prime}
).
We can thus analyse the trace distance separately for each
\tilde{j}^{\prime}
selection.

Here, we focus on an arbitrary
\tilde{j}^{\prime}
for analysis.
We first note that with the change to idealised checks, the protocol does not utilise any of the shared secrets when
\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}=\phi
and thus would not leak them.
As such, the only operation on the secrets is the tracing away of
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i}
for
i\leq\min\{j,\tilde{j}^{\prime}+1\}
, which will no longer be used.
For input state with

\rho^{\textup{in},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
, tracing away
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i}
would lead to removal of its corresponding channel
\mathcal{E}_{K_{1}^{h}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i}E_{i}\rightarrow E_{i+1}}
for
i\in[\min\{j,j^{\prime}\},\in\{j,\tilde{j}^{\prime}+1\}]
, resulting in a state of the form
\rho^{\textup{in},j,\tilde{j}^{\prime}+1}_{LSE}
above.
Therefore, the output state is ideal.
∎

We can consider the cases of
(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})=(0,\phi)
and
\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}=\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}=0
cases together, since they share a common event
\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}=0
.

Theorem 18
.

Consider the QAKE protocol
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. Then, there exists
\sigma_{LSE}^{0\phi,jj^{\prime}},\sigma_{LSE}^{00,jj^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
such that

\begin{split}&\Delta\left(p_{0\phi}^{\textup{real}}\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE|(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})=0\phi}+p_{00}^{\textup{real}}\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE|(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})=00},\sum_{\tilde{j}\geq j}p_{0\phi,\tilde{j}}\sigma^{0\phi,\tilde{j}+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}+\sum_{\tilde{j}\geq j,\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}}p_{00,\tilde{j}\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\sigma^{00,\tilde{j}+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime}+1}_{LSE}\right)=0.\end{split}

Proof.

We can split the
trace distance according to the
\tilde{j}
and
\tilde{j}^{\prime}
values,

\sum_{\tilde{j}\geq j}p_{0\phi,\tilde{j}}\Delta\left(\rho^{{}^{\prime}\text{out},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE|(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})=0\phi,\beta=\tilde{j}},\sigma^{0\phi,\tilde{j}+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}\right)+\sum_{\tilde{j}\geq j,\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}}p_{00,\tilde{j}\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\Delta\left(\rho^{{}^{\prime}\text{out},jj^{\prime}}_{LSE|(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})=00,(\beta,\beta^{\prime})=\tilde{j}\tilde{j}^{\prime}},\sigma^{00,\tilde{j}+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime}+1}_{LSE}\right),

(50)

and evaluate the individual terms.
We note that when
\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}}\neq 1
,

K_{2}
and
R
remain private since they are not used.

We begin with studying the terms of the
(\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}},\tilde{F}_{\textup{B}})=(0,\phi)
case, which we have to split further into two sub-cases: (1)
\tilde{j}+1\leq j^{\prime}
and (2)
\tilde{j}+1>j^{\prime}
.
Since Alice sends tag
T_{\textup{AV}}
irrespective of
\tilde{F}_{\textup{A}}
, some information of
(K_{1}^{h},K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\tilde{j}})
can be leaked, though how it manifests in the ideal state depends on the sub-case.
In sub-case (1), we note that
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i}
for any
i\leq\tilde{j}
