n_{rnd}=

1\text{\times}{10}^{10}\text{\,}
signals sent,
\varepsilon_{\textup{rob}}=

1\text{\times}{10}^{-10}\text{\,}
, and
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}=

1\text{\times}{10}^{-15}\text{\,}
. The PRNG parameter is assumed to be
\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}=2^{-128}
, with
l_{\tilde{\theta}}=256
. The authentication tag used is 80-bits, i.e.
\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\AV}}=\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\BV}}=80
, and we assume the error correction efficiency to be
\gamma_{\textup{EC}}=1.2
. The system parameters are chosen to be
\mu_{0}=0.45
,
\mu_{1}=0.225
,
\mu_{2}=0
as the decoy intensity,
p_{\mu_{0}}=0.2
,
p_{\mu_{1}}=0.6
and
p_{\mu_{2}}=0.2
as the respective probabilities, and we assume the system to have zero dark counts and a QBER of
2\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
.

To study the effect of the use of PRNG, we compare the results with the keys generated in a standard BB84 scheme
[
38
]
and a BB84 scheme with unbalanced basis choices
[
24
]
.
To make the comparison fair, we utilise the same analysis results, but remove the penalties associated with PRNG, and make corresponding changes to the sifting and testing probability.
The standard BB84 scheme
[
38
]
we compare to uses the same experimental model, but due to sifting, has an additional sifting factor resulting in

\Pr[\det,\text{sift},\mu_{j}]=\frac{p_{\mu_{j}}}{2}(1-e^{-\eta\mu_{j}}).

For the BB84 scheme with unbalanced basis choice
[
24
]
, the probability of selecting
X
basis,
p_{X}
, is varied, and all rounds in the
X
basis is used as the test round.
As such, the resulting model statistics has

\displaystyle\Pr[\det,\text{test},\mu_{j}]=p_{X}^{2}p_{\mu_{j}}(1-e^{-\eta\mu_{j}})

\displaystyle\Pr[\det,\text{key},\mu_{j}]=(1-p_{X})^{2}p_{\mu_{j}}(1-e^{-\eta\mu_{j}}),

while the test rounds in the earlier schemes have only an
f_{P_{1}}
factor to the detected rate.
For both BB84 cases, due to lack of PRNG use, we simply set
\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}=\varepsilon_{\hat{\theta},\textup{PRNG}}=\varepsilon_{\textup{IRNG}}=0
, with no basis seed to refresh,
l_{\tilde{\theta}}=0
, phase error

e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime}=e_{\textup{bit},1,\textup{tol}}+g(N_{P_{1},1}^{\textup{tol}},N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}},\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1}).

They are simulated similarly by optimising
l_{K_{\textup{B}}}
with the respective changes on Matlab.
The results are shown in Fig.
7
, where the protocol with PRNG generated basis has an advantage, mostly stemming from the lack of sifting.
This shows up as a doubling of key rate of BB84, and an increasingly better key rate relative to BB84 with unbalanced basis as channel loss increases.
There is a crossover at low key lengths, likely due to the impact of the 256-bit basis seed that has to be refreshed.

D.4
Discussion

We note that compared to the original QAKE protocol, when PRNG is utilised, it loses the key recycling property.
It only has a weaker key refreshing property, where most shared secrets can be recycled when the protocol passes, except for the basis seed
\tilde{\theta}
, which needs to be refreshed every session.
If one extends the assumption on the PRNG to remain secure for time
t_{\textup{sec}}\gg t^{\prime}
and
m_{\textup{sec}}
sessions of protocol execution, one can recover the key recycling property by removing Bob’s announcement of
\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}_{\beta^{\prime}}
and using the random selection of test round to ensure that the adversary cannot successfully report a test round choice to Alice before it has received that information from Bob (i.e. Bob measured the quantum systems).
The same basis generation seed could be used with a different public input (e.g. counter) to the PRNG for basis generation, thereby removing the need to refresh basis seed
\tilde{\theta}
when the protocol passes, allowing for full key recycling.
We note that
\tilde{\theta}
would still have to be updated once
t_{\textup{sec}}
has passed or when
n_{sess}
sessions has reached, and one could refer to NIST recommendations for the refresh rate for such symmetric keys
[
2
]
.

One technical challenge to implement PRNG basis choice is the requirement of an active basis choice at the receiver to guarantee measurement in the same basis and the key rate advantage.
This requires an extremely fast optical switch at the receiver to switch between the X and Z basis.
A pulsed laser with a
500\text{\,}\mathrm{MHz}
repetition rate would require a nanosecond optical switch.
Nanosecond optical switches are commercially available with a low insertion loss of
0.7\text{\,}\mathrm{dB}
, across the whole optical communication band.
They have been demonstrated in data center networks
[
44
]
and dynamic optical switches with nanoseconds switching speed and large inputs/outputs (448
\times
448) have also been previously demonstrated
[
45
]
.
With the availability of nanosecond optical switches with low insertion loss, it may be feasible for high-speed implementation of the QAKE protocol with active basis selection.

Appendix E
Decoy-State BB84 with Pseudorandom Basis

E.1
Motivation

The QAKE protocol with pseudorandom basis has key secrecy parameter dependent on the secrecy condition of decoy-state BB84 with shared pseudorandom basis.
Here, we study such a protocol, where the main changes made relative to decoy-state BB84 are:

1.

Alice and Bob pre-share a basis generation seed
\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}
.

2.

During the protocol, Alice and Bob would use a PRNG
G^{\textup{PRNG}}
to generate the basis to prepare and measure the quantum state respectively.

3.

The sifting step is removed since Alice and Bob will always prepare and measure in the same basis.

Due to the removal of the sifting step, decoy state BB84 with shared pseudorandom basis is expected to gain a factor of 2 advantage in key generation rate.
Similar protocols have been of interest in the literature, where basis information is generated or encrypted using a PRNG
[
43
,
31
]
.
However, a complete proof of security of the use of PRNG for basis selection remains elusive in the literature, with a security proof provided in Ref.
[
43
]
against only intercept-resend attacks.
Here, we provide a complete security analysis through the
entropic uncertainty relation
(EUR).

Since the main goal in this section is to prove the security from the use of PRNG for basis generation, we focus on the main quantity of interest in the secrecy condition of QKD
[
41
,
38
]
: the smooth min-entropy
H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(X_{P_{2}}|\beta P_{1}P_{2}\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}E)_{\rho_{\beta X_{P_{2}}P_{1}P_{2}\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}E|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}
.
The smooth min-entropy quantifies the maximum length of key that can be extracted from
X_{P_{2}}
that is private from the adversary.
The smooth min-entropy is evaluated on the output state of the following process:

1.

Alice prepares decoy state BB84 states, using the basis generated from the basis generation seed
\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}
, bit value
X
and intensity choice.

2.

The adversary performs its attack on the state, mapping systems
QE
to
PBE^{\prime}
.

3.

Bob randomly selects
P_{1}
rounds and measure them in the basis generated from
\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}
, and outputs
X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}
.

4.

Based on
X
,
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
and
X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}
, the decision of whether the parameter estimation step passes is made (
\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
being the event where the parameter estimation passes).

In decoy-state BB84, entropic uncertainty relation (EUR) is one method to tightly bound the smooth min-entropy of the bit values of single photon events.
Importantly, EUR lower bounds the smooth min-entropy by a function of the phase error rate, i.e. the mismatch of measurement outcomes in the complementary basis.
Without PRNG and assuming uniform basis choice, the phase error rate matches the bit error rate (mismatch of measurement outcomes in the original basis) which can be estimated from the experiment.
We argue that this gap between bit and phase error is small when PRNG is used, resulting in a slightly lower smooth min-entropy bound (and thus key rate).
We present these arguments in later sub-sections, starting with analysing EUR with the use of PRNG in Appendix
E.2
, followed by the proof of the min-entropy lower bound in Appendix
E.3
.
Detailed proof of the theorems on EUR with PRNG in presented in the final two sub-sections.

E.2
Entropic Uncertainty Relation with PRNG

Entropic uncertainty relation describes the uncertainty a party has of performing incompatible measurements on a quantum state.
Let us define a measurement on a subsystem
C
with initial basis choice
\theta
, a bijective function
f_{b}
to map
\theta
to a different basis choice, and measurement outcome
Y
by

\mathcal{M}_{f_{b}(\theta)C\rightarrow Y}(\rho):=\Tr_{C}\,\left[\sum_{\gamma,y}\ket{y}_{Y}(\outerproduct{\gamma}{\gamma}_{\theta}\otimes O^{f_{b}(\gamma),y}_{C})\rho(\outerproduct{\gamma}{\gamma}_{\theta}\otimes O^{f_{b}(\gamma),y}_{C})\bra{y}_{Y}\right],

(82)

where
O_{C}^{f_{b}(\theta),y}
is the measurement operator on subsystem
C
with basis
f_{b}(\theta)
.
The form of EUR of particular interest in our proof examines the following scenario:

1.

The basis
\theta
is selected, and a quantum state with subsystems held by three parties, Alice (
A
), Bob (
B
), and (
E
), is generated, i.e. state
\rho_{\theta ABE}
.

2.

If Alice measures
A
in basis
\theta
and obtains outcome
Y
, i.e. perform
\mathcal{M}_{\theta A\rightarrow Y}
, Bob attempts to guess the value of
Y
from his subsystem
B
and
\theta
.

3.

If Alice measures
A
in basis
f_{b}(\theta)
for some bijective map
f_{b}
, and obtains outcome
\tilde{Y}
, i.e. perform
\mathcal{M}_{f_{b}(\theta)A\rightarrow\tilde{Y}}
, Eve attempts to guess the value of
\tilde{Y}
from her subsystem
E
and
\theta
.

The EUR then quantifies the uncertainty on the outcomes
Y
and
\tilde{Y}
that subsystem
E
and
B
has respectively, which is related to the measurement operator incompatibility
[
40
]
.
The more certain Eve can be on his guess of
Y
(smaller conditional entropy), the more uncertain Bob must be on her guess of
\tilde{Y}
(larger conditional entropy).
Here, we present a modified variant of EUR, adapted from Ref.
[
38
]
.
We note also that the EUR does not make any assumptions on how
\theta
is generated.

Theorem 29
.

Let
\rho_{\theta ABE}
be a tripartite quantum state and
f_{b}
be a bijective function on
\theta
.
Then,

H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(Y|\theta E)_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta A\rightarrow Y}(\rho_{\theta ABE})}\geq q-H_{\textup{max}}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{Y}|\theta B)_{\mathcal{M}_{f_{b}(\theta)A\rightarrow\tilde{Y}}(\rho_{\theta ABE})},

where
c_{q}=\max_{\theta}\max_{y,\tilde{y}}\norm{O^{\theta,y}_A (O^{f_b(\theta),\tilde{y}}_A)^{\dagger}}_{\infty}^{2}
and
q=\log_{2}\frac{1}{c_{q}}
.

Proof.

We follow closely the proof of EUR in Ref.
[
38
]
, where a similar form of EUR is proven, and taking inspiration from Ref.
[
40
]
.
We begin by introducing the Stinespring dilation isometry of both measurement maps
\mathcal{M}_{\theta A\rightarrow Y}
and
\mathcal{M}_{f_{b}(\theta)A\rightarrow\tilde{Y}}
respectively,

\begin{gathered}V=\sum_{\gamma,y}\ket{yy}_{YY^{\prime}}\otimes\outerproduct{\gamma}{\gamma}_{\theta}\otimes O_{A}^{\theta,y}\\
W=\sum_{\gamma,\tilde{y}}\ket{\tilde{y}\tilde{y}}_{\tilde{Y}\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}\otimes\outerproduct{\gamma}{\gamma}_{\theta}\otimes O_{A}^{f_{b}(\theta),\tilde{y}}.\end{gathered}

(83)

We can purify the state
\rho_{\theta ABE}
to

\ket{\psi}_{\theta\theta^{\prime}ABED}=\sum_{\gamma}\sqrt{p_{\alpha}}\ket{\gamma\gamma}_{\theta\theta^{\prime}}\otimes\ket{\phi_{\alpha}}_{ABED},

(84)

where
\ket{\phi_{\alpha}}_{ABED}
is a purification of
\rho_{ABE|\theta=\gamma}
, which can be performed by selecting
D
to be a sufficiently large auxiliary system.
The post-measurement state after performing the latter measurement map
\mathcal{M}_{f_{b}(\theta)A\rightarrow\tilde{Y}}
can thus be written as

\rho_{\theta\theta^{\prime}ABE\tilde{Y}\tilde{Y}^{\prime}D}=W\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{\theta\theta^{\prime}ABED}W^{\dagger}

(85)

Since the state is pure, we can apply the duality relation of smooth min- and max-entropy
[
22
,
37
]
,

H_{\textup{max}}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{Y}|\theta B)+H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{Y}|\theta^{\prime}AE\tilde{Y}^{\prime}D)=0,

(86)

and what remains is to bound the smooth min-entropy term, which is upper bounded by
H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{Y}|\theta AE\tilde{Y}^{\prime})
since
\theta=\theta^{\prime}
, and
D
can be removed by the data-processing inequality for CPTP map
\Tr_{D}

[
42
]
.

The smooth min-entropy term is evaluated on the state
[W\rho_{\theta AE}W^{\dagger}]
, where we note that it is possible to reverse the measurement of system
A
using basis
f_{b}(\theta)
and measure it using basis
\theta
.
This is expressed as a CPTP map, using the Stinespring dilation isometry of the measurement maps,

\mathcal{E}=\sum_{\gamma}\Tr_{AY^{\prime}}[VW^{\dagger}\outerproduct{\gamma}{\gamma}_{\theta}\,\cdot\,\outerproduct{\gamma}{\gamma}_{\theta}WV^{\dagger}],

(87)

where we have

\begin{split}&\mathcal{E}(\rho_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}\tilde{Y}^{\prime}})\\
=&\sum_{\gamma}\Tr_{AY^{\prime}}\,\{VW^{\dagger}[\outerproduct{\gamma}{\gamma}_{\theta}\otimes\sum_{yy^{\prime}}\ket{yy}_{YY^{\prime}}O_{A}^{f_{b}(\gamma),y}\rho_{AE|\theta=\gamma}(O_{A}^{f_{b}(\gamma),y^{\prime}})^{\dagger}\bra{y'y'}_{YY^{\prime}}]WV^{\dagger}\}\\
=&\sum_{\gamma}\Tr_{AY^{\prime}}\,\{V[\outerproduct{\gamma}{\gamma}_{\theta}\otimes\sum_{y}(O_{A}^{f_{b}(\gamma),y})^{\dagger}O_{A}^{f_{b}(\gamma),y}\rho_{AE|\theta=\gamma}\sum_{y^{\prime}}(O_{A}^{f_{b}(\gamma),y^{\prime}})^{\dagger}O_{A}^{f_{b}(\gamma),y^{\prime}}]V^{\dagger}\}\\
=&\Tr_{AY^{\prime}}[V\rho_{\theta AE}V^{\dagger}]\\
=&\rho_{\theta YE},\end{split}

(88)

where
\rho_{\theta YE}
is the post-measurement state on
\rho_{\theta ABE}
with measurement map
\mathcal{M}_{\theta A\rightarrow Y}
and the appropriate trace, and we note that
\sum_{y}(O_{A}^{f_{b}(\gamma),y})^{\dagger}O_{A}^{f_{b}(\gamma),y}=\mathbb{I}_{A}
.

By definition of the smooth min-entropy, there exists a state
\sigma_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}
and
\omega_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}
such that purified distance
P(\rho_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}\tilde{Y}^{\prime}},\sigma_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}\tilde{Y}^{\prime}})\leq\varepsilon
, and

\sigma_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}\leq 2^{-\lambda}\mathbb{I}_{\tilde{Y}}\otimes\omega_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}^{\prime}},

(89)

where
\lambda=H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{Y}|\theta AE\tilde{Y}^{\prime})
.
We note here that any CPTP map should not increase the purified distance, which allows us to define a state
\sigma_{\theta YE}:=\mathcal{E}(\sigma_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}\tilde{Y}^{\prime}})
, such that it is
\varepsilon
-close in purified distance to
\rho_{\theta YE}
,

P(\sigma_{\theta YE},\rho_{\theta YE})\leq P(\sigma_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}\tilde{Y}^{\prime}},\rho_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}\tilde{Y}^{\prime}})\leq\varepsilon.

(90)

We can apply the channel
\mathcal{E}
on both sides of Eq. (
89
), giving

\sigma_{\theta YE}\leq 2^{-\lambda}\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{I}_{\tilde{Y}}\otimes\omega_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}).

(91)

The RHS term can be expanded as

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{I}_{\tilde{Y}}\otimes\omega_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}^{\prime}})=&\sum_{\gamma y\tilde{y}}\outerproduct{\gamma y}{\gamma y}_{\theta Y}\otimes\matrixelement{\tilde{y}}{\Tr_A[O^{\gamma,y}_A(O^{f_b(\gamma),\tilde{y}}_A)^{\dagger} \hat{\omega}_{AE\tilde{Y}'}^{\gamma}O^{f_b(\gamma),\tilde{y}}_A(O^{\gamma,y}_A)^{\dagger}]}{\tilde{y}}_{\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}\\
\leq&\sum_{\gamma y\tilde{y}}\outerproduct{\gamma y}{\gamma y}_{\theta Y}\otimes\norm{O^{\gamma,y}_A(O^{f_b(\gamma)}_{\tilde{y}})^{\dagger}}_{\infty}^{2}\matrixelement{\tilde{y}}{\Tr_A(\hat{\omega}_{AE\tilde{Y}'}^{\gamma})}{\tilde{y}}_{\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}\\
\leq&\left[\max_{\gamma}\max_{y,\tilde{y}}\norm{O^{\gamma,y}_A(O^{f_b(\gamma)}_{\tilde{y}})^{\dagger}}_{\infty}^{2}\right]\sum_{\gamma y}\outerproduct{\gamma y}{\gamma y}_{\theta Y}\otimes\Tr_{A\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}[\hat{\omega}_{AE\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}^{\gamma}]\\
=&c_{q}\mathbb{I}_{Y}\otimes\hat{\omega}_{\theta E},\end{split}

(92)

where
\hat{\omega}_{\theta E}:=\sum_{\gamma}\outerproduct{\gamma}{\gamma}_{\theta}\otimes\Tr_{A\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}[\hat{\omega}_{AE\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}^{\gamma}]
and
\hat{\omega}_{AE\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}^{\gamma}:=\matrixelement{\gamma}{\omega_{\theta AE\tilde{Y}'}}{\gamma}
.
Therefore, by definition, the min-entropy is lower bounded,

H_{\textup{min}}(Y|\theta E)_{\sigma_{\theta YE}}\geq H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{Y}|\theta AE\tilde{Y}^{\prime})+q,

(93)

where
q=\log_{2}\frac{1}{c_{q}}
.
Since
\sigma_{\theta YE}
is
\varepsilon
-close in purified distance to
\rho_{\theta YE}
, the smooth min-entropy can be bounded,

H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(Y|\theta E)_{\rho_{\theta YE}}\geq H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{Y}|\theta AE\tilde{Y}^{\prime})+q.

(94)

Combining the results, we end up with

H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(Y|\theta E)_{\rho_{\theta YE}}+H_{\textup{max}}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{Y}|\theta B)_{\rho_{\theta\tilde{Y}B}}\geq q.

(95)

Noting that
\rho_{\theta YE}=\Tr_{B}[\mathcal{M}_{\theta A\rightarrow Y}(\rho_{\theta ABE})]
and
\rho_{\theta\tilde{Y}B}=\Tr_{E}[\mathcal{M}_{f_{b}(\theta)A\rightarrow\tilde{Y}}(\rho_{\theta ABE})]
, we arrive at the form of the theorem.
∎

To bound the smooth min-entropy, we thus have to compute the smooth max-entropy
H_{\textup{max}}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{Y}|\theta B)_{\mathcal{M}_{f_{b}(\theta)A\rightarrow\tilde{Y}}(\rho_{\theta ABE})}
.
A typical reduction
[
32
,
39
]
of the smooth max-entropy is to simplify it via data-processing inequality and bound it with
h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph}})
, where
e_{\textup{ph}}=\frac{wt(\tilde{Y}_{A}\oplus\tilde{Y}_{B})}{n}
is termed the phase error rate and
n
is the number of entries in
\tilde{Y}_{A}
(also number of qubit of subsystem
A
), obtained from from the state
\mathcal{M}_{f_{b}(\theta)A\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{A}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{f_{b}(\theta)B\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{B}}(\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})
.
Since we never measure in the
f_{b}(\theta)
basis in the protocol, the phase error is typically computed from the bit error rate,
e_{\textup{bit}}:=\frac{wt(Y_{A}\oplus Y_{B})}{n}
, from the state
\mathcal{M}_{\theta A\rightarrow Y_{A}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{\theta B\rightarrow Y_{B}}(\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})
.
Typically, it is argued that the expectation values of the phase and bit error matches,
\mathbb{E}[e_{\textup{bit}}]=\mathbb{E}[e_{\textup{ph}}]
if the basis
\theta\in\{0,1\}^{n}
is chosen uniformly at random
and independent from subsystems
ABE

[
40
,
38
]
.

Figure 8:
Description of quantum algorithms
\mathcal{B}_{e}^{\textup{RNG}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{e^{\prime}}^{\textup{RNG}}
. Each algorithm begins with a run of the random number generator
G^{\textup{RNG}}
which outputs
\theta
, followed by the generation of quantum state
\rho_{\theta ABE}
via a protocol
\mathcal{P}_{Adv}
(e.g. QAKE protocol with adversarial attack), and ends with a measurement on subsystems
AB
to compute the bit error
e_{\textup{bit}}
and phase error
e_{\textup{ph}}
respectively. The output
D_{\textup{PE}}
forms a decision which defines the event set
\Omega=\{D_{\textup{PE}}=1\}
. We label the resources required for state preparation and measurement as
t
, with the other steps (e.g. flipping of
\theta
when computing phase error, and computing of bit/phase error) contributing resource
t_{MG}
, and overall resource requirement
t_{B}=t+t_{MG}
.

Figure 9:
Description of distinguishing adversary with output decision
D
. The adversary computes
D_{\textup{PE}}\land D_{\textup{PE}}^{\prime}
, where
D_{\textup{PE}}
and
D_{\textup{PE}}^{\prime}
are obtained from the quantum process
\mathcal{P}_{Adv}
. The SW function is a switch which sets
\hat{e}_{\textup{bit}}=0
and
\hat{e}_{\textup{ph}}=0
respectively when
D_{\textup{PE}}\land D_{\textup{PE}}^{\prime}=0
, and leave
\hat{e}_{\textup{bit}}=e_{\textup{bit}}
and
\hat{e}_{\textup{ph}}=e_{\textup{ph}}
when
D_{\textup{PE}}\land D_{\textup{PE}}^{\prime}=1
. The decision
D
is made by comparing the bit-phase error gap with a target
\delta_{\textup{gap}}
. The resource required for the adversary is defined as
t_{D}
, which comprises of
2t_{B}
and extra steps
t_{\text{ex}}
.

This argument breaks down when a PRNG is utilised since the basis is no longer uniformly distributed.
Instead, we argue that the difference between the phase and bit error rates in the case where a quantum-secure PRNG is used (bit-phase error gap) is small.
Consider two separate instances of an adversary that is bounded by resource
t
, and prepares the state
\rho_{\theta ABE}
, which would be measured in
\theta
and
\bar{\theta}
respectively (
f_{b}
refers to bit flip in this case) to obtain the bit and phase errors (see Fig.
8
).
The pseudorandomness property of the PRNG ensures that a similarly small bit-phase error gap must be present when a PRNG is utilised.
Otherwise, the bit-phase error gap can be utilised by a distinguisher with
t^{\prime}\geq t_{D}
resources (see Fig.
9
) to distinguish a PRNG from an ideal RNG with probability greater than
\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}
, where
t_{D}=2(t+t_{MG})+t_{ex}
.
We recall that the PRNG is chosen with
t^{\prime}=2(t+t_{MG})+t_{ex}+t_{SG,PR}
.

We first show that the phase error rate can be lower bounded by the bit error rate with some penalty, stemming from the low bit-phase error gap for PRNG.

Theorem 30
.

Suppose that the basis
\theta
is generated from a
(t^{\prime},\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}})
-quantum secure PRNG with
t^{\prime}\geq t_{D}
and let
\rho_{\theta ABE}^{\textup{PRNG}}
be the state prepared by the adversary (via protocol
\mathcal{P}_{Adv}
), where
A
and
B
are
n
-qubit states, and
\theta
remain independent when generated by an IRNG,
\rho_{\theta ABE}^{\textup{IRNG}}=\tau_{\theta}\otimes\rho_{ABE}
.
Let
\Omega:=\{D_{\textup{PE}}=1\}
be an event that can be decided from
E
, and
e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}
be the tolerance value for an upper bound on the bit error value, i.e.
\Pr[e_{\textup{bit}}\geq e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}|\Omega]_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta A\rightarrow Y_{A}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{\theta B\rightarrow Y_{B}}(\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})}\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{bit}}
.
Then, an upper bound on the phase error for state
\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}}
after measurement is

\begin{gathered}\Pr[e_{\textup{ph}}\geq e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}]_{\mathcal{M}_{\bar{\theta}A\rightarrow\tilde{Y}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{\bar{\theta}B\rightarrow\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{\rho}_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})}\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}\\
e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}=e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}+2\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}\ln\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\textup{IRNG}}}}\\
\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}=2\varepsilon_{\textup{IRNG}}+\frac{\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}}{p_{\Omega}^{2}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{bit}}.\end{gathered}

Proof.

We first show that the bit-phase error gap is small.
Since the algorithm in Fig.
9
,
\mathcal{A}_{D}^{G^{\textup{PRNG}}_{\mathcal{K}},t^{\prime}}
has resource restriction
t_{D}
, the quantum-secure PRNG cannot be distinguished via this algorithm,

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\vars{A}_D^{G^{\PRNG}_{\vars{K}},t_D}=1]-\Pr[\vars{A}_D^{G^{\TRNG},t_D}=1]}\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}.

(96)

By definition of the outcome
D
, the bit-phase error gap obtained from PRNG is no much different from that of an IRNG,

\Pr[\hat{e}_{\textup{ph}}-\hat{e}_{\textup{bit}}>\delta_{\textup{gap}}]_{\mathcal{A}_{D}^{G^{\textup{PRNG}}_{\mathcal{K}},t_{D}}}-\Pr[\hat{e}_{\textup{ph}}-\hat{e}_{\textup{bit}}>\delta_{\textup{gap}}]_{\mathcal{A}_{D}^{G^{\textup{IRNG}},t_{D}}}\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}},

(97)

What remains to evaluate the IRNG bit-phase error rate gap.

We first show that the expected value of the bit and phase error matches for the IRNG case.
When we have
D_{\textup{PE}}=0
or
D_{\textup{PE}}^{\prime}=0
, both bit and phase error would be set to 0,
\hat{e}_{\textup{ph}}=\hat{e}_{\textup{bit}}
, and we can expand the probability

\Pr[\hat{e}_{\textup{ph}}-\hat{e}_{\textup{bit}}>\delta_{\textup{gap}}]_{\mathcal{A}_{D}^{G^{\textup{IRNG}},t_{D}}}=p_{\Omega}^{2}\Pr[e_{\textup{ph}}-e_{\textup{bit}}>\delta_{\textup{gap}}|\Omega,\Omega^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{A}_{D}^{G^{\textup{IRNG}},t_{D}}}.

(98)

This conditional probability matches the probability of the phase-bit error gap exceeding
\delta_{gap}
, when the phase error and bit error are drawn from the conditional state
\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{IRNG}}
in both instances instead.
By assumption, when IRNG is utilised,
\theta
is independent of the prepared quantum state.
Furthermore, since
\Omega
is decided from
E
,
\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{IRNG}}=\tau_{\theta}\otimes\rho_{ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{IRNG}}
.
Let
e_{i}=Y_{A,i}\oplus Y_{B,i}
and
e_{i}^{\prime}=\tilde{Y}_{A,i}\oplus\tilde{Y}_{B,i}
be the bit error and phase error of round
i
of the two instances.
Since
\theta
is uniformly random, the post-measurement state
\rho_{Y_{A}Y_{B}|\Omega}
and
\rho_{\tilde{Y}_{A}\tilde{Y}_{B}|\Omega^{\prime}}
are identical
(after tracing out
\theta E
).
As such, noting that errors between rounds can be correlated, we have that the expected bit and phase error matches, conditioned on earlier rounds,
\mathbb{E}[e_{i}|e_{1}^{i-1}]=\mathbb{E}[e_{i}^{\prime}|e_{1}^{{}^{\prime}i-1}]
, where the indexing
e_{i}^{j}=e_{i}\cdots e_{j}
.

Here, we demonstrate that both the bit and phase error are close to some combination of the conditional expectation value
\mathbb{E}[e_{i}|e_{1}^{i-1}]
to find the bit-phase error gap.
We define

Z_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{j}e_{i}-\mathbb{E}[e_{i}|e_{1}^{i-1}],

(99)

where
e_{1}^{i-1}
labels the first
i-1
outcomes for error measurement.
The observed bit error can be expressed as

e_{\textup{bit}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}=\frac{Z_{n}}{n}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[e_{i}|e_{1}^{i-1}].

(100)

The variable
Z_{j}
is bounded, the set
n
is finite and

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[Z_{j}|Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{j-1}]\\
=&Z_{j-1}+\mathbb{E}[e_{j}|Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{j-1}]-\mathbb{E}[e_{j}|e_{1}^{j-1}]\\
=&Z_{j-1},\end{split}

(101)

since
Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{j-1}
values can be computed from
e_{1}^{j-1}
and vice versa, using the values of
\mathbb{E}[e_{i}|e_{1}^{i-1}]
recursively.
These properties implies that
Z_{j}
is a martingale, and since
\absolutevalue{Z_j-Z_{j-1}}\leq 1
, we can apply the Azuma-Hoeffding inequality
[
25
]
,

\begin{gathered}\Pr[Z_{n}-Z_{0}\leq-n\delta]\leq e^{-\frac{n\delta^{2}}{2}}\\
\Pr[Z_{n}-Z_{0}\geq n\delta]\leq e^{-\frac{n\delta^{2}}{2}}.\end{gathered}

(102)

Performing the same analysis for
e_{ph}
, we arrive at

\begin{gathered}\Pr[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbb{E}[e_{i}|e_{1}^{i-1}]}{n}\geq e_{\textup{bit}}+\delta]\leq e^{-\frac{n\delta^{2}}{2}}\\
\Pr[e_{\textup{ph}}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbb{E}[e^{\prime}_{i}|e_{1}^{{}^{\prime}i-1}]}{n}+\delta]\leq e^{-\frac{n\delta^{2}}{2}},\end{gathered}

(103)

where a different (upper or lower) bound is utilised for the two instances.

Since the states are identical, the sum of conditional expectations of
e_{i}
matches.
As such, the bit-phase error gap is

\Pr[e_{\textup{ph}}-e_{\textup{bit}}\geq 2\delta|\Omega,\Omega^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{A}_{D}^{G^{\textup{IRNG}},t_{D}}}\leq 2e^{-\frac{n\delta^{2}}{2}}.

(104)

Defining
\delta_{\textup{gap}}=2\delta
, the bit-phase error of the algorithm is

\Pr[e_{\textup{ph}}-e_{\textup{bit}}\geq 2\delta|\Omega,\Omega^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{A}_{e_{\textup{bit}},e_{\textup{ph}}}^{G^{\textup{PRNG}}_{\mathcal{K}},t_{D}}}\leq 2e^{-\frac{n\delta^{2}}{2}}+\frac{\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}}{p_{\Omega}^{2}}.

(105)

by combining the results.
We note that the bit and phase error are obtained from two separate instances of bit value measurement and phase value measurement on identical
\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}}
state.
Given that the bit error value measured is, with probability larger than
1-\varepsilon_{\textup{bit}}
, larger than
e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}
, we are guaranteed that the phase error value measured on the same state satisfies

\Pr[e_{\textup{ph}}\geq e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}+2\delta|\Omega,\Omega^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{A}_{e_{\textup{bit}},e_{\textup{ph}}}^{G^{\textup{PRNG}}_{\mathcal{K}},t_{D}}}\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}},

(106)

where
\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}=2e^{-\frac{n\delta^{2}}{2}}+\frac{\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}}{p_{\Omega}^{2}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{bit}}
.
Since the probability no longer depends on the bit error rate
e_{\textup{bit}}
, we can replace
\mathcal{A}_{e_{\textup{bit}},e_{\textup{ph}}}^{G^{\textup{PRNG}}}
with simply a phase error measurement on the common input state,

\Pr[e_{\textup{ph}}\geq e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}+2\delta|\Omega]_{\mathcal{M}_{\bar{\theta}A\rightarrow\tilde{Y}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{\bar{\theta}B\rightarrow\tilde{Y}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{\rho}_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})}\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}.

(107)

Further letting
\varepsilon_{\textup{IRNG}}:=e^{-\frac{n\delta^{2}}{2}}
, a simple rearrangement would yield the theorem.
∎

Combining the modified EUR and PRNG results, we can obtain a lower bound on the smooth min-entropy of interest.

Theorem 31
.

Suppose that the basis
\theta
is generated from a
(t^{\prime},\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}})
-quantum secure PRNG, with
t^{\prime}\geq t_{D}
, and let
\rho_{\theta ABE}^{\textup{PRNG}}
be the state prepared by the adversary (via protocol
\mathcal{P}_{Adv}
), where
A
and
B
are
n
-qubit states,
and
\theta
remain independent when generated by an IRNG,
\rho_{\theta ABE}^{\textup{IRNG}}=\tau_{\theta}\otimes\rho_{ABE}
.
Furthermore, assume the measurement operators of Alice act independently and identically on the subsystems,
O_{A}^{\theta,y}=\bigotimes_{i}O_{A_{i}}^{\theta_{i},y_{i}}
and have overlap
c_{q_{1}}:=\max_{\theta_{i}}\max_{y_{i},\tilde{y}_{i}}\norm{O^{\theta_i,y_i}_{A_i}(O^{\bar{\theta}_i,\tilde{y}_i}_{A_i})^{\dagger}}_{\infty}^{2}
, with
q_{1}=\log_{2}\frac{1}{c_{q_{1}}}
.
Let
\Omega:=\{D_{\textup{PE}}=1\}
be an event that can be decided by
E
, then the conditional smooth min-entropy for a protocol with resource utilisation
t
(including adversary’s resources) be

H_{\textup{min}}^{\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}}}(Y|\theta E)_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta A\rightarrow Y}(\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})}\geq n[q_{1}-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}})],

where

\begin{gathered}e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}=e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}+2\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}\ln\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\textup{IRNG}}}}\\
\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}=2\varepsilon_{\textup{IRNG}}+\frac{\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}}{p_{\Omega}^{2}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{bit}},\end{gathered}

e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}
is the tolerance value for an upper bound on the bit error value, and
\Pr[e_{\textup{bit}}\geq e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}|\Omega]_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta A\rightarrow Y_{A}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{\theta B\rightarrow Y_{B}}(\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})}\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{bit}}
.

Proof.

For the state
\rho_{\theta ABE}^{\textup{PRNG}}
, we are interested in finding the conditional smooth-min entropy, of
Y
conditioned on
E
, for the PRNG state conditioned on event
\Omega
,
H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(Y|\theta E)_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta A\rightarrow Y}(\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})}
.
Applying the entropic uncertainty relation presented in Thm.
29
with the bijective function
f_{b}(\theta)=\bar{\theta}
, we get

\begin{split}&H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(Y|\theta E)_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta A\rightarrow Y}(\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})}\\
\geq&q-H_{\textup{max}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(\tilde{Y}|\theta B)_{\mathcal{M}_{\bar{\theta}A\rightarrow Y}(\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})}\\
\geq&q-H_{\textup{max}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(\tilde{Y}_{A}|\tilde{Y}_{B})_{\mathcal{M}_{\bar{\theta}A\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{A}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{\bar{\theta}B\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{B}}(\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})},\end{split}

(108)

where the second inequality is obtained by the data processing inequality
[
42
]
.
We can expand
q=\log_{2}\frac{1}{c_{q}}
with

c_{q}=\max_{\theta}\max_{y,\tilde{y}}\norm{O^{\theta,y}_A(O^{\bar{\theta},\tilde{y}}_A)^{\dagger}}_{\infty}^{2},

(109)

where the bijective function is a flip from
\theta
to
\bar{\theta}
.
Since the measurement operators are assumed to be independent and identical operators with overlap
and applied independently on each subsystem
A_{i}
,
the norm can be simplified as

\begin{split}\norm{O^{\theta,y}_A(O^{\bar{\theta},\tilde{y}}_A)^{\dagger}}_{\infty}^{2}=&\norm{\otimes_{i=1}^{n}(O^{\theta_i,y_i}_{A_i})(\otimes_{i=1}^{n}(O^{\bar{\theta}_i,\tilde{y}_i}_{A_i}))^{\dagger}}_{\infty}^{2}\\
=&\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\norm{F^{\theta_i,y_i}_{A_i}(O^{\bar{\theta}_i,\tilde{y}_i}_{A_i})^{\dagger}}_{\infty}^{2}\end{split}

(110)

Taking the maximum
\theta
would mean taking the maximum
\theta_{i}
for each index
i
.
As such, we have

q=nq_{1}
.

From Thm.
30
, we know that the state
\mathcal{M}_{\bar{\theta}A\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{A}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{\bar{\theta}B\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{B}}(\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})
has a phase error upper bounded by
e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}
with probability larger than
1-\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}
.
As such, there exist a state
\sigma_{\tilde{Y}_{A}\tilde{Y}_{B}}
that is
\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}
-close in trace distance with a maximum phase error of
e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}
.
The purified distance of these states can be bounded by
\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}}

[
42
]
.
Therefore, if we set
\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}\geq\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}}
, we can upper bound the smooth max-entropy by the max-entropy evaluated on
\sigma
,

H_{\textup{max}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(\tilde{Y}_{A}|\tilde{Y}_{B})_{\mathcal{M}_{\bar{\theta}A\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{A}}\circ\mathcal{M}_{\bar{\theta}B\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{B}}(\rho_{\theta ABE|\Omega}^{\textup{PRNG}})}\leq H_{\textup{max}}(\tilde{Y}_{A}|\tilde{Y}_{B})_{\sigma_{\tilde{Y}_{A}\tilde{Y}_{B}}}.

(111)

From Ref.
[
32
]
, the max-entropy can be computed from the minimum number of bits required to correct for the error.
Since the two values can only differ in at most
\lfloor ne_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}\rfloor
locations, we have
[
39
]

\begin{split}H_{\textup{max}}(\tilde{Y}_{A}|\tilde{Y}_{B})_{\sigma_{\tilde{Y}_{A}\tilde{Y}_{B}}}\leq&\log_{2}\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor ne_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}\rfloor}{n\choose i}\\
\leq&nh_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}).\end{split}

(112)

which completes the proof.
∎

E.3
Security of decoy-state BB84 with PRNG basis

We can now adapt the min-entropy result to the actual decoy-state BB84 protocol.

Theorem 32
.

Considering an adversary with resource limitation
t
and the use of
(t^{\prime},\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}})
-secure PRNG with
t^{\prime}\geq 2(t+t_{MG})+t_{ex}
, the min-entropy of interest, which can be described by the steps above, is lower bounded by

H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(X_{P_{2}}|\beta P_{1}P_{2}\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}E)_{\rho_{\beta X_{P_{2}}P_{1}P_{2}\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}E|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}\geq N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}-N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime}),

where
\varepsilon\geq\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}}
, with

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}=2\varepsilon_{\textup{IRNG}}+\frac{\varepsilon_{\textup{PRNG}}}{p_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}^{2}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1}

\displaystyle e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime}=e_{\textup{bit},1,\textup{tol}}+g(N_{P_{1},1}^{\textup{tol}},N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}},\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1})+2\sqrt{\frac{2}{N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}}\ln\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\textup{IRNG}}}}

and
g(a,b,c)=\sqrt{\frac{(b+a)(a+1)}{2a^{2}b}\ln\frac{1}{c}}
.

Proof.

We first reduce the protocol to a worse case protocol to simplify analysis.
The choice of decoy state intensity can be delayed, by allowing Alice to first sample a random photon number count for
n_{rnd}
rounds,
N_{\textup{PNR}}
, and select decoy choice by sampling
v
based on
N_{\textup{PNR}}
.
In general, the photon numbers
N_{\textup{PNR}}
can be accessible to the adversary, and any rounds
Q_{i}
where there are multi-photon events could leak the basis and bit value.
As such, we let Alice announce the basis and bit value for all rounds with multi-photon events.
For single-photon events, preparing BB84 states on system
Q_{i}
is identical to preparing
\ket{\Phi^+}_{A_{i}Q_{i}}
, and performing measurement on
A_{i}
with the X and Z-basis operators.
This modification allows us to delay the measurement on
A_{i}
to a later step.
We note that the corresponding measurement operators
O_{A_{i}}^{\theta_{i},X_{i}}
acts independently and identically on the subsystems and has overlap
q_{1}=1
, assuming ideal preparation of the BB84 states.
As such, we have a worse case of Alice preparing and sending out
N_{\textup{PNR}}
,
\theta_{\geq 2}
,
X_{\geq 2}
, and
Q_{i}
of single-photon events instead of that in step 1 of the protocol.
In fact, WLOG, we can let the adversary prepare the single-photon states, and select
P
at the same time, preparing
\rho_{A_{P,1}B_{P,1}E^{\prime}}
.
For steps 3 and 4, Alice and Bob can operate with simply these single-photon events since
\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
is decided only on such events.
This worse case protocol can thus be summarised as

1.

The adversary announces
\beta
to Alice and Bob.

2.

Alice draws a random photon number count for
n_{rnd}
rounds,
N_{\textup{PNR}}
, based on a probability distribution computed from the intensity and probability settings.

3.

From the basis generation seed
\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}
and
N_{\textup{PNR}}
, Alice announces the basis
\theta_{\geq 2}
and random bit string
X_{\geq 2}
.

4.

The adversary announces
P
and prepares accordingly the state
\rho_{A_{P_{*},1}B_{P_{*},1}E^{\prime}}
, where
A_{P_{*},1}
is handed to Alice and
B_{P_{*},1}
is handed to Bob, where
P_{*,1}
is the set of single-photon events in
P
.

5.

Bob selects randomly a subset
P_{1}
from set P. Define
P_{2}=P\setminus P_{1}
.

6.

Bob measures
B_{P_{1},1}
to obtain
X_{P_{1},1}^{\prime}
and Alice measures
A_{P_{1},1}
to obtain
X_{P_{1},1}
.

7.

Alice and Bob computes and checks the condition
\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
, and they announce
\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}
.

8.

Alice measures
A_{P_{2},1}
to obtain
X_{P_{2},1}
.

Note that
X_{P_{1},0}
would be announced in the original protocol, but this is simply a random string uncorrelated with the rest of the variables and can be removed from the min-entropy.

We begin by first considering another worse case where Alice truncates the number of single-photon rounds in set
P_{2}
to
N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}
, which can occur when
\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
occurs.
For
\tilde{\Omega}_{PE}
, Alice and Bob can replace the subsystem with
N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}
Bell states, which does not affect min-entropy since it is evaluated conditioned on
\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
.
As such,

H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(X_{P_{2}}|\beta P_{1}P_{2}\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}E^{\prime})_{\rho_{\beta X_{P_{2}}P_{1}P_{2}\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}E^{\prime}|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}\geq H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon}(X_{P_{2}^{\textup{trunc}},1}|\beta P_{1}P_{2}\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}X_{P_{1},1}^{\prime}X_{P_{1},1}N_{\textup{PNR}}X_{\geq 2}E^{\prime})_{\rho_{|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}},

where
trunc
superscript indicate truncation.

The state generated,
\rho_{\beta X_{P_{2}^{\textup{trunc}},1}P_{1}P_{2}\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}X_{P_{1},1}^{\prime}N_{\textup{PNR}}X_{\geq 2}E^{\prime}|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}
, matches that described in EUR, Thm.
31
.
We note that the state can be expressed as

\rho_{X_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{trunc}}\beta\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}E|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}=\mathcal{M}_{\theta_{P_{2},1}A_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{trunc}}\rightarrow X^{\textup{trunc}}_{P_{2},1}}(\rho_{\beta\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}A_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{trunc}}B_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{trunc}}E|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}),

(113)

where
E=P_{1}P_{2}X_{P_{1},1}^{\prime}X_{P_{1},1}N_{\textup{PNR}}X_{\geq 2}E^{\prime}
.
We note that
\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
can be determined from
X_{P_{1},1}^{\prime}X_{P_{1},1}N_{\textup{PNR}}P_{1}P_{2}
, which are part of
E
.
By the fair sampling assumption,
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are independent of any basis selection.
Moreover, if the basis choice
\theta_{\beta,P_{2}^{\textup{trunc}},1}
is selected from an IRNG, it would be uniformly random and independent from the adversary (also from
\beta
), since
\theta_{P_{1}}
and
\theta_{P_{2}}
are uncorrelated, i.e. we have
\tau_{\theta_{P_{2},1}}
and uncorrelated from the rest of the state.

The final quantity to define is
e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}
, which in this case is the phase tolerance bound for the set
P_{2,1}^{\textup{trunc}}
.
This can be obtained by the modified Serfling bound
[
12
,
39
]
, since the choice of sets
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
is random.
Since
\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
gives
N_{P_{1},1}^{\textup{tol}}
as a lower bound on the number of single photon events, and that the bit error rate is upper bounded by
e_{b,1,tol}
, we have that

\Pr[e_{\textup{bit}}\geq e_{\textup{bit},1,\textup{tol}}+g(N_{P_{1},1}^{\textup{tol}},N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}},\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1})]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{serf},1}.

(114)

Therefore, we can apply Thm.
31
to arrive at the result.
∎

Appendix F
Step-Efficient Client Authentication

F.1
Protocol Description

Figure 10:
Summary of step-efficient CA protocol with two communication steps. In the first communication step, the server sends quantum states to the client, alongside its tag for validation by the client. After validation and client’s measurement, the response would consist of the test round results, client validation tag for server to validate the client’s identity, and information for error correction. At the end of the protocol, both parties would separately decide whether to update secrets or the label (indicating a protocol failure).

Inspired by the client authentication protocol in Ref.
[
17
]
, we developed a step-efficient client authentication (CA) protocol requiring only two communication steps.
The key modifications made to the proposed QAKE protocol with pseudorandom basis selection are:

1.

Removing server validation since they are not required for client authentication. The validation is instead performed at the start of the next session, where it is required to ensure updated secrets are secure.

2.

Replacing label agreement with the server utilising his own index
\alpha^{\prime}
for the session. Any index mismatch would be picked up during client or server validation, and would be corrected with an additional session.

Incorporating these changes, alongside compression of communication rounds, leads to a protocol with two communication steps summarised in Fig.
10
.

The client and server pre-share secrets and functions similar to the AKE protocol, except authentication key
K_{2}
and hash function
h_{2}
, which are not necessary in this protocol.
The protocol is described in Protocol 3.

F.2
Security Definition

The security definition for the protocol follows from standard client authentication security requirements.
We begin with the first condition of robustness, which guarantees that the protocol should always succeed when a valid client approaches the server.
We note that since the protocol has no label agreement step, any mismatch in indices requires 2 sessions to resolve.
However, since index mismatch is unlikely in the honest case, we relax the robustness requirement to ensure that authentication passes in 2 sessions (similar to how clients has to try a second time to login when the first login fails).
As such, we formally define this two session robustness as

Protocol

3
Step-Efficient Client Authentication

Goal.
Server authenticates client.

1.

Server’s Tag and Label
: Server sends its authentication tag
T_{\textup{SV},\alpha^{\prime}}
and index
\alpha^{\prime}
to the client.

2.

Server State Preparation
: The server generates a
n_{rnd}
-bit basis string using the basis seed,
\theta_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{\prime}=g(\tilde{\theta}_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{\prime})
, randomly chooses a
n_{rnd}
-bit string
X
and a
n_{rnd}
-trit string
v\in\{0,1,2\}^{n_{rnd}}
according to probability distribution
p_{v}
. The server then sends
n_{rnd}
quantum systems
Q_{i}
in phase-randomised coherent BB84 states
\left\{\rho_{Q_{i}}^{\theta_{i}^{\prime},X_{i},\mu_{v_{i}}}\right\}_{i\in[1,n_{rnd}]}
, with basis
\theta_{i}^{\prime}
, bit value
X_{i}
, and intensity
\mu_{v_{i}}
, to the client, acting as the “challenge”.

3.

Server Validation and Client Label Alignment
: Based on the received label
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}
, the client aligns its label. If
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}>\alpha
, the client updates
\beta=\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}
and sets
D_{\textup{C}}=1
. If
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}=\alpha
, the server sets
\beta=\alpha
and checks if
\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\alpha}=T_{\textup{SV},\alpha^{\prime},\textup{r}}
. If it matches, the client sets
D_{\textup{C}}=1
. Otherwise, the client sets
D_{\textup{C}}=0
and replaces any further responses with a random string. After the alignment is complete, the client sends
D_{\textup{C}}
and
\alpha
to the server.

4.

Client Measurement
: The client measures subsystems
Q_{i}
using basis
\theta_{\beta}=g(\tilde{\theta}_{\beta})
, and records outcome
X_{i}^{\prime}
. If the client detects no clicks, it declares
X_{i}^{\prime}=\perp
. If multiple clicks are detected, the bit value
X_{i}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
is randomly selected.

5.

Test Round Announcement
: The client records the detection rounds,
P=\{i:X_{i}^{\prime}\neq\perp\}
, and randomly splits it into two sets,
\absolutevalue{P_1}=\lceil f_{P_{1}}\absolutevalue{P}\rceil
. The client announces
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
and
X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}
, and the server receives the announcement
P_{1,r}
,
P_{2,r}
and
X_{P_{1},r}^{\prime}
.

6.

Error Correction
:
The client generates a syndrome
syn=f_{\textup{syn}}(X_{P_{2}}^{\prime})
and forwards it to the server. The server receives the syndrome
syn_{\textup{r}}
and computes the corrected bit string
\hat{X}_{P_{2}}^{\prime}=f_{\textup{ec}}(X_{P_{2,\textup{r}}},syn_{\textup{r}})
.

7.

Client Validation
: The client generates a tag
T_{\textup{CV}}=h_{1}(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}||P_{1}||P_{2}||X_{P_{2}}^{\prime}||syn)\oplus K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}
and forwards it to the server. The server receives tag
T_{\textup{CV},\textup{r}}
and generates verification tag
\tilde{T}_{\textup{CV}}=h_{1}(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},X_{P_{1,\textup{r}}}||P_{1,\textup{r}}||P_{2,\textup{r}}||\hat{X}_{P_{2}}^{\prime}||syn_{\textup{r}})\oplus K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\alpha^{\prime}}
and checks if
T_{\textup{CV},\textup{r}}=\tilde{T}_{\textup{CV}}
. If the tags matches, the server validates the client and output
D_{\textup{CV}}=1
, otherwise, he sets
D_{\textup{CV}}=0
.

8.

Parameter Estimation
: The server checks if: (1) the number of test rounds matches the expected number
\absolutevalue{P_{1,\rr}}=\lceil f_{P_{1}}\absolutevalue{P}\rceil
, (2) number of rounds in set
P_{1}
exceeds a threshold,
\absolutevalue{P_1}\geq P_{1,\textup{tol}}
, (3) the lower bound on single-photon events in sets
P_{2,\textup{r}}
and
P_{1,\textup{r}}
(estimated via decoy state analysis) exceeds a threshold,
\hat{N}^{\textup{LB}}_{P_{2,\textup{r}},1}\geq N_{P_{2,\textup{r}},1}^{\textup{tol}}
,
\hat{N}^{\textup{LB}}_{P_{1,\textup{r}},1}\geq N_{P_{1,\textup{r}},1}^{\textup{tol}}
, (4) the upper bound on single-photon bit-error rate in set
P_{1,\textup{r}}
(estimated via decoy-state analysis) falls below a threshold,
\hat{e}_{\textup{bit},P_{1,\textup{r}},1}^{\textup{UB}}\leq e_{\textup{bit},1,\textup{tol}}
, and (5) the upper bound on bit error rate in set
P_{2,\textup{r}}
(estimated via Serfling bound) falls below a threshold,
\hat{e}_{\textup{bit},P_{2,\textup{r}}}^{\textup{UB}}\leq e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}
. If all checks pass, the server sets
D_{\textup{PE}}=1
, otherwise it sets
D_{\textup{PE}}=0
.

9.

Secret Update and Label Update
: The server compares the index received
\alpha_{\textup{r}}
,
D_{\textup{C}}
and its own index
\alpha^{\prime}
. The server first updates
\alpha_{\textup{r}}=\alpha_{\textup{r}}+1
if
D_{\textup{C}}=0
. If updated
\alpha_{\textup{r}}>\alpha^{\prime}
, the server sets
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha_{\textup{r}}-1
and sets
D_{\textup{SA}}=0
, otherwise he sets
D_{\textup{SA}}=1
(aligned indices). The client decides whether to generate the server’s tag and update the basis seed based on
D_{\textup{C}}
, while the server decides on the success of the authentication via
F_{\textup{S}}=D_{\textup{PE}}\land D_{\textup{CV}}\land D_{\textup{SA}}
. When the client (resp. the server) decides to generate the tag and update the basis seed, i.e.
D_{\textup{C}}=1
(resp.
F_{\textup{S}}=1
), it performs privacy amplification
\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\beta}||\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}=h_{\textup{PA}}(R,X_{P_{2}}^{\prime})
(resp.
T_{\textup{SV},\alpha^{\prime}}||\tilde{\theta}_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{\prime}=h_{\textup{PA}}(R,\hat{X}_{P_{2}}^{\prime})
) and sets
\alpha=\beta
. If basis seed update and server tag generation is not performed, then the client (resp. the server) would update its label,
\alpha=\beta+1
(resp.
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha^{\prime}+1
).

Definition 8

(
\varepsilon_{2,\textup{rob}}
-2-session-robustness)
.

A CA protocol is
\varepsilon_{2,\textup{rob}}
-2-session-robustness if it passes with high probability in the absence of any adversary in either of the next two rounds, i.e. it fails to pass both rounds with low probability

\Pr[F_{\textup{S},j}=0,F_{\textup{S},j+1}=0]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{rob}},

where
F_{\textup{S},i}
refers to the server’s label in the
i
-th round, and
j\in[1,n_{sess}-1]
, where
n_{sess}
is the maximum number of sessions.

We note here that there can be other ways of defining the robustness condition.
One such method is to assume that all prior sessions of the protocol are performed in the absence of an adversary.
This is unlikely to result in any index mismatch at the current session, and thus robustness can be guaranteed with high probability with a single session.

The second security condition in client authentication requires that a server interacting with an invalid client (or adversary) return an authentication failure with high probability.
As such, when the authentication passes, the server can be highly confident that the client is valid.
Therefore, we formally define the client authentication condition as

Definition 9

(
\varepsilon_{\textup{CV}}
-client validation)
.

An CA protocol has
\varepsilon_{\textup{CV}}
-client validation if the authentication fails with high probability when the adversary attempts to impersonate the client, i.e.

\Pr[F_{\textup{S}}=1|D_{\textup{C}}=\phi]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{CV}}.

While there is no imposition on the secrets of the protocol, they would obey additional conditions for future sessions to remain secure.
This separate security condition has the same definition as shared secrets privacy in QAKE, albeit with different ideal input and output states.

F.3
Overall Protocol Security

We can similarly define a
n_{sess}
-session protocol of CA, where all sessions has to satisfy the security condition, with each session satisfying some
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},i}
-security condition defined by the trace distance from the ideal output state with
\Pr[F_{\textup{S}}=1,D_{\textup{C}}=\phi]=0
.
We follow the same idea from Appendix.
C.1

to reduce the multi-session analysis to single session, where additional shared secret preservation is necessary in the intermediate steps.
However, since there is only a single security condition, client authentication, we shall examine both conditions together, defining the overall security as

\Delta(\rho_{D_{\textup{C}}F_{\textup{S}}LSE},\rho^{\textup{ideal},\textup{int}}_{D_{\textup{C}}F_{\textup{S}}LSE})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}},

(115)

and it remains to identify the set of ideal input and output states.

The main difference in the identification of ideal input/output states for this protocol is the ability of the adversary to gain partial information of secrets and partial control of the client’s action.
Firstly, the client’s tag can almost always be generated since there is no server check during the session itself.
This leads to the authentication key
K^{\textup{h}}_{1}
and authentication masking key
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}
being correlated with the adversary, although this correlation is via the tag
T_{\textup{CV}}
.
This is without a corresponding trace of the authentication masking key in the session since the client’s index is not updated, though one expects the corresponding trace to occur when the client performs the server check in later sessions.
Secondly, the generation of the server’s tag verification value by the client,
\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\alpha}
, can be sometimes influenced by the adversary when it has prior knowledge of the basis generation seed.
However, the adversary would not have direct access to this value since it is not announced.
To account for these two leakages/controls available to the adversary, we define two oracles, the first
\mathcal{E}_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}M_{\textup{CV}}\rightarrow T_{\textup{CV}}}
, allows the adversary to choose any message
M_{\textup{CV}}
and have the corresponding client tag generated for him.
The second
\mathcal{E}_{RM_{\textup{SV}}\rightarrow\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\alpha}}
allows the adversary to select any message
M_{\textup{SV}}
, and generate the server authentication check tag for the client, who would utilise it to perform checks in later sessions when necessary.
Note that for simplicity, we will label the oracles as
\mathcal{E}_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}E\rightarrow E^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{RE\rightarrow\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\alpha}E^{\prime}}
since the adversary would decide the messages according to its system
E
, and
T_{\textup{CV}}
is implicitly included in
E^{\prime}
since it is directly accessible to the adversary.

To monitor the secrets and oracles accessible, we introduce a new label
\alpha^{\textup{U}}
, which indicates the client’s index if the server authentication is performed in the same session.

\alpha^{\textup{U}}
is updated as follows: (1) if
D_{\textup{C}}=\phi
within a session, leave
\alpha^{\textup{U}}
unchanged, (2) if
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}\geq\alpha^{\textup{U}}
, set
\alpha^{\textup{U}}=\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}
, and (3) if
D_{\textup{C}}=1
and
F_{\textup{S}}\neq 1
, set
\alpha^{\textup{U}}=\alpha^{\textup{U}}+1
.
The second scenario occurs when the client’s index is updated to an index that may not fail in the next session, while the third scenario requires that the server not pass server validation since server did not participate/did not select a server authentication tag that matches the client’s.
A general input state can be defined as a mixture of two variants of states

\rho^{in}=\sum_{jj^{\prime}}p_{jjj^{\prime}}\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE}+p_{j,j+1,j^{\prime}}\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}
, where

\begin{gathered}\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE}=\outerproduct{jjj'}{jjj'}_{\alpha\alpha^{\textup{U}}\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{SE}\\
\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{SE}=\tau_{RK^{\textup{mask}}_{1,[j,m]}}\otimes\tilde{\tau}_{\tilde{\theta}_{[j_{\max},m]}\tilde{\theta}_{[j_{\max},m]}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,[j^{\prime},j-1]}E^{\prime}\rightarrow E}(\tau_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,[j^{\prime},j-1]}}\otimes\rho_{E^{\prime}}),\end{gathered}

(116)

j_{\max}=\max\{j,j^{\prime}\}
, and
E^{\prime}
contains the lost secrets
\tilde{\theta}_{[1,j_{\max}-1]}\tilde{\theta}_{[1,j_{\max}-1]}^{\prime}
, and the state

\begin{gathered}\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}=\outerproduct{j,j+1,j'}{j,j+1,j'}_{\alpha\alpha^{\textup{U}}\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{SE}\\
\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{SE}=\tau_{K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,[j+1,m]}}\otimes\tilde{\tau}_{\tilde{\theta}_{[j^{\textup{U}}_{\max},m]}\tilde{\theta}_{[j^{\textup{U}}_{\max},m]}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{RK^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,[j^{\prime},j]}E^{\prime}\rightarrow\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j}E}(\tau_{RK^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,[j^{\prime},j]}}\otimes\rho_{E^{\prime}}),\end{gathered}

(117)

and

j^{\textup{U}}_{\max}=\max\{j+1,j^{\prime}\}
, and the oracle access for
\mathcal{E}_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,[j^{\prime},j-1]}E^{\prime}\rightarrow E}
and
\mathcal{E}_{RK^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\textup{mask}}}E^{\prime}\rightarrow\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j}E}

can have any order of
\mathcal{E}_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}E\rightarrow T_{CV}E^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{RE\rightarrow\tilde{T}_{SV,\alpha}E^{\prime}}
in general.
As such, we can link the two input state

\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{SE}=\mathcal{E}_{RE^{\prime}\rightarrow\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j}E}\circ\mathcal{E}_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j}E^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow E^{\prime}}(\rho^{\textup{in},j+1,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{SE^{\prime\prime}}),

(118)

which would be useful to simplify the analysis.

The set of ideal output state
\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}
has form
\rho^{\textup{out}}=\sum_{j,j^{\textup{U}}\in\{j,j+1\},j^{\prime}}p_{jj^{\textup{U}}j^{\prime}}\rho^{\textup{out},jj^{\textup{U}}j^{\prime}}
with the form

\begin{split}\rho^{\textup{out},jj^{\textup{U}}j^{\prime}}=&\outerproduct{\phi 0}{\phi 0}_{D_{\textup{C}}F_{\textup{S}}}\otimes\sum_{\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}+1}p_{\phi 0,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\otimes\sigma^{\phi 0,jj^{\textup{U}},\tilde{j}^{\prime}}_{LSE}+p_{0\phi}\outerproduct{0\phi}{0\phi}_{D_{\textup{C}}F_{\textup{S}}}\otimes\sigma^{0\phi,j+1,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}\\
&+\outerproduct{1\phi}{1\phi}_{D_{\textup{C}}F_{\textup{S}}}\otimes\sum_{\tilde{j}\geq j}p_{1\phi,\tilde{j}}\sigma^{1\phi,\tilde{j},\tilde{j}+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}+\outerproduct{00}{00}_{D_{\textup{C}}F_{\textup{S}}}\otimes\sum_{\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}+1}p_{00,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\sigma^{00,j+1,j+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}_{LSE}\\
&+\outerproduct{10}{10}_{D_{\textup{C}}F_{\textup{S}}}\otimes\sum_{\tilde{j}\geq j,\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}+1}p_{10,\tilde{j}\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\sigma^{10,\tilde{j},\tilde{j}+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}_{LSE}+p_{11}\outerproduct{11}{11}_{D_{\textup{C}}F_{\textup{S}}}\otimes\sigma^{11,j^{\prime}j^{\prime}j^{\prime}}_{LSE},\end{split}

(119)

where

\sigma_{LSE}^{f_{\textup{A}}f_{\textup{B}},jj_{U}j^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
.
Note that unlike the QAKE protocol where
D_{\textup{C}}=1
is disallowed when
F_{\textup{S}}=\phi
, the lack of a check for server validation in the same session allows an adversary to request
T_{\textup{SV},\alpha^{\prime}}
from the server in a separate session and pass the server validation, i.e.
D_{\textup{C}}=1
.
However, the mismatch of
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\textup{U}}
guarantees that the next session with client involvement would result in
D_{\textup{C}}=0
, and an update of
\alpha
to indicate no confidence in the updated secrets.

By definition, the output state belongs to
\mathcal{S}_{in}
, thereby satisfying condition 3 in Thm.
1
when reducing to single round security.
Condition 1 is trivially satisfied since all secrets remain private and indices begin with 1.
As such, the security analysis reduces to the single-session security analysis for a single input component.
The security can be summarised as

Theorem 33
.

The CA protocol is secure with security parameter

\begin{split}\varepsilon_{\textup{sec},\textup{int}}=&4\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}}+2\varepsilon_{2}+6\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}+5\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+2\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}+\frac{1}{2}\times 2^{-\frac{1}{2}[N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})]-l_{\tilde{\theta}}-\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\SV}}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}-\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}]}\\
&+\sqrt{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}-1+2^{\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}+\log_{2}\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{2}}+1\right)-N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})]+\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}}}+\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}}+\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\SV}}},\end{split}

where
\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}=2^{-P_{1,\textup{tol}}[1-h_{bin}(e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}})]}
,
\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}
is related to the smoothing parameter for decoy state with PRNG basis (Thm.
32
),
\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}
accounts for errors associated with decoy state analysis,
\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}
and
\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\SV}}
are the size of the respective tags,
\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}
is the information leakage from error correction,
N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}
is the minimum tolerated number of single photon rounds in set
P_{2}
,
e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime}
is the phase error tolerance inclusive of the correction due to Serfling bound and
\varepsilon_{2}
is a variable to optimise over.

Sketch Proof.

We need to analyse the security for the two different input states
\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE}
and
\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}
.
In Thm.
35
, we show that the security of
\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}
can be related to the security of
\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE}
.
The key observation for this proof is that only in one specific scenario where
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}\geq j+1
and
j\neq j^{\prime}
, i.e. the adversary forces the client’s index to update to one
\geq j+1
, the client would proceed in the protocol using the updated index – and that matches with the case where the client begins with an index
j+1
and a restricted adversary.
In all other cases, we have that the authentication fails with high probability,
F_{\textup{S}}=0
, (argued in Thm.
34
) and the output state matches the ideal case closely.

With the reduction, focus is placed on the input state
\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE}
.
The analysis can be broken in different scenarios based on the
(D_{\textup{C}},F_{\textup{S}})
values.
Thm.
36
presents the case when the adversary tries to cheat the server without the client,
D_{\textup{C}}=\phi
, for which the security is a consequence of Thm.
34
ensuring
F_{\textup{S}}=0
with high probability.
The case where the adversary tries to gain information from the client,
F_{\textup{S}}=\phi
, is shown in Thm.
37
.
Since the adversary can in general trigger the client to proceed with the protocol, leakage of the basis seed, the announcement of
T_{\textup{CV}}
and providing control to
\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\tilde{j}}
follow.
However, this is exactly the situation accounted for as part of the input state definition for
\alpha^{\textup{U}}=\alpha+1
.

For cases where neither
D_{\textup{C}}
nor
F_{\textup{S}}
are
\phi
, both client and server are involved in the protocol.
Similar to earlier analysis in Sec.
D.2.3
, we can simplify the analysis by defining idealised version of
D_{\textup{PE}}
and
D_{\textup{C}}
, with small penalty shown in Eqn. (
126
).
With this change, when
D_{\textup{C}}=0
, Thm.
38
shows that similar to the
D_{\textup{C}}=\phi
case,
\tilde{F}_{S}=0
with high probability, and the leakage of
T_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}
and
\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}
can be accounted for by the updated index (
j^{\prime}
index no longer utilised).
When
D_{C}=1
and
\tilde{F}_{S}=0
(
F_{S}
but with ideal checks), the adversary can easily exploit the protocol to gain access to the oracle generating
T_{\textup{AV}}
and have control over the generation of
\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\alpha}
.
However, as shown in Thm.
39
, this is exactly accounted for when
\alpha^{\textup{U}}
is updated such that
\alpha^{\textup{U}}=\alpha+1
.
Finally, Thm.
40
shows that in the case of
D_{C}=\tilde{F}_{S}=1
, the ideal checks guarantee that
X_{P_{2}}^{\prime}
has high min-entropy based on Thm.
32
, thereby allowing the authentication key, authentication masking keys and
R
to remain secret.
∎

F.3.1
Security for
\alpha^{\textup{U}}\neq\alpha
input state

Before beginning our analysis, let us define a useful theorem regarding the value of
F_{\textup{S}}
when
D_{\textup{C}}\neq 1
.

Theorem 34
.

For any ideal input state
\sigma_{LSE}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
where the adversary has no access to the basis seed
\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}
and oracle
\mathcal{E}_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}M_{\textup{CV}}\rightarrow T_{\textup{CV}}}
,

\Pr[F_{\textup{S}}=1|D_{\textup{C}}\neq 1]\leq\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}}

On the other hand, if the oracle and basis seed is accessible

\Pr[F_{\textup{S}}=1|D_{\textup{C}}\neq 1]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{match}},

where the matching probability is

\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}=2^{-\absolutevalue{P_{1,\tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}})]},

noting that
\absolutevalue{\tilde{P}_1}\geq P_{1,\textup{tol}}
, where
\tilde{P}_{1}
is the set
P_{1}
as decided in the message
M_{\textup{CV}}
.

Proof.

Let us first consider the case where the oracle and basis seed are inaccessible.
While the adversary may have some control of the message
M_{\textup{r}}
sent to the client, since the adversary has no knowledge of
K^{\textup{h}}_{1}
and
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}
,
the uniformity property of the strongly 2-universal hash function ensures that

\Pr[F_{\textup{S}}=1|D_{\textup{C}}\neq 1]\leq\Pr[T_{\textup{CV},\textup{r}}=h_{1}(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},M_{\textup{r}}||f(M_{\textup{r}},X))\oplus K^{\textup{mask}}_{1}]\leq\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}},

(120)

where
f(M_{\textup{r}},X)
labels the other terms required in the hash described in step 7 of the main protocol, for any
T_{\textup{CV},\textup{r}}
selected by the adversary.

When access to the basis seed and oracle are available,
the adversary is able to perform a call to the oracle for any message
M_{\textup{CV}}
, receive the outcome
T_{\textup{CV}}
, before given access to
X
, and deciding on the message
M_{\textup{r}}
and tag
T_{\textup{CV},\textup{r}}
to send to the server.
Crucially, this forces the adversary to commit to a
\tilde{P}_{1}
set and
\tilde{X}_{\tilde{P}_{1}}
bit values when performing the oracle call.
We can simplify the probability

\begin{split}&\Pr[D_{\textup{CV}}\land D_{\textup{PE}}=1]\\
\leq&\Pr[D_{\textup{PE}}=1,(M_{\textup{r}}||f(M_{\textup{r}},X))\neq M,T_{\textup{CV},1,\textup{r}}=\tilde{T}_{\textup{CV},1}]+\Pr[D_{\textup{PE}}=1,(M_{\textup{r}}||f(M_{\textup{r}},X))=M,T_{\textup{CV},1,\textup{r}}=\tilde{T}_{\textup{CV},1}]\\
\leq&\Pr[T_{\textup{CV},1,\textup{r}}=\tilde{T}_{\textup{CV},1}|(M_{\textup{r}}||f(M_{\textup{r}},X))\neq M]+\Pr[D_{\textup{PE}}=1,(M_{\textup{r}}||f(M_{\textup{r}},X))=M]\\
\leq&\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\Pr[wt(X_{P_{1,\textup{r}}}^{\prime}\oplus X_{P_{1,\textup{r}}})\leq\absolutevalue{P_{1,\rr}}e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}},\tilde{P}_{1}=P_{1,\textup{r}},X_{P_{1,\textup{r}}}^{\prime}=\tilde{X}_{\tilde{P}_{1}},\absolutevalue{P_{1,\rr}}\geq P_{1,\textup{tol}}]\\
\leq&\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\Pr[wt(\tilde{X}_{\tilde{P}_{1}}\oplus X_{\tilde{P}_{1}})\leq\absolutevalue{\tilde{P}_1}e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}},\absolutevalue{\tilde{P}_1}\geq P_{1,\textup{tol}}]\\
\leq&\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\max_{\absolutevalue{\tilde{P}_1}\geq P_{1,\textup{tol}}}\sum_{i=0}^{\absolutevalue{\tilde{P}_1}e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}}}\begin{pmatrix}\absolutevalue{\tilde{P}_1}\\
i\end{pmatrix}2^{-\absolutevalue{\tilde{P}_1}}\\
\leq&\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+2^{-P_{1,\textup{tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{bit},\textup{tol}})]}.\end{split}

(121)

The first inequality splits the probability based on the equality of the messages, and the second line simplifies them by removing some conditions.
The third line notes that by the strongly 2-universal property of the hash function, with the keys not known to the adversary except via
T_{\textup{CV}}
, the probability that the adversary can guess the correct tag for input
(M_{\textup{r}}||f(M_{\textup{r}},X))
is bounded by
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}
.
The third and fourth lines further removes conditions on
D_{\textup{PE}}=1
and the messages being equal, and focuses mainly on terms we can use to bound the probability, where
\tilde{P}_{1}
and
\tilde{X}_{\tilde{P}_{1}}
are the corresponding terms from the message
M
.
The fifth line expands the probability of the bound on the Hamming weight, noting that the
X
is randomly generated and uncorrelated to
\tilde{X}_{\tilde{P}_{1}}
, which has to be decided before
X
is accessible to the adversary.
The final line uses the property of the binomial coefficient to simplify, and noting that the maximum value corresponds to
\absolutevalue{\tilde{P}_1}=P_{1,\textup{tol}}
.
It is clear that the result upper bounds
\Pr[F_{\textup{S}}=1|D_{\textup{C}}\neq 1]
.
∎

Figure 11:
Schematic of the proof of Thm.
35
.

Here, we provide a reduction of the security for input state variants with
\alpha^{\textup{U}}=\alpha+1
to the security for input state variants with
\alpha^{\textup{U}}=\alpha
, the latter of which we analyse in the later sections.

Theorem 35
.

Consider the CA protocol
\mathcal{P}
.
Suppose that for any valid input state
\rho^{\textup{in},j+1,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}
and adversarial strategy, there exists an ideal output state
\rho^{\textup{out}}\in\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}
such that

\begin{gathered}\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho^{\textup{in},j+1,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}),\rho^{\textup{out}})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}.\end{gathered}

Then, for any valid input state
\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}
, there exists an output state
\rho^{\textup{out}}\in\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}
such that

\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}),\rho^{\textup{out}})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}+\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}}+\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\SV}}}+2\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+2\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}.

Proof.

The protocol consists of three general steps:

1.

Server’s state generation and message.

2.

Client’s message receipt and response.

3.

Server and Client post-processing.

where WLOG, the third step will always occur at the end of the protocol since it does not contain additional outputs to the adversary.
Moreover, since the server’s state generation and message requires no external inputs, the adversary will find no advantage in delaying it.
As such, we can always take the protocol as one which follows the three step process.
Fig.
11
provides a schematic of the proof for the different cases discussed below.

Let us consider first the case where the client is involved in the protocol,
D_{\textup{C}}\neq\phi
(case 1).
Furthermore, let us consider the case where
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}<j
(case 1a), the client sets
D_{\textup{C}}=0
by the protocol.
If instead
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}=j
(case 1b), the decision would depend on
\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j}
and
T_{\textup{SV},\alpha^{\prime},r}
.
Since the adversary has oracle access for
R
, it is able to choose a message
M_{\textup{SV}}
and have the client generate
\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j}=h_{\textup{PA}}(R,M_{\textup{SV}})
.
Since
R
itself is inaccessible to the adversary, the uniformity property of the hash function means that

\Pr[D_{\textup{C}}=1|\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}=j]=\Pr[\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j}=T_{\textup{SV},\alpha^{\prime},r}]\leq\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\SV}}},

(122)

and the client sets
D_{\textup{C}}=0
with high probability.
In both cases, if
F_{\textup{S}}=\phi
, then no secrets are leaked and shared secrets privacy is maintained.
Otherwise, when
D_{\textup{C}}=0
, then from Thm.
34
, whether an oracle is accessible or not,
\Pr[F_{\textup{S}}=1|D_{\textup{C}}=0]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}
, i.e.
F_{\textup{S}}=0
with high probability.
When
D_{\textup{C}}=0
and
F_{\textup{S}}=0
, only
\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}
may in general be leaked to the adversary from the server state prepared.
If
j+1>j^{\prime}
, this information has already been assumed to be accessible to the adversary by the form of the input state.
If instead
j+1\leq j^{\prime}
, since
\alpha^{\prime}
is increased by 1 when the authentication fails,
F_{\textup{S}}=0
, and this leakage is allowed (i.e. the ideal input state includes this leakage).
Therefore, one is able to find an ideal output state with trace distance

\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}})_{\land D_{\textup{C}}\neq\phi},\rho^{\textup{out}}_{\land D_{\textup{C}}\neq\phi})\leq\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\SV}}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}

(123)

from the actual output state.

In the case where
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}\geq j+1
(case 1c), the client updates
\alpha
to
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}
and set
D_{\textup{C}}=1
.

Note that this implicitly means that the adversary does not utilise the
\mathcal{E}_{RM_{\textup{SV},j}\rightarrow\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j}}
oracle during this session and future sessions.
After updating
\alpha
, the client proceeds with the protocol using the updated
\alpha
, and its action would be similar with one where the protocol begins with
\rho^{\textup{in},j+1,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}
instead, and for adversarial strategies which ensures
D_{\textup{C}}=1
.
The main difference between starting with
\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}
and
\rho^{\textup{in},j+1,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}
then would be the access to the oracle
\mathcal{E}_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j}M_{\textup{CV}}\rightarrow T_{\textup{CV},j}}
if
j\geq j^{\prime}
.
If we consider (1)
j\neq j^{\prime}
, starting with either state makes no difference since
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j}
is not utilised in the client validation step.
This implies that the state
\rho^{\textup{out}}\in\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}
for which
\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho^{\textup{in},j+1,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}),\rho^{\textup{out}})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}
is also close to the case when
\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}
is utilised, i.e.

\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho^{\textup{in},j+1,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE\land D_{C}\neq\phi,\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}\geq j+1}),\rho^{\textup{out}}_{\land D_{C}\neq\phi,\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}\geq j+1})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}.

(124)

In the case where (2)
j=j^{\prime}
, since the client does not generate
T_{\textup{CV},j}
during the protocol (recall
\alpha\geq j+1
), the adversary has to rely on the oracle to pass,
F_{\textup{S}}=1
.
From Thm.
34
, the probability of such an event is maximally
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}
.
In this case, we therefore have
F_{\textup{S}}=0
or
F_{\textup{S}}=\phi
.
Since the client accepts the round,
D_{\textup{C}}=1
, additional oracles
\mathcal{E}_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\tilde{j}}M_{\textup{CV}}\rightarrow T_{\textup{CV},\tilde{j}}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{RM_{\textup{SV}}\rightarrow\tilde{T}_{SV,\tilde{j}}}
for
\tilde{j}
representing the updated
\alpha
value are provided to the adversary, and the basis seeds
\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{j}}
and
\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}
are leaked.
Since
F_{\textup{S}}\neq 1
and
D_{\textup{C}}=1
, we have that the final index is
\alpha=\tilde{j}
and
\alpha^{\textup{U}}=\tilde{j}+1
, for which this leakage and additional oracle accesses are accounted for in the ideal output state (see form of
\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}
).

Let us now consider the case where
D_{\textup{C}}=\phi
(case 2), where the client is not involved in the protocol.

When
j^{\prime}\leq j
(case 2a), the adversary would have access to
\tilde{\theta}_{[j^{\prime},j]}
and oracle access to generate
T_{\textup{CV},i}
for
i\in[j^{\prime},j]
.
From Thm.
34
, this would result in the server rejecting the session with high probability,
\Pr[F_{\textup{S}}=1|D_{\textup{C}}=\phi]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}
.
Therefore, since
F_{\textup{S}}=0
would result in no further leakage except
\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}
which is already accessible to the adversary, we can find an ideal output state close to it.
In the other case where
j^{\prime}>j
(case 2b), the adversary has no oracle access.
By Thm.
34
, the probability of
F_{\textup{S}}=1
is bounded by
\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}}
.
From the server state generation,
\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}
can be leaked.
However, since
\alpha^{\prime}
is updated when
F_{\textup{S}}=0
and
j^{\prime}>j
,
j_{\max}>j^{\prime}
and thus the leakage of
\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}
is accounted for in the ideal state.

Combining the results, the trace distance can be reduced to
\varepsilon_{\textup{sec}}+\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}}+\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\SV}}}+2\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+2\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}
.
∎

F.3.2
One-Sided Attack

With accounting for all input state variant
\rho^{\textup{in},j,j+1,j^{\prime}}_{LSE}
complete, we present the security for input state variant
\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE}
where only one party is involved in the protocol.
We begin with the case of
D_{\textup{C}}=\phi
,

Theorem 36
.

Consider the CA protocol
\mathcal{P}
. Then, for any valid input state
\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE}
, there exists
\rho^{\textup{out}}\in\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}
such that

\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE})_{\land D_{\textup{C}}=\phi},\rho^{\textup{out}}_{\land D_{\textup{C}}=\phi})\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}.

Proof.

This proof follows closely that of case 2 in Thm.
35
.
Let us consider two scenarios separately:
j\leq j^{\prime}
and
j>j^{\prime}
.
For the case of
j\leq j^{\prime}
, the adversary has no access to any oracles but has access to
\tilde{\theta}_{[j,j^{\prime}-1]}
.
From Thm.
34
,
\Pr[F_{\textup{S}}=1]\leq\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}}
and the authentication fails with high probability.
In terms of secrets leaked,
\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}
is leaked from the server state generation.
However, since
F_{\textup{S}}=0
prompts the server to update index
\alpha^{\prime}
and
j^{\prime}\geq j
, the leakage of
\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}
would have been accounted for in the ideal state since
j_{\max}
is increased.

For the second case of
j\geq j^{\prime}+1
, the adversary has knowledge of
\tilde{\theta}_{[j^{\prime},j-1]}\tilde{\theta}_{[j^{\prime},j-1]}^{\prime}
, and with oracle access from
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}
to
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j-1}
, where the relevant values
\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}
and oracle access for
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}
are present.
From Thm.
34
, the authentication will fail with high probability,
\Pr[F_{\textup{S}}=1|D_{\textup{C}}=\phi]\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}
.
As such, no additional secret is leaked since
\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}
is already assumed to be accessible to the adversary prior to the session.
∎

Another one-sided attack involves
F_{\textup{S}}=\phi
, where the adversary can potentially gain access to the two oracle variants for
(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i})
and
R
, since server authentication checks do not occur within the same session.

Theorem 37
.

Consider the CA protocol
\mathcal{P}
. Then, for any valid input state
\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE}
, there exists
\rho^{\textup{out}}\in\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}
such that
