\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE})_{\land F_{\textup{S}}=\phi},\rho^{\textup{out}}_{\land F_{\textup{S}}=\phi})=0.

Proof.

When the adversary attacks only the client, it is in general possible for the adversary to trigger
D_{\textup{C}}=1
using a tag
T_{\textup{SV},j}
generated from an earlier session or select a suitable
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}>\alpha
.
Since the client proceeds with the protocol when
D_{\textup{C}}=1
, this would result in (1) the client leaking
\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{j}}
due to the client measurement step and announcement of
X
values, where
\tilde{j}
represents the updated
\alpha
value, (2) the client announcing a client tag
T_{\textup{CV}}
for which the adversary has some control of the input message, and (3) the adversary being able to influence the server validation tag
\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\tilde{j}}
generated by the client.
Since
D_{\textup{C}}=1
and
F_{\textup{S}}\neq 1
, the index
\alpha^{\textup{U}}
is updated, i.e.
\alpha^{\textup{U}}=\tilde{j}+1
, for which the ideal state accounts for all three control/leakage available to the adversary in the form of leaked basis seed and oracle access to
\mathcal{E}_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\tilde{j}}\rightarrow T_{\textup{CV}}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{RM_{\textup{SV}}\rightarrow\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\tilde{j}}}
.
In other words, the output state matches an ideal output state with the secrets of the form
\rho^{\textup{in},\tilde{j},\tilde{j}+1,j^{\prime}}_{SE}
.

On the other hand, if
D_{\textup{C}}=0
, none of the three events occur since the client responds with random strings.
As such, the output state would match an ideal output state in
\mathcal{S}_{out}^{\prime}
.
∎

F.3.3
Man-in-the-Middle Attack

The final attack variant to consider is one where both parties are involved.
We begin the analysis for a man-in-the-middle attack by replacing the decoy state parameter estimation with actual parameter checks and the authentication check with a matching check, similar to that in Appendix
D.2.3
.
The
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}
decision modification would be the the same (note
D_{\textup{PE}}
can have slight differences), and this would result in the same
2\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}}
penalty.
The authentication check to be replaced is the client validation step, where we replace
D_{\textup{CV}}
with
\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}
, defined as

\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}=\begin{cases}1&\begin{subarray}{c}\{\alpha>\alpha^{\prime},M=(\hat{X}_{P_{2}},P_{2,\textup{r}},P_{1,\textup{r}},X_{P_{1},\textup{r}}^{\prime},S_{\textup{r}}),T_{CV,\alpha^{\prime}}=T_{\textup{CV},\textup{r}}\}\\
\lor\{\beta=\alpha^{\prime},D_{\textup{C}}=1,T_{\textup{CV}}=T_{\textup{CV},\textup{r}},(X_{P_{2}}^{\prime},P_{1},P_{2},X_{P_{1}}^{\prime},syn)=(\hat{X}_{P_{2}},P_{2,\textup{r}},P_{1,\textup{r}},X_{P_{1},\textup{r}}^{\prime},syn_{\textup{r}})\}\end{subarray}\\
0&otherwise\end{cases},

(125)

where
\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}=1
includes two scenarios: (1)
the client validation tag with the index
\alpha^{\prime}
comes from the client’s response or (2) the client validation tag is generated from the adversary’s oracle.
In either case, the same condition of matching message and an untouched transmission (
D_{\textup{C}}=1
ensures a tag is generated for
\beta=\alpha^{\prime}
) gives
D_{\textup{CV}}=1
.
The probability that
D_{\textup{CV}}=1
when
\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}=0
would be bounded by
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}
, since an mismatched message would not allow the adversary to guess the correct tag, by the
\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}
-strong 2-universal property of the hash function.
As such, the overall penalty can be summarised as

\Delta(\mathcal{P}(\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE}),\rho^{\textup{out},jjj^{\prime}})\leq\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE}),\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jjj^{\prime}})+2\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}+2\varepsilon_{\textup{ds}},

(126)

and we can simplify the analysis, noting that the output state alters to
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}
and
\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}
as well.

When both client and server are involved in the protocol, WLOG, the server’s state generation is the first step of the protocol, since it does not take in any input from the adversary and thus can always be shifted to before the client’s response.
It can also be argued that the client’s and server’s index and secrets update would be the final steps, since they do not result in any output accessible to the adversary at the end of the session.
The most general description of the protocol thus has fixed protocol steps, namely

\begin{split}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=&\mathcal{A}_{RX^{\prime}P_{2}\rightarrow\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\tilde{\alpha}}\tilde{\theta}_{\alpha}}\circ\mathcal{B}_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha_{\textup{r}}\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}Xsyn_{\textup{r}}R\rightarrow\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}T_{\textup{SV},\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}}\tilde{\theta}_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha_{\textup{r}}XX_{P_{1},\textup{r}}^{\prime}P_{1,\textup{r}}P_{2,\textup{r}}syn_{\textup{r}}T_{\textup{CV},\textup{r}}E^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}}\\
&\circ\mathcal{E}_{D_{\textup{C}}X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}P_{1}P_{2}synT_{\textup{CV}}E^{\prime}\rightarrow X_{P_{1},\textup{r}}^{\prime}P_{1,\textup{r}}P_{2,\textup{r}}syn_{\textup{r}}T_{\textup{CV},\textup{r}}\alpha_{\textup{r}}E^{\prime\prime}}\circ\mathcal{A}_{\alpha\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}T_{\textup{SV},\alpha,\textup{r}}\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\alpha}B\tilde{\theta}_{\beta}K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\beta}\rightarrow D_{\textup{C}}X^{\prime}X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}P_{1}P_{2}synT_{\textup{CV}}}\\
&\circ\mathcal{E}_{T_{\textup{SV},\alpha^{\prime}}QE\rightarrow\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}T_{\textup{SV},\alpha^{\prime},\textup{r}}BE^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{B}_{X\tilde{\theta}_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{\prime}\rightarrow Q},\end{split}

(127)

where
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
refer to quantum channels performed at the server and client respectively and
\mathcal{E}
are quantum channels associated with adversary strategy.
Note that we collate all decision for
\tilde{F}_{S}
and
\tilde{D}_{PE}
into a single channel
\mathcal{C}
separate from the rest of the steps and information for
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}
and
\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}
(same as
F_{S}
except that checks
\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}
and
\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}
are “ideal”) checks are captured within
E^{\prime\prime}
, such as the original values of the message sent by the client or the message utilised in the oracles.

Figure 12:
Schematic of proofs of Thm.
38
(case 1 and 2), Thm.
39
(case 3) and Thm.
40
(case 4).

The security analysis for man-in-the-middle attack can then be split into multiple cases.
Fig.
12
provides a summary of the arguments of the various cases.
The first case we consider for the analysis is the
D_{\textup{C}}=0
case.

Theorem 38
.

Consider the CA protocol
\mathcal{P}
, then there exists
\sigma_{LSE}^{00,j+1,j+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
such that

\Delta\left(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE})_{\land D_{C}=0},\sum_{\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}+1}p_{00,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\sigma_{LSE}^{00,j+1,j+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\right)\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}.

Proof.

The trace distance can be split into two components based on the
\tilde{F}_{S}
value by triangle inequality, where the
\tilde{F}_{S}=1
case (case 1) can be further upper bounded by
\Pr[\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}=1,D_{\textup{C}}=0]
.

By definition of
\tilde{F}_{S}
, we can further upper bound the probability by
\Pr[\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}=1,\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}=1,D_{\textup{C}}=0]
.
With these conditions, the only case where it can occur is when

\alpha>\alpha^{\prime}
, and the adversary correctly matches the messages.
Following the proof of Thm.
34
, since
X
is uncorrelated to the value
\tilde{X}_{\tilde{P}_{1}}
committed during the oracle call, the probability that the two values matched would be bounded by
\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}
.

For the case of
\tilde{F}_{S}=1
(case 2), the definition of
\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}
necessarily requires
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}\leq j
, leading the index update to be
\tilde{\alpha}=j+1
.
The server on the other hand updates based on
\alpha_{\textup{r}}=\tilde{j}^{\prime}>j^{\prime}
announced to it, otherwise, it updates to
j^{\prime}+1
.
As such, in general, the output indices are a mixture of
(j+1,j+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime})
where
\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}+1
, matching the index of the
\sigma_{LSE}^{j+1,j+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}
as required.
Furthermore, since
D_{C}=0
implies the client responds with random strings, the only value that are leaked are
T_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}
and
\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}
.
The updating of the indices to
\alpha=j+1
and
\alpha^{\prime}>j^{\prime}
means that these values are no longer utilised (i.e. these leaks are already accounted for).
Note that any oracle access to
(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,i})
for indices
i<\tilde{j^{\prime}}
can be discarded according to Thm.
4
since the corresponding masking key are no longer utilised, and the basis seeds for the same indices are traced out.
As such, the output state for the secret matches the ideal states
\sigma_{SE}^{j+1,j+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}
, resulting in 0 trace distance.

Combining both cases 1 and 2, the resulting trace distance is bounded by
\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}
.
∎

The next case to consider
is
D_{\textup{C}}=1,\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}=0
(case 3), which can occur without requiring complex attacks from the adversary since the client does not perform an authentication check of the server within the same session.
It would, however, trigger an update of
\alpha^{\textup{U}}
, indicating that server authentication check would fail in the next session involving a client.

Theorem 39
.

Consider the CA protocol
\mathcal{P}
. Then, there exists
\sigma_{LSE}^{10,\tilde{j},\tilde{j}+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
such that

\Delta\left(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE})_{\land D_{C}=1,\tilde{F}_{S}=0},\sum_{\tilde{j}\geq j,\tilde{j}^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}+1}p_{10,\tilde{j}\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\sigma_{LSE}^{10,\tilde{j},\tilde{j}+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}\right)=0.

Proof.

To achieve
D_{C}=1
, the adversary is free to select
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}=\tilde{j}\geq j
and since
\tilde{F}_{S}=0
, the adversary is also free to select
\alpha_{\textup{r}}=\tilde{j}^{\prime}-1\geq j^{\prime}
.
As such, the updated indices would be
\alpha=\tilde{j}
,
\alpha^{\prime}=\tilde{j}^{\prime}
with
\alpha^{\textup{U}}=\tilde{j}+1
due to the condition
D_{\textup{C}}=1
and
\tilde{F}_{S}\neq 1
.
This matches with the indices of the ideal state as required.

What remains is to prove that the output state matches the ideal state in subsystems
SE
as well.
For any combination of
j
and
j^{\prime}
, we can always consider a more powerful adversary that has access to the client’s basis seed
\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{j}}
, which allows the adversary to prepare quantum states that results in a chosen
X^{\prime}
to be received by the client.
This has two results: (1) the adversary is able to partially select the message used to generate
T_{\textup{CV}}
from authentication masking key
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\tilde{j}}
and (2) the adversary is able to partially select the message used by the client to generate
\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\tilde{j}}
(and updated
\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{j}}
).
We can proceed with providing the adversary more power – to be able to fully select the messages for both (1) and (2), without requiring that the messages be correlated.
This corresponds with the adversary being provided with an oracle access to
\mathcal{E}_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\tilde{j}}\rightarrow T_{\textup{CV}}}
and one access to
\mathcal{E}_{RM_{\textup{SV}}\rightarrow\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},\tilde{j}}}
, and where
\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{j}}
can be correlated to the adversary (but for which the adversary has no direct access to).
The additional access to these two oracles and correlation of
\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{j}}
(part of
E
) are accounted for in the states
\sigma_{LSE}^{\tilde{j},\tilde{j}+1,\tilde{j}^{\prime}}
when
\alpha^{\textup{U}}=\alpha+1
.
Therefore, the output state (in the worst case) corresponds to a state in
\mathcal{S}_{in}
and thus the trace distance is 0.

∎

The final case to consider is the case when
D_{\textup{C}}=\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}=1
(case 4).
A critical component of security is provided by
\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}
, where the requirement for the message selection limits the success to the case of
\beta=\alpha^{\prime}
, along with an unmodified message.
This allows us to reduce the analysis to a protocol that is similar to that of Thm.
32
, where the entropic uncertainty relation is applied.

Theorem 40
.

Consider the CA protocol
\mathcal{P}
. Then, there exists
\sigma_{LSE}^{j^{\prime}j^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
such that

\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE})_{\land D_{C}=\tilde{F}_{S}=1},p_{11}\sigma_{LSE}^{j^{\prime}j^{\prime}j^{\prime}})\leq 4\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}}+2\varepsilon_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\times 2^{-\frac{1}{2}[N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})]-l_{\tilde{\theta}}-\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\SV}}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}-\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}]}\\
+\sqrt{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}-1+2^{\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}+\log_{2}\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{2}}+1\right)-N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})]+\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{match}},

where
\varepsilon_{2}>0
is a parameter to be optimised.

Proof.

By definition, when
\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}=1
, the announced index value
\alpha_{\textup{r}}\leq\alpha^{\prime}
, which leaves
\alpha^{\prime}
unchanged throughout the protocol.
The definition of
\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}=1
requires additionally that the client’s updated index be
\beta\geq\alpha^{\prime}
.
Let us consider the case of
\beta>\alpha^{\prime}
(case 4a), which necessarily requires
\alpha>\alpha^{\prime}
for
\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}=1
.
In this case, the adversary has oracle access to
(K^{\textup{h}}_{1},K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,\alpha^{\prime}})
, where the message inserted to the oracle has to match the server’s received message.
We can thus follow the proof of Thm.
34
to show that the probability of the message matching and thus this event occuring (
\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}=1
) is upper bounded by
\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}
.
Therefore, we can split off
case 4a of
\beta\geq\alpha>\alpha^{\prime}
, where the trace distance between the output state and ideal output would be bounded by
\varepsilon_{\textup{match}}
.

What remains is the case where
\beta=\alpha^{\prime}
(case 4b), which has
\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
, where
\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}=1
ensures that the messages from the client are not tampered with.
Since
\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}=D_{\textup{C}}=1
, the final indices are
\alpha=\alpha^{\textup{U}}=\alpha^{\prime}=j^{\prime}
, which corresponds to the index of the ideal state
\sigma_{LSE}^{j^{\prime}j^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
.
Let us consider one such state
\sigma_{LSE}^{j^{\prime}j^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}_{in}
, defined by

\sigma_{LSE}^{j^{\prime}j^{\prime}j^{\prime}}=\tilde{\Pi}^{j^{\prime}j^{\prime}j^{\prime}}_{\alpha\alpha^{\textup{U}}\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes\tau_{RK^{\textup{mask}}_{1,[j^{\prime},m]}}\otimes\tilde{\tau}_{\tilde{\theta}_{[j^{\prime},m]}\tilde{\theta}_{[j^{\prime},m]}^{\prime}}\otimes\rho_{E\land D_{\textup{C}}=\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}=1\land\beta=j^{\prime}}.

(128)

The output state can be written as

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE|j\leq j^{\prime}})_{\land D_{\textup{C}}=\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}=1\land\beta=j^{\prime}}=\tilde{\Pi}^{j^{\prime}j^{\prime}j^{\prime}}_{\alpha\alpha^{\textup{U}}\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes\tau_{K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,[j^{\prime}+1,m]}}\otimes\tilde{\tau}_{\tilde{\theta}_{[j^{\prime}+1,m]}\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}_{[j^{\prime}+1,m]}}\otimes\rho_{RK^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}T_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}E^{\prime\prime}\land\Omega},

(129)

where
\Omega:=\{\alpha_{\textup{r}}\leq j^{\prime},\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}=j^{\prime},\tilde{D}_{\textup{PE}}=\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}=D_{\textup{C}}=1\}
(which matches
\{D_{\textup{C}}=\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}=1,\beta=j^{\prime}\}
) and
the
\tilde{D}_{S}
and
D_{\textup{C}}
values has been absorbed into
E^{\prime\prime}
.
We can simplify the trace distance as

\begin{split}&\Delta(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE|j\leq j^{\prime}})_{\land\Omega},\rho^{\textup{out},jjj^{\prime}}_{\land\Omega})\\
\leq&\Delta(\rho_{RK^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}T_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}E^{\prime\prime}\land\Omega},\tau_{R}\otimes\tilde{\tau}_{\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}}\otimes\tilde{\tau}_{T_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}}\otimes\rho_{E^{\prime\prime}\land\Omega})\\
\leq&\Delta(\rho_{RK^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}T_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}E^{\prime\prime}\land\Omega},\tau_{R}\otimes\tilde{\tau}_{\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}}\otimes\tilde{\tau}_{T_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}}\otimes\rho_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}E^{\prime\prime}\land\Omega})\\
&+\Delta(\rho_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}E^{\prime\prime}\land\Omega},\tau_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}}\otimes\rho_{E^{\prime\prime}\land\Omega})\\
\leq&\Delta(\rho_{RK^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}T_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}E^{\prime\prime}\land\Omega},\tau_{R\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}T_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}}}\otimes\rho_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}E\land\Omega})+\Delta(\rho_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}E^{\prime\prime}\land\Omega},\tau_{K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}}\otimes\rho_{E\land\Omega})\\
\leq&p_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}\{2\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}+\frac{1}{2}\times 2^{-\frac{1}{2}[H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm},1}}(\hat{X}_{P_{2}}^{\prime}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}E^{\prime\prime})_{\rho_{\land\Omega^{\prime}|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}-l_{\tilde{\theta}}-\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\SV}}]}\\
&+2(\varepsilon_{sm,2}+\varepsilon_{2})+\sqrt{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}-1+2^{\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}+\log_{2}\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{2}}+1\right)-H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{sm,2}}(\hat{X}_{P_{2}}^{\prime}|E^{\prime\prime})_{\rho_{\land\Omega^{\prime}|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}}}\},\end{split}

(130)

where the first inequality considers a specific state in the class of ideal output states where
T_{SV,j}
and
\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j}
are equal and independent from the adversary.
The second inequality applies the triangle inequality, while the third inequality invokes
\tilde{D}_{\textup{CV}}=1
, which indicates that the variables
\hat{X}^{\prime}_{P_{2}}=X_{P_{2}}^{\prime}
and thus the hashed outcomes
(\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}},\tilde{T}_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}})=(\tilde{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime},T_{\textup{SV},j^{\prime}})
, allowing the trace distance to reduce to one set of variables (more formally, this is simulated by introducing a “copy” channel from one set to another and removing the CPTP “copy” channel).
The fourth inequality applies the quantum leftover hash lemma (Thm.
5
),
noting again that
\Omega
implies
\hat{X}^{\prime}_{P_{2}}=X_{P_{2}}^{\prime}
, and that
\Omega=\Omega^{\prime}\land\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
, which is defined later.

Here, we analyse the first min-entropy term, which would also acts as a lower bound the second term.
Let us list explicitly the conditions of
\Omega
,

1.

\alpha_{\textup{r}}\leq j^{\prime}
,
\alpha_{\textup{r}}^{\prime}=j^{\prime}
,
D_{\textup{C}}=1
: Conditions associated with index selection, and passing the initial client check.

2.

(P_{2,\textup{r}},P_{1,\textup{r}},X_{P_{1},\textup{r}}^{\prime},syn_{\textup{r}})=(P_{1},P_{2},X_{P_{1}}^{\prime},syn)
: Message sent from client to server is unchanged.

3.

T_{\textup{CV}}=T_{\textup{CV},\textup{r}}
: The tag sent from the client to server is unchanged.

4.

X_{P_{2}}^{\prime}=\hat{X}_{P_{2}}
: The server’s corrected bitstring matches with the client’s bitstring.

5.

\frac{wt(X_{P_{1,\textup{r}}}\oplus X^{\prime}_{P_{1},r}[\{P_{1,\textup{r}}\}])}{N_{P_{1,\textup{r}}}}\leq e_{b,tol}
,
\absolutevalue{P_{1,\rr}}\geq P_{1,\textup{tol}}
: Remaining parameter estimation checks.

6.

\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
: Standard parameter estimation checks.

where we list the events for the first five events from
\Omega_{1}
to
\Omega_{5}
as being part of
\Omega^{\prime}
.
We note here that
\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
estimates directly on the
(P_{1},P_{2},X_{P_{1}}^{\prime})
values instead of the received values, since
\Omega_{\textup{PE}}\land\Omega_{2}=\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}\land\Omega_{2}
.
We first simplify by noting that
\hat{X}_{P_{2}}^{\prime}
is computed from
X_{P_{2,\textup{r}}}
and the syndrome
syn_{\textup{r}}
received.
Therefore, by data processing inequality
[
42
]
,
we have that

H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(\hat{X}_{P_{2}}^{\prime}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}E^{\prime\prime})_{\rho_{\land\Omega^{\prime}|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}\geq H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(X_{P_{2,\textup{r}}}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}syn_{\textup{r}}E^{\prime\prime})_{\rho_{\land\Omega^{\prime}|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}},

(131)

noting that
syn_{\textup{r}}
is in general able to be determined from
E^{\prime\prime}
(adversary stores a copy of the
syn_{\textup{r}}
it sends to the server).
Further imposing that
P_{2,\textup{r}}=P_{2}
from the
\Omega_{2}
condition, we can simplify the min-entropy before removing the conditions of
\Omega_{2}
to
\Omega_{5}

[
38
]
, i.e.

H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(X_{P_{2,\textup{r}}}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}syn_{\textup{r}}E^{\prime\prime})_{\rho_{\land\Omega^{\prime}|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}\geq H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(X_{P_{2}}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}syn_{\textup{r}}E^{\prime\prime})_{\rho_{\land\Omega_{1}|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}.

(132)

Here, we can reverse the CPTP map used to generate
syn_{\textup{r}}E^{\prime\prime}
, leaving us with

H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(X_{P_{2}}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}syn_{\textup{r}}E^{\prime\prime})_{\rho_{\land\Omega_{1}|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}\geq H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(X_{P_{2}}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}P_{1}P_{2}synT_{\textup{CV}}E^{\prime})_{\rho_{\land\Omega_{1}|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}.

(133)

We can remove the syndrome
syn
and the tag
T_{\textup{CV}}
utilising the min-entropy chain rule
[
42
]
.
Furthermore, with the removal of
T_{\textup{CV}}
,
K^{\textup{h}}_{1}
and
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}
is uncorrelated with any other variables in the state and can be removed, leaving us with

H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(X_{P_{2}}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}P_{1}P_{2}synT_{\textup{CV}}E^{\prime})_{\rho_{\land\Omega_{1}|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}\geq H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(X_{P_{2}}|\tilde{\theta}X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}P_{1}P_{2}E^{\prime})_{\rho_{|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}-\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}-\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}},

(134)

where we also remove the
\Omega_{1}
condition, and included additional
\tilde{\theta}
conditioning.
Here, the protocol steps are simply:

1.

The server prepares decoy state BB84, using the basis generated from
\tilde{\theta}
and bit value
X
.

2.

The adversary performs its attack on the state, mapping
QE
to
PBE^{\prime}
.

3.

The client randomly select
P_{1}
rounds and measure them in the basis generated from
\tilde{\theta}
and outputs
X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}
.

4.

Based on
X
,
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
and
X_{P_{1}}^{\prime}
, the parameter estimation decision is made (event
\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}
),

This is exactly the same protocol steps as that in Thm.
32
, and we can utilise the same analysis to demonstrate that

H_{\textup{min}}^{\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}}(\hat{X}_{P_{2}}^{\prime}|K^{\textup{h}}_{1}K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}E^{\prime\prime})_{\rho_{\land\Omega^{\prime}|\tilde{\Omega}_{\textup{PE}}}}\geq N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})]-\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}-\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}},

(135)

with
\varepsilon_{\textup{sm}}=\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}}
.
The second min-entropy term can follow a similar analysis, but since
K^{\textup{h}}_{1}
and
K^{\textup{mask}}_{1,j^{\prime}}
is traced out,
T_{\textup{CV}}
would be a random string, and can be simply removed without incurring the
\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}
penalty.
As such, the overall trace distance is

\Delta(\Pi_{D_{\textup{C}}\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}}^{11}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\rho^{\textup{in},jjj^{\prime}}_{LSE})\Pi_{D_{\textup{C}}\tilde{F}_{\textup{S}}}^{11},\rho^{{}^{\prime}\textup{out},jjj^{\prime}}_{\land\Omega})\leq 4\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\textup{ph}}}+2\varepsilon_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\times 2^{-\frac{1}{2}[N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})]-l_{\tilde{\theta}}-\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\SV}}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}-\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}]}\\
+\sqrt{\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}\varepsilon_{\textup{MAC},1}-1+2^{\log_{2}\absolutevalue{\vars{T}_{\CV}}+\log_{2}\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{2}}+1\right)-N_{P_{2},1}^{\textup{tol}}[1-h_{\textup{bin}}(e_{\textup{ph},\textup{tol}}^{\prime})]+\textup{leak}_{\textup{EC}}}}.

(136)

∎
